# The Erotic Map of Britain
## A Tour of the Places Where Britain Misbehaved

*The Marquis de Mayfair*

---

## A note on what is true

This book makes a promise it intends to keep: it tells you when it does not know.

Britain's erotic history has been retold for so long, by so many people with an
interest in improving it, that a great deal of what "everyone knows" turns out to
be Victorian invention, tourist-board embroidery, or a single unreliable memoir
repeated until it hardened into fact. Where that has happened, this book says so —
in the text, not in a footnote you will never read.

So: documented history, literary fiction and folklore are labelled apart, every
time. Where a story rests on one hostile source, you will be told. Where the record
simply goes quiet, you will be told that too. The debunking is not a spoilsport's
footnote; it is frequently the best part.

Corrections are welcome and are made, never defended:
**marquis@marquisdemayfair.com**

---

## A note on the maps

Every place in this book has been given coordinates, checked and graded for
confidence on a five-point scale. They appear at the head of each section and in
full in the Gazetteer. Many of these places are private homes, working farms or
consecrated ground. Being on a map is not an invitation. Please behave.

# Foreword
## Britain, in Private

This book began as a map, and the map began as an argument I lost.

I had said — over dinner, with the confidence of a man who has not checked — that Britain has no erotic history worth the name. That we are a nation of raised eyebrows and separate bedrooms, and that if you want the good stuff you go to Venice, or Paris, or anywhere with better weather and a looser grip on the concept of shame. Somebody disagreed. Somebody produced, with the air of a card player who has been waiting all evening, a single fact about a Dorset hillside. And that was the end of my dinner and the beginning of this book.

So I started putting pins in a map. Not metaphorical pins — actual coordinates, actual streets, actual buildings you can stand outside and feel faintly odd about. And within about a month a pattern emerged that I have not been able to unsee since: the pins do not fall randomly. They cluster. And they cluster, with an almost comic regularity, along edges.

They gather on the wrong bank of rivers. They pile up on the far side of city walls, just past the gate where the corporation's writ ran out. They collect eight miles over the Scottish border, in a village whose entire economy was built on the fact that English law stopped at a small stream. They accumulate in basements, on islands, on moorland, in the unlit end of a pleasure garden — and in one instance in a wood on the edge of Hampstead long associated with clandestine encounters. Britain's erotic history is not merely a history. It is a geography problem. Tell me where the authorities' jurisdiction ended, and I will tell you where everybody went.

This turns out to be the key to the whole thing, and it explains the great national contradiction that visitors have been remarking on for four hundred years — that we are simultaneously among the most repressed and the most inventive people in Europe. Both are true. The repression is real; the debates are there in Hansard and the laws in the statute book, drearily, century after century. But so is the ingenuity, and the ingenuity is what this book is actually about. Every wall built in this country to keep desire out has had, within about a fortnight, a door cut in it by somebody enterprising — and quite often that somebody was charging admission.

The evidence, I should warn you, is strange stuff, and the Introduction that follows explains why. What I noticed first was simply how much of it I was reading over somebody's shoulder — a constable's, a magistrate's, a blackmailer's. Very little of this was written down by the people it happened to.

This means that erotic history is inseparable from every other kind of British history — and that is one of the pleasures of the thing. You cannot tell it without the coal boom that built a port, or the railway that built a resort, or the Reformation, the Restoration, the Blitz and the Pill. A bishop's rent roll helps explain a red-light district. A change in marriage law creates an industry. A fashion for sea-bathing produces a moral panic, a magistrate and eventually a postcard. Follow desire through these islands and you find yourself, almost by accident, being told the story of the place — its class system, its lawyers, its landladies, its genius for the licensing committee — from an angle nobody teaches at school.

And what it reveals about us is, I think, rather flattering, though not in the way one might hope.

We are not, as a people, given to abandon. Let us be honest about that. Other nations own that story and are welcome to it. What we are is discreet — and discretion is not the same thing as shame, though the two have been confused by every foreign visitor since the sixteenth century. Shame hides a thing because it believes the thing is wrong. Discretion hides it because it believes the thing is nobody else's business — which is a completely different proposition, and a far more robust one. It is the instinct that puts the inscription on the inside of the ring. It is the small door with no sign on it. It is the shop that wraps the parcel without comment and asks after your mother.

The censors, meanwhile, are the comic relief, and I make no apology for enjoying them. A gentleman's pornography collection was accepted by a great national museum, catalogued and locked in a cupboard, where it survived precisely because it had been locked in a cupboard. This is a country that has rarely managed to suppress anything but has developed world-beating expertise in filing it.

The arc, though — and this is the part I did not expect when I started sticking in pins — bends. It genuinely does. You begin with women working in licensed houses from which a bishop drew rent, and you end within living memory with the same trade conducted by women, for women, on their own terms and in their own front rooms. Somewhere between those points it came out of the shadows, stopped being furtive and — this is the part I care about — started being good. Well made. Beautiful, even.

At which point I must declare an interest, and I would rather do it here than have you find it in a footnote. I sell things — beautiful things, for private use, from a shop in Mayfair. Whether that gives me any business writing this book is a question I return to at the end, when you will be in a position to judge it. For now it is enough that you know.

One last thing, and then I will get out of your way.

Everything here is sourced, and the book tells you where certainty ends. This subject has been written about for centuries by pornographers, moralists and people with an axe to grind, which means that a great deal of what “everyone knows” turns out to have been invented by a Victorian with a theory and a subscription list. Where the record is solid, I say so. Where it is folklore, I say that too.

Take a coat. Some of this is outdoors.

*The Marquis de Mayfair*  
*Mayfair, London*

# Introduction


Almost everything we know about what the British got up to in private, we know because somebody was trying to stop them.

Consider how the evidence reaches us. We can describe the inside of a Georgian molly house in Field Lane, Holborn — the fire, the drink, the men dancing and mimicking women's voices, the back room they called the marrying room — because a constable named Samuel Stevens infiltrated the place in 1725 and swore to what he had seen, and because the Old Bailey printed his deposition before three of the men he described were hanged at Tyburn. We can reconstruct a night at the Caravan Club in Covent Garden, a bohemian basement that stayed open for six weeks in the summer of 1934, because the Metropolitan Police watched it from an empty theatre across the road, logged who came and went, seized the membership cards, and photographed the draped interior on the night they closed it down. The fullest surviving record of interwar queer London is a prosecution file.

There is one great exception, and it is instructive. Anne Lister of Shibden Hall wrote roughly five million words of diary, and encrypted about a sixth of them in a code of her own devising, and in that code she recorded her lovers, her techniques and her own frank vocabulary for both. She is the rare case of a person in these pages who documented her desires herself, on purpose, in the reasonable expectation that nobody would ever break the cipher. When someone finally did, in the 1890s, his first instinct was to advise burning the lot.

That is the shape of the problem, and it is also the shape of this book. The erotic history of Britain survives in two forms: what people hid well enough to keep, and what the authorities wrote down while trying to destroy it. Everything else has gone — and it went, most often, at the hands of somebody who loved the dead and thought they were being kind.

What that surviving record shows, once you have enough of it laid out on a map, is a single continuing argument. Britain has spent something over a thousand years building rules about who may desire whom, where, at what price, and in what light. It has spent exactly the same thousand years getting round them. The rules are worth studying because they tell you what each age was frightened of. The evasions are worth studying because they tell you what people actually wanted, which is rarely the same thing and is almost always more interesting.

The oldest evasion is geographical, and it is beautifully simple: go somewhere the rule does not reach. The licensed brothels of medieval Bankside sat on the south bank of the Thames because the City's writ stopped at the water, and because the manor on the far side answered to the Bishop of Winchester, who was content to license, regulate and tax what London forbade. The women who worked there were nicknamed Winchester Geese after their landlord. Six centuries later, couples thwarted by Lord Hardwicke's Marriage Act of 1754 discovered the same principle and drove north for a village on the far side of a small stream, where a different law ran. Vauxhall's pleasure gardens were across the river; Soho and Covent Garden sat outside the old City; Shepherd Market flourished in the richest square mile in Britain precisely because nobody in Mayfair wished to make a scene. Britain's erotic geography is, to a striking degree, a map of jurisdictions and the gaps between them.

The second force is class, and it runs through this book like a seam of coal. For the better part of four decades the Georgian state tolerated an annual directory of Covent Garden prostitutes — names, addresses, prices and specialities — selling thousands of copies at half a crown. When the Proclamation Society finally prosecuted its publishers in 1794, James Roach's defence was that nobody had ever been prosecuted for publishing it before. He was fined £100 and sent to Newgate for a year, and the List ended. In the following century, historians argue, prosecution intensified as pornography became cheap enough to reach working-class and youthful readers — an interpretation consistent with Lord Campbell's stated concern for the morals of youth, though not a causal claim he made himself. A century later, prosecuting Penguin Books over Lady Chatterley's Lover, counsel for the Crown asked a jury whether this was a book they would wish his wife or his servants to read — and lost the case in a single sentence, because he had said the quiet part aloud. When a male brothel was uncovered in Cleveland Street in 1889, the working-class telegraph boys were named, dismissed from the Post Office and pressed to emigrate; the aristocratic clients were allowed to reach France. British law has been remarkably relaxed about the pleasures of people it trusted, and remarkably vigilant about everyone else.

Third, and inseparable from class, is commerce. Somebody was always selling. A shilling bought you into Vauxhall, and what the shilling really bought was the right to walk the same gravel as a duchess, in a landscape engineered so that a great deal might happen and nothing need be admitted. The same instinct built the Georgian bagnio behind a respectable frontage, put a card reading "Model" beside a Mayfair doorbell, and, in 1981, moved the whole trade into the front rooms of suburban Britain when a twenty-one-year-old realised that the customer for sex toys had been a woman all along and would buy them cheerfully among friends. Desire in Britain has almost never been free, and following the money is usually the fastest route to the truth.

Fourth is censorship, which is where the comedy lives. The Lord Chamberlain licensed the British stage for more than two centuries, and Soho built an industry around the absurd distinctions his office enforced. Chapter Ten follows the motionless nudes, private-club loopholes and licensing committees in full. Censorship in this country has rarely been effective and frequently ridiculous, but it should not be laughed at too easily: the machinery that regulated a tableau belonged to the same culture of control that jailed Oscar Wilde for two years' hard labour.

Fifth is policing, and here the book's map leaves London behind. Enforcement was often not a matter of statute at all but of neighbours. Robert Burns was hauled before his kirk session and set on the cutty stool to be rebuked by name in front of the congregation, and the parish minute books of Scotland record thousands who were treated the same way with no verse to console them. Before there were vice squads there were elders, ministers, chapel societies and servants who talked.

Sixth is technology, which arrives repeatedly and always does the same thing: it makes desire cheaper to reproduce and harder to police. Print gave the eighteenth century its bestselling directory of prostitutes. Photography and the stereoscope stocked the back rooms of Holywell Street. The party plan, of all things, opened the sex trade to half the population.

And underneath all six, unchanging, is the seventh: private appetite. It is the one variable in this book that does not move. What alters is the vocabulary, the address, the price and the penalty.

A word about method, because it bears on the pleasure of the thing. This book flags what it cannot prove in the prose rather than hiding uncertainty in a footnote. Where a place has been restored, a legend is late, or a lurid biography rests on one hostile or salacious source, the distinction is made at the point where it matters. That discipline is not a caveat. It is the method.

Finally, and most importantly: the people. A great many of those who appear here were treated abominably by their own century, and the comedy in these pages is aimed at the powerful, the pious and the absurd, never at them. The women buried in unconsecrated ground at Crossbones had no choice about much. Neither did the boys of Cleveland Street. Wilde died broken in a Paris hotel. Desire is treated in this book as ordinary, universal and interesting, which is what it is, and the moral panics are treated as the historical curiosities they generally turn out to be.

We begin where the stakes were highest. In most of Britain, for most of its history, who you took to bed was a private matter that might, if you were unlucky, become a parish matter. In exactly one bedroom in England it was a matter of treason, succession and the constitution — where a king's boredom could dissolve a marriage, a church and the standing of a nation in Europe, and where a queen's alleged adultery was tried as a capital crime against the state.

So we start at Hampton Court.

# Chapter 1 · Crowns and Bedchambers

*Royal desire, and the constitutional trouble it causes*


> `[PLATE 1 — chapter frontispiece]`


In most of England, for most of its history, the consequences of an ill-judged love affair were a fine, a spell of public shame, and a village that remembered. In roughly a dozen bedrooms in this chapter, the consequences were a broken church, a contested succession, a beheading, or the loss of a throne.

That is the only real difference between royal desire and everybody else's, and it is a difference of scale rather than of kind. Henry VIII wanted a woman who would not sleep with him, waited the better part of seven years, and dismantled England's relationship with Rome to get her — then decided he wanted somebody else and had her tried for treason on evidence that would not now convict a shoplifter. Mary Stuart married for love, or something adjacent to it, and had her secretary butchered in front of her by her husband's friends while she was six months pregnant. George IV married a Catholic widow in a Mayfair drawing room in 1785, in a ceremony he knew perfectly well to be void twice over under two separate Acts of Parliament, and then had his friend Charles James Fox deny it flatly in the House of Commons.

The mechanism that made these things constitutional rather than merely sad was inheritance. A crown descends through bodies, so the law took a close interest in whose body had been where and with whom. This produced a legal apparatus of quite spectacular intrusiveness — the Royal Marriages Act of 1772, the Act of Settlement, the whole machinery of annulment, legitimacy and precontract — and it produced the peculiar situation in which a nation's foreign policy could be reorganised around one man's boredom.

It also produced the evasions, which is why this chapter opens the book. Faced with rules of this weight, royal lovers did precisely what the fishwife and the curate did, only with better lawyers and more expensive houses. They married in secret. They kept establishments at a discreet distance from the palace, and when Nell Gwyn objected to holding her Pall Mall house on a mere lease, Charles II granted the freehold on 1 December 1676 to two trustees, who conveyed it to her own trustees the following April and settled it on her for life. As late as 1960 it was still the only property on the south side of Pall Mall not held of the Crown: a piece of conveyancing that is really a love letter. They built a house on the Isle of Wight where nobody could see in. They retreated to a lodge in Windsor Great Park to make the least private decision in modern British history. And when the affair was over, the family burned the letters. Beatrice copied her mother's journals into a hundred and eleven notebooks and burned most of the originals as she went, finishing in 1931 over the objections of George V and Queen Mary. In 1905 Edward VII destroyed more than three hundred letters Victoria had written to her Balmoral factor Alexander Profeit about John Brown, after they were recovered from a blackmailer. A great deal of what this chapter would like to tell you went up a chimney in the interests of the institution.

What survives is therefore skewed, and skewed in a particular direction: towards what the state needed to write down, and away from what the parties would have preferred to keep. Seventeen love letters in Henry's own hand survive in the Vatican Library. How they reached Rome is unknown; the most recent theory, advanced by Peter Lake, Michael Questier, Kendra Packham and Estelle Paranque in 2023, argues that they were still in England in 1601 and reached Rome through an English Jesuit. It remains a hypothesis. Holyrood has the murder because a murder requires depositions. Osborne has the marriage because Victoria could not stop writing about it and only her daughter's editorial hand stands between us and the whole of it.

We start at the top of the social order for the same reason a physicist starts with the frictionless case. Here the forces are enormous and unmistakable — law, money, succession, Rome, Parliament — and you can watch them act on private appetite without anything getting in the way. Everything that follows in this book is the same collision, at lower voltage, among people who did not have a palace to conduct it in.



## 1. A Machine for Making and Unmaking Wives

*Hampton Court Palace · East Molesey, Surrey (historically Middlesex) · c.1514–1547*


> **Hampton Court Palace** — East Molesey, Surrey, England
> 51.40361° N, 0.33778° W · confidence 5/5


> `[PLATE 2 — Hampton Court Palace · 51.40361° N, 0.33778° W]`


At its Tudor height Hampton Court produced over 800 meals a day, served up by a staff of some 200 cooks, sergeants, grooms and pages working across a range of kitchens that Henry VIII had enlarged until they sprawled through more than fifty rooms and burned through well over a million logs a year. Beef arrived by the carcass. Wine came up the Thames by the barrel. This was not a house. It was a small, ravenous city devoted to the appetite of one man, and appetite is rather the point of the place.

Hampton Court was not Henry's idea. It was Cardinal Thomas Wolsey's. By an indenture of 11 January 1514 Wolsey took a ninety-nine-year lease on a manor from the Knights Hospitaller, at £50 a year, and spent the following years and a genuinely startling amount of money — the traditional figure is 200,000 gold crowns — turning it into the grandest house in England. He built so well that he rather fatally out-built his king. When Wolsey failed to extract from the Pope the annulment Henry wanted from Catherine of Aragon, he handed Hampton Court over in a doomed attempt to keep the royal favour. It did not work. Wolsey died in 1530 at Leicester Abbey, on his way south to almost certain trial. The palace passed to Henry, who set about making it his own.

What Henry built there tells you exactly how a Tudor monarch thought about privacy, power and sex, because at Hampton Court those three things were the same thing wearing different hats. A visitor moved through a deliberate sequence of rooms, each one harder to enter than the last: the Great Hall, then the Great Watching Chamber where the guard stood, then the Presence Chamber, then the Privy Chamber, and finally the innermost secret lodgings and the bedchamber. The architectural historian Simon Thurley has shown how this worked as a filtering system: the closer you got to the King's bed, the more important you were. Access was the currency. The bedchamber was the treasury.

Henry pushed the private rooms further and further from the crowds, and to guarantee genuine seclusion he raised the Bayne Tower — the name comes from the French *bain*, a bath — a three-storey block beside his bedchamber containing his study, his library, his jewels, and a bathroom with hot and cold running water piped from cisterns on the roof. Here was a king who could lock the world out and soak in warm water while, a few rooms away, several hundred courtiers jostled for the right to hand him his shirt.

All of this magnificence had one blunt biological purpose. Henry needed a son. The whole convulsion of the 1530s — the break with Rome, the founding of the Church of England, the deaths — flowed from a dynastic anxiety about a male heir, and the marriage bed at Hampton Court was where that anxiety was supposed to be resolved. A royal marriage was not private life. Consummation was a matter of state, witnessed in its consequences by ambassadors and recorded in dispatches. A queen's body was a public instrument, and the palace was the machine built around it.

Anne Boleyn is written faintly into the fabric. She and Henry stayed here in the early 1530s while grand new lodgings were built for her overlooking the park; those rooms were demolished later in the seventeenth century. After her execution in 1536 her badges were stripped out — though the palace's gatehouse is still called the Anne Boleyn Gateway, and high in the Great Hall ceiling, missed by the workmen, the entwined initials H and A survive: one of the more poignant clerical oversights in English history. She had connected this place to Hever Castle in Kent, where Henry had courted her for years; she left it as a ghost in the plasterwork.

It was Jane Seymour, Henry's third wife, who gave him what he wanted. In October 1537, at Hampton Court, she bore a son, the future Edward VI. The court erupted in relief. Twelve days later Jane was dead, of complications following the birth. Henry, by all accounts genuinely grief-stricken, had at last got his heir and lost the wife who produced him in a fortnight — the transaction of Tudor queenship laid bare in a single terrible week.

Then there is Catherine Howard, and here we must be careful, because history and the tourist trade have not been. Catherine was a girl — probably about seventeen when Henry married her in July 1540, weeks after annulling his marriage to Anne of Cleves. Henry was forty-nine, obese, and increasingly frightening. Catherine had been placed at court by ambitious Howard relatives who saw in her a route to influence, exactly as a previous generation had used her cousin Anne Boleyn. In November 1541 evidence of her earlier relationships reached the King. She was confined to her rooms, then taken away, and executed at the Tower in February 1542. She was a young woman used by the adults around her and destroyed by a system that treated her body as property; that is the true story, and it is grim enough without embroidery.

The embroidery is the Haunted Gallery. The legend holds that Catherine broke free of her guards, ran screaming along a gallery towards the chapel where Henry was at prayer, and was dragged back — and that her ghost re-enacts the dash to this day. Historic Royal Palaces, which runs the place, is admirably honest about this: it labels the tale a legend. So should we. Lucy Worsley, who was a curator at the palace for twenty-one years, has pointed out that the famous cold spot in the gallery is caused by a draught from a concealed staircase, and adds, drily, that she does not believe in ghosts. There is no solid contemporary evidence for the screaming run at all. What we have instead is a real, documented cruelty — a terrified teenager under arrest — which the Victorians, who understood that terror sells tickets, reupholstered as a spook story. The debunking is not a killjoy footnote. It is the whole moral of the room: a real girl's suffering turned into a visitor attraction.

Anne of Cleves supplies the comic counterpoint, and the comedy is entirely at Henry's expense. His fourth marriage, in January 1540, was a diplomatic arrangement with a German duchy; on meeting Anne he decided he did not fancy her and never consummated the match. Within six months it was annulled. Anne, no fool, agreed at once, was rechristened the King's "beloved sister", handsomely housed, and quietly outlived every one of them — later holding Hever Castle itself. She is the one wife who read the machine correctly and stepped off it before it could close.

Over the inner gateway hangs the astronomical clock, installed in 1540, designed by the King's astronomer Nicholas Kratzer and made by the clockmaker Nicholas Oursian. Fifteen feet across, gloriously pre-Copernican, it puts the earth at the centre of the universe and tells you the hour, the month, the phase of the moon and — most usefully for courtiers arriving by barge — the time of high water at London Bridge. It is, in a sense, the perfect emblem of the place: a beautiful, expensive machine with the King's world firmly at its centre, everything else revolving obediently around it. Much of its mechanism has in fact been renewed over the centuries, the Victorians doing a good deal of the work now taken for Tudor, but the theatre of it is authentic enough.

Stand in Clock Court today and the argument makes itself. This was the most sophisticated pleasure palace in England, wired for warm baths and wine fountains, and it was also a device for producing and disposing of women according to their dynastic usefulness. Two of Henry's six wives died as a direct result of their duty to him — one in childbed, one on the block — and both were connected to these rooms. The bedchamber was a room of state. The women who entered it were instruments of policy. The wonder of Hampton Court is not that it is beautiful. It is that we can still walk through the exact machinery and feel how it worked.



## 2. Seventeen Letters and a Seven-Year No

*Hever Castle · Hever, Edenbridge, Kent · c.1526–1533*


> **Hever Castle (Henry VIII courts Anne Boleyn)** — Hever, Edenbridge, Kent, England
> 51.18722° N, 0.11444° E · confidence 5/5


"Having been for above a whole year stricken with the dart of love," Henry VIII wrote, "and not yet sure whether I shall fail of finding a place in your heart and affection." A king who by his own admission loathed writing letters had picked up a pen, and gone on picking it up, because a young woman from Kent kept not quite saying yes.

The woman was Anne Boleyn, and the place that made her was Hever Castle, a moated manor near Edenbridge that her family had held since her father inherited it in 1505. It is worth being honest about how little we know of her beginnings, because the uncertainty starts with the most basic fact of all. Anne's birth year is genuinely disputed: most modern historians settle on around 1501, but two seventeenth-century sources point to 1507, and the six-year gap changes how we read everything she did. A woman of thirty holding out for a crown is a different story from a girl of twenty-four. The record simply does not tell us which we are looking at.

What we do know is her education, and it was extraordinary for an English gentlewoman. In 1513 her father sent her abroad, first to the sophisticated court of Margaret of Austria at Mechelen in the Low Countries, then to France, where she served Mary Tudor (briefly queen to the ageing Louis XII) and afterwards Queen Claude, wife of Francis I. She stayed in France for the better part of a decade. She came home around 1521 speaking flawless French, versed in music, dancing, fashion and theology, and carrying a continental polish that made the daughters of the English gentry look provincial. She had, in effect, been finished at the two most cultured courts in Europe. Henry never stood a chance.

The seventeen surviving love letters he wrote her are among the most remarkable documents in English royal history, and not only for their content. Eight are in English, nine in French — the language of courtly love — and none is dated, which is why scholars still argue over their order and place them loosely between 1527 and 1528, with Henry's pursuit beginning perhaps a year earlier. They are, frankly, the letters of a besotted and slightly desperate man. He signs some of them by drawing a heart around Anne's initials, like a lovestruck adolescent with the powers of a tyrant. In one he closes wishing himself "in my sweetheart's arms," in language warm enough to make a modern reader look away and check nobody is reading over their shoulder. And here is the strange, sad asymmetry at the heart of the archive: we have every one of Henry's letters and not a single one of Anne's replies. We hear only half the conversation. We must infer her from the shape of the silence — from what Henry answers, complains about, and pleads against.

What she was doing, across those years, was refusing. Anne's sister, Mary Boleyn, had already been Henry's mistress, and the arrangement had left her precisely nowhere; when it ended, so did the King's interest. Anne appears to have drawn the obvious conclusion. She would not be a mistress. She held out — for years — for marriage and a crown, and in doing so turned a private infatuation into a constitutional emergency. Because the only way Henry could marry Anne was to be rid of Catherine of Aragon, and the only way to be rid of Catherine was to break with the Pope who would not permit it. The English Reformation has many causes, but one of them, undeniably, is a woman at Hever saying no and meaning it.

A word about Mary Boleyn, because a great deal is said about her and much of it is later gossip dressed as fact. That Mary was Henry's mistress is reasonably well evidenced; Henry himself later obtained a dispensation that only makes sense if he had slept with Anne's sister. That she had earlier been a mistress of Francis I of France is repeated everywhere and rests on far thinner ground — a couple of hostile continental remarks, hardened by retelling into "the great prostitute" of Tudor legend. The careful position is to say that the relationship with Henry is documented and the rest is rumour, and to resist the centuries of eager embroidery.

In the summer of 1528 the story nearly ended in a different way entirely. The sweating sickness — a terrifying disease that could kill within hours — swept through England, and Anne caught it at Hever. Henry, who was frightened of illness to the point of cowardice and fled from it whenever he could, did not come. But he sent his own physician to attend her, and wrote anxiously. She recovered. Had she not, there would have been no Queen Anne, no Elizabeth I, and quite possibly no Church of England — a reminder of how much of history hangs on the constitution of a single immune system.

Hever survives, and survives handsomely, largely because an American gave it a second life: William Waldorf Astor bought and restored it in the early twentieth century, laying out the gardens and filling the rooms. You can stand in the house where Anne grew up, and where those letters arrived by messenger, and where she formed the resolve that would remake the country. There is a neat, almost novelistic coda in the deeds, too: after Anne's fall, Hever eventually passed to Anne of Cleves — the wife who survived Henry by knowing when to yield, inheriting the childhood home of the wife who never yielded at all.

As for the letters, they took an even stranger journey than their author. They are not in England. They sit in the Vatican Library, in Rome, catalogued as Vat. lat. 3731, and how they got there was for centuries a mystery answered only with a shrug and the word "stolen" — probably, people said, to be used against Henry in the annulment fight. Recent scholarship tells a better story. In a 2023 essay in the *Times Literary Supplement*, the historians Peter Lake, Michael Questier, Kendra Packham and Estelle Paranque traced the letters still sitting in England as late as 1601, in the hands of an English Jesuit who meant to send them to a colleague in Rome writing a history of the Reformation. The most intimate words the King of England ever wrote were carried to the heart of the church he had defied — not by a thief in the 1530s, but by a Catholic exile, half a century later, as evidence in the long argument over what desire had cost.



## 3. The Properest Long Man She Ever Saw

*Wemyss Castle, Fife, and the Palace of Holyroodhouse, Edinburgh · 1565–1566*


> **Wemyss Castle (Mary, Queen of Scots meets Darnley)** — East Wemyss, Fife, Scotland
> 56.14431° N, 3.08100° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Palace of Holyroodhouse (Mary & Darnley's marriage)** — Edinburgh, Midlothian, Scotland
> 55.95278° N, 3.17194° W · confidence 5/5


> `[PLATE 3 — Wemyss Castle (Mary, Queen of Scots meets Darnley) · 56.14431° N, 3.08100° W]`


On the evening of 9 March 1566, in a small supper room off her bedchamber at Holyroodhouse, Mary, Queen of Scots was six months pregnant and having dinner with a few friends when armed men came up the private stair. What happened next would end her marriage, endanger her crown and echo down Scottish history. But to understand why it happened, you have to go back thirteen months and a little way up the coast, to the moment she first laid eyes on the man who caused it.

The place was Wemyss Castle, perched on the sea cliffs of Fife above the Firth of Forth, and the date was 17 February 1565. Mary was twenty-two: tall, clever, and — as reigning Queen of Scots, dowager Queen of France and a serious claimant to the English throne — very possibly the most eligible woman in Europe. She had been widowed for four years and needed a husband, an heir, and, one suspects, a bit of pleasure in a life that had so far been mostly duty. Into this walked Henry Stuart, Lord Darnley: nineteen, over six feet tall, elegant, a decent hand with a lute, and — this mattered, because Mary herself stood about six feet — someone she did not tower over on the dance floor.

The one contemporary voice we have for the meeting is Sir James Melville, whose memoirs record that Mary reckoned Darnley "the properest and best proportioned long man that ever she had seen." It is worth attributing that carefully — it is Melville's recollection, written later, not a transcript — but it has the ring of truth, and it did the damage. Within weeks the courtship was a love affair. Mary nursed Darnley through an illness. On 29 July 1565 they married in the Chapel Royal at Holyroodhouse, she twenty-two and he nineteen, and for a very short while it seemed a triumph.

It curdled almost at once. Darnley was vain, idle, given to drink and to sulking, and above all he wanted power he had not earned. He demanded the Crown Matrimonial — the right to rule as king in his own name and, crucially, to keep the throne if Mary died before him. It was an enormous thing to ask, and Mary, sensibly, would not grant it. A marriage that had begun in physical infatuation became a cold war over the constitution, which is a very Scottish sort of tragedy.

Into the gap between them stepped David Rizzio, an Italian musician who had risen to become Mary's private secretary and trusted confidant. He and the Queen shared a love of music and talk; he had her ear; and Darnley, encouraged by nobles with their own reasons for wanting Rizzio gone, convinced himself — or allowed himself to be convinced — that the friendship was something more. It almost certainly was not. But sexual jealousy rarely troubles itself with evidence, and a jealous consort with a grievance about power is a dangerous combination.

Which brings us back to that supper room. On 9 March 1566 Darnley and a large body of armed men, led by Lord Ruthven, made their way to the Queen's private chambers and seized Rizzio. That the Queen was six months pregnant with the child who would become James VI did not stay their hands; a loaded pistol was reportedly held to her. Rizzio was dragged from the room and killed, and Darnley's own dagger was left in the body to mark his complicity. It was a political murder staged deliberately in front of a pregnant queen — an act designed as much to terrorise and control Mary as to remove her secretary. I will not linger on the killing itself, which was as brutal as these things are. The point that matters is what it revealed: that Mary's husband would spill blood in her own rooms, and that her marriage was now a threat to her life and her throne.

She responded with extraordinary nerve. Within days she had detached the weak-willed Darnley from his fellow conspirators, and under cover of darkness the royal couple slipped out of Holyrood to safety. In June 1566 she was delivered of a healthy son. The marriage, though, was finished in all but name, and its final chapter belongs to another kind of book: in February 1567 Darnley himself was killed, at Kirk o' Field, in circumstances that would help bring Mary down. That is a consequence, and I leave it as one line.

What survives of all this is worth knowing. Wemyss Castle still stands above the Forth, the ancient seat of the Wemyss family, a Category A listed building and very much a private home — its walled gardens are opened to visitors on a limited basis, but the castle itself is not on show. It is, appropriately, a place you glimpse rather than tour: the first meeting happened somewhere the public cannot quite reach.

Holyroodhouse is the opposite. Now the King's official residence in Scotland and cared for by the Royal Collection Trust, it shows visitors the oldest part of the palace — the James V tower — including Mary's bedchamber and the little supper room where it all went wrong. And here we must deal with the tourist fiction, because it is a good one. For generations, visitors have been shown a dark stain on the floorboards and told it is Rizzio's blood, indelible after four and a half centuries. It is nothing of the sort. The Royal Collection Trust notes that the fascination with Mary's tragedy sent visitors flocking from the later eighteenth century onward, and the "bloodstain" grew up to meet the demand; the floorboards themselves were largely replaced long after 1566, and the stain has, over the years, been helpfully refreshed. Chip away the legend and you are left with something more interesting than a magic bloodstain: a real room, a real terror, and a queen who kept her head when men were losing theirs around her.

The long shadow of that meeting at Wemyss stretches further than anyone in 1565 could have known. The child Mary carried through the supper-room ordeal became James VI of Scotland, and in 1603 he became James I of England too, uniting the crowns and founding the Stuart line that would, two generations on, give England its most sexually candid king of all — Charles II, whose court we shall visit shortly. A tall young man with a lute, glimpsed across a hall in Fife, set in motion a dynasty.



## 4. Pretty, Witty, and Freehold

*Banqueting House, Whitehall, and 79 Pall Mall · City of Westminster, London · 1660–1687*


> **Banqueting House, Whitehall (Charles II's court)** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.50417° N, 0.12583° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Nell Gwyn's house, 79 Pall Mall** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.50600° N, 0.13560° W · confidence 4/5


Everybody knows the building at 79 Pall Mall is Nell Gwyn's house. Everybody is wrong. The handsome Italianate pile standing there today went up in 1866–68, designed by David Brandon for the Eagle Insurance Company, and Nell had been dead for nearly two centuries before the first brick was laid. What is true is that on this plot of ground once stood the house of the most beloved royal mistress in English history — and that the ground itself carries a genuine constitutional peculiarity, which we will come to, because it is the best thing about her.

First, the world she rose into. When Charles II came back from exile in 1660, he brought with him an appetite for pleasure that eleven years of Puritan rule had only sharpened, and he installed it at Whitehall Palace — by common consent the most sexually frank court in English history. The diarist John Evelyn, no prude but easily shocked, described the King as "addicted to women," and the King spent his reign proving him right. Of that sprawling palace almost nothing survives; it burned in 1698. The one great fragment left standing is the Banqueting House, built by Inigo Jones in 1622 and ceilinged with Rubens's vast painted apotheosis of the Stuarts — a fitting relic, since it was also the building outside which Charles's father was executed in 1649. The son grew up knowing exactly what the family business cost.

Charles conducted his love life at a scale that beggars the modern imagination. His mistresses were famous in their own right: Barbara Villiers, Lady Castlemaine and later Duchess of Cleveland, imperious and grasping; Louise de Kérouaille, Duchess of Portsmouth, the French Catholic widely assumed to be a spy for Louis XIV; the actress Moll Davis; the flamboyant Hortense Mancini; and Nell. He publicly acknowledged fourteen illegitimate children by seven mistresses — six of them borne by Barbara Villiers alone — and produced, with his patient and childless queen, Catherine of Braganza, not one legitimate heir. The whole gaudy edifice of Restoration royal fertility rested on a barren marriage, which is the kind of irony the age's poets could not resist. The Earl of Rochester, the court's laureate of libertinism, skewered his monarch in a couplet — "Restless he rolls from whore to whore, / A merry monarch, scandalous and poor" — and, remarkably, kept his head.

Nell Gwyn came up from nothing, and never pretended otherwise. Born around 1650 into real poverty — her mother kept a brothel — she sold oranges at the King's Theatre before the actor Charles Hart took her in hand and turned her into the finest comic actress of the age. Samuel Pepys, who saw everything, adored her and called her "pretty, witty Nell." By about 1668 she had caught the King, and she held him, on and off, until his death, bearing him two sons — the elder of whom became Duke of St Albans, an English dukedom that descends from an orange-seller from Drury Lane.

In February 1671 Charles installed her in a townhouse at 79 Pall Mall. Evelyn, walking with the King through St James's Park on 2 March that year, was scandalised to overhear "a very familiar discourse between the King and Mrs Nelly," she leaning over her garden wall on a raised terrace, he standing on the green walk below. Evelyn confided that he was "heartily sorry" at the scene, which tells you rather more about Evelyn than about Nell.

Here is the constitutional oddity. The Pall Mall house was Crown property, granted to Nell on a lease. She objected. According to a tradition recorded in the eighteenth century and preserved by the *Survey of London*, she declared that "she had always conveyed free under the Crown, and always would," and refused to accept the house until the freehold was conveyed to her outright by Act of Parliament. In 1676 she got her way. The freehold has been in private hands ever since — making the site of 79 Pall Mall, as the *Survey of London* confirmed, the only ground on the south side of the street not owned by the Crown. It is a small, permanent joke lodged in the London land registry: a king's mistress, born in a bawdy-house, holds the one scrap of Pall Mall the Crown could not keep. The exact wording of her retort is traditional rather than documented from her own lips, but the freehold itself is solid fact, and it is the perfect monument to her — earthy, shrewd, and unbudgeable.

The other thing everyone knows about Nell is the "Protestant whore" story: that her coach was mobbed at Oxford (or London — the versions differ) by a crowd who mistook her for the Catholic Louise de Kérouaille, whereupon she leaned out and cried, "Good people, you are mistaken; I am the Protestant whore," and won them over. It is a wonderful line and it belongs to her in spirit, but honesty compels a note: the anecdote is not contemporaneously documented, comes down through later retellings, and varies in its details. Treat it as the truest sort of legend — the kind that is right about a person even when it cannot be proved about an event. It survives because it captures something real: unlike the aloof foreign duchesses, Nell was a Londoner the London mob claimed as their own.

She stayed loyal to Charles to the end. On his deathbed in 1685 he is said to have asked his brother to "let not poor Nelly starve," and James II, to his credit, paid her debts. She did not long outlive her king. After a stroke she died on 14 November 1687, aged about thirty-seven, and was buried at St Martin-in-the-Fields; her funeral sermon was preached, at her own request, by Thomas Tenison, the future Archbishop of Canterbury, who "said much to her praise" and took some criticism for it. A vicar who would rise to the top of the English church, standing up to eulogise an orange-seller and royal mistress — it is a very Nell Gwyn kind of ending.

And the house? Gone, of course, replaced by that Victorian insurance office, which now bears a blue plaque noting the association. You can stand outside it. There is not a great deal to see. But somewhere under the Portland stone and the nineteenth-century pretension lies the only freehold on the south side of Pall Mall — the one thing she made sure the Crown would never get back.



## 5. The Wife Who Was and Wasn't

*Royal Pavilion and St John the Baptist Church · Brighton, East Sussex · 1785–1837*


> **Royal Pavilion, Brighton (George IV & Maria Fitzherbert)** — Brighton, East Sussex, England
> 50.82230° N, 0.13740° W · confidence 5/5


> `[PLATE 4 — Royal Pavilion, Brighton (George IV & Maria Fitzherbert) · 50.82230° N, 0.13740° W]`


On a memorial in a Catholic church on the Bristol Road in Brighton, the effigy of a woman wears three wedding rings. She had three husbands, which would be unremarkable were it not that the third was the King of England, and that the marriage which mattered most to her was, in the eyes of English law, no marriage at all. The woman is Maria Fitzherbert, and her three rings are the neatest possible summary of one of the strangest romantic tangles in British royal history.

Maria was born Maria Anne Smythe in 1756, a member of the Catholic gentry, and by her late twenties she had already buried two husbands — Edward Weld, who died within months of their wedding, and Thomas Fitzherbert, who left her a wealthy and self-possessed widow. She entered London society and there, in the spring of 1784, met George, Prince of Wales, heir to the throne and six years her junior. He fell for her with the headlong, theatrical intensity that would characterise his whole life; on one occasion he is said to have staged a suicidal gesture to force her hand. Maria, devout and sensible, would not become his mistress. So George did the one thing guaranteed to cause maximum constitutional trouble. He married her.

The ceremony took place on 15 December 1785, in the drawing room of her house in Park Street, Mayfair, before a tiny company: her uncle and her brother as witnesses, and a clergyman, the Reverend Robert Burt, sprung from the Fleet debtors' prison for the occasion by the payment of his £500 debts (perhaps £80,000 today). It was, in the sincerity of the moment, a real wedding. In law it was doubly, comprehensively void. The Royal Marriages Act of 1772 made any marriage by a member of the royal family invalid without the sovereign's consent, and George III had most certainly not been asked. And the Act of Settlement of 1701 provided that any heir who married a Roman Catholic forfeited his place in the succession. So the marriage that George had contracted could not be legal — and had it somehow been legal, it would have cost him the throne. He had found a way to lose either way, which was a kind of genius.

The secret could not hold. By 1787 rumours of the marriage were loud enough to threaten the Prince's finances, which depended on Parliament clearing his monumental debts. His ally Charles James Fox rose in the House of Commons and denied the marriage outright, calling the rumour a malicious falsehood — on what he believed was the Prince's own authority. Maria, who had gone through a ceremony she regarded as sacred, found herself publicly declared never to have been a wife at all, by the Prince's own spokesman. She was humiliated and furious. That she eventually forgave him tells you something about the peculiar gravity he exerted.

Brighton is where they went to be, as far as possible, themselves. The Prince had first come to the little seaside town in 1783; in 1786 he took a farmhouse, and in 1787 he had the architect Henry Holland transform it into a neat neoclassical villa, the Marine Pavilion. Maria was installed nearby. Later, in 1804, William Porden built her Steine House a short walk away — she was the not-quite-wife kept in comfortable, deniable proximity. And then, between 1815 and 1823, John Nash wrapped Holland's tidy villa in the astonishing confection we see today: the Royal Pavilion, all Indian domes and minarets outside and delirious chinoiserie within, a pleasure-dome raised on cast iron and royal debt. It is one of the maddest, happiest buildings in England, and it exists because a prince wanted a private world by the sea.

The private world did not last, because the public duties of a prince intervened in the crudest way. By the mid-1790s George was, once again, catastrophically in debt, and Parliament would only relieve him if he made a proper, legal, Protestant marriage. In 1795 he married his cousin, Caroline of Brunswick, a union so instantly and mutually loathsome that the couple separated almost immediately after producing a daughter, Princess Charlotte. George reportedly needed to be drunk to get through the wedding. The contrast could hardly be sharper: the marriage the law recognised was a disaster, and the marriage the law denied was the one he actually wanted. In the years that followed he returned to Maria more than once — there were separations and reconciliations — and Rome, characteristically, took her side: around 1800 the papacy ruled that in the eyes of the Catholic Church she was, and had always been, George's lawful wife.

Maria outlived him. She spent her later years quietly in Brighton and died at Steine House in 1837, and was buried at the church of St John the Baptist on the Bristol Road, where her memorial shows those three rings. George had died seven years earlier, in 1830, King at last and long estranged from her — and yet, when they laid him out, he was found to be wearing a miniature portrait of Maria Fitzherbert around his neck, as he had asked to be. Whatever the law said, and it said a great deal, he went into his coffin married to her in the only court that finally mattered to him.

There is a pattern here worth naming, because it runs right through this book. British erotic history is, over and over, a history of desire colliding with regulation — of laws written to control who may marry whom, and of the endless human ingenuity applied to getting around them. The Royal Marriages Act that voided George and Maria's wedding is the same impulse that sent runaway couples bolting to Gretna Green, that filled the Fleet prison with clandestine weddings, that drew the desperate to a holy well in Wales; we will meet all of them later. And the rhyme carries forward, too. A century and a half after George stood in a Mayfair drawing room and married a twice-widowed Catholic he was forbidden to marry, another Prince of Wales would face the same collision between the crown and an unacceptable twice-married woman — and Edward VIII, unlike his ancestor, would choose the woman over the throne. George never had to make that choice. He kept the crown, and kept Maria's face against his chest, and let the law say what it liked.



## 6. Victoria, Twice Over

*Osborne House, East Cowes, Isle of Wight & Balmoral Castle, Crathie, Aberdeenshire, Scotland · 1845–1901*


> **Osborne House (Victoria & Albert's private idyll)** — East Cowes, Isle of Wight, England
> 50.75028° N, 1.27056° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Balmoral (Victoria & John Brown)** — Crathie, Aberdeenshire, Scotland
> 57.03972° N, 3.22861° W · confidence 5/5


The woman we picture when we say "Victorian" — small, black, unamused, a national grandmother carved from suet — spent her wedding night writing in her diary like a girl who has just discovered what all the fuss is about. "To lie by his side, and in his arms, and on his dear bosom," she recorded on 10 February 1840, "was bliss beyond belief!" She and Albert did not, she noted, sleep much. In the morning she watched him shave and thought him the most beautiful thing she had ever seen. She was twenty; she was head over heels; and she had nine children in seventeen years to show for how the marriage continued.

None of this fits the legend, which is rather the point. The legend was built later, partly by Victoria herself in widowhood and partly by the daughter who edited her.

Osborne is where the private Victoria lived. She and Albert bought the estate on the Isle of Wight in 1845 — she called it "quite a paradise" and loved that she could "walk anywhere without being followed or mobbed" — and pulled down the existing house to build something better. The result is an Italianate villa with two campanile towers, designed by the London builder Thomas Cubitt with Albert acting as architect in all but name. Albert chose the style because the sea light over the Solent reminded him of the Bay of Naples. He laid out the terraces, planted the grounds, and filled the house with the thing that most upsets the suet-pudding version of these people: art of the frankly sensual kind.

The couple gave each other nudes. For Albert's birthday in 1852 Victoria bought him *Florinda* by Franz Xaver Winterhalter, a large canvas of semi-clothed women preparing to bathe while a king spies on them from the bushes, and hung it opposite their writing desks, where it still hangs. She gave him William Edward Frost's *Una among the Fauns and Wood Nymphs*, more bare flesh, and *The Disarming of Cupid*. English Heritage's own curator has pointed out the irony: Victoria was the less prudish of the two, "open to nudity and the sensuous," rather more than Albert, who was surprisingly buttoned-up about it.

The most telling object is smaller and quieter. In 1843 Victoria commissioned Winterhalter to paint her in secret as a birthday surprise for Albert: not the Queen, but the wife, her hair unpinned and falling loose over one bare shoulder, her head against a red cushion, looking at the viewer with unmistakable warmth. She called it her "secret picture." Albert thought it so intimate he kept it in his private writing room and it became, by her account, his favourite painting of her. It was not shown to the public for well over a century. When the BBC finally aired it, the response was mild surprise that a queen had ever looked like that.

The estate is a monument to a very Victorian idea of family privacy, which is to say it was elaborately staged. The royal children each had a plot in the grounds and a scaled-down Swiss Cottage, imported in sections and rebuilt, where they learned to cook and garden and keep a small museum of their own curiosities. Down through the woods was the family's private beach, and there Victoria kept a bathing machine — a wheeled wooden hut with a changing room, a veranda, curtains and a plumbed-in lavatory, from which she could be trundled into the Solent and emerge to swim with her dignity intact. "I thought it delightful," she wrote of her first dip in 1847, "till I put my head under water, when I thought I should be stifled." The machine survives, having spent part of the twentieth century as a chicken coop, and now sits back on the beach, which reopened to the public in 2012.

For all the bliss, Victoria was clear-eyed about the price of it. She loathed being pregnant, disliked newborn babies, and called the whole business the "shadow side" of marriage. On 7 April 1853, giving birth to her eighth child, Prince Leopold, she accepted chloroform from Dr John Snow and recorded the result in her journal: "that blessed Chloroform" whose effect was "soothing, quieting & delightful beyond measure." It was a royal endorsement that did more to make pain relief in childbirth respectable than any medical paper could. Do the arithmetic her biographers recommend: nine children in seventeen years is not the record of a woman enduring her duty with her eyes shut.

Then, on 14 December 1861, Albert died at Windsor, probably of typhoid, aged forty-two. Victoria was thirty-two years a wife and would be nearly forty years a widow. She wore black for the rest of her life and died at Osborne in 1901 in the same devout grief.

Which is where the record starts to be tampered with — and it is worth being precise about who did the tampering. Victoria wrote roughly two thousand words a day for most of her life; 141 volumes of journal survive in one form or another. She instructed her youngest daughter, Princess Beatrice, to act as her literary executor. Beatrice spent about forty years transcribing the diaries covering 1837 to 1901 into fair copies — 111 volumes in all — omitting anything she judged might wound the living or the family's reputation, and then burning the originals. Of Victoria's own hand only thirteen early volumes survive, covering 1832 to 1836; everything after the accession we read in a daughter's expurgated version, made against the explicit objection of King George V and Queen Mary. What we have of the most documented woman of the nineteenth century is, for the crucial decades, a censored draft. Remember that when we get to Balmoral.

Balmoral is the other Victoria, and the more interesting evidential puzzle. Albert leased the Aberdeenshire estate in 1848 and bought it outright in 1852; the small existing castle was too cramped, so a new one was built a hundred yards off in pale local granite, designed by William Smith of Aberdeen to Albert's amendments and finished in 1856. It was Albert's Scottish idyll. After his death it became the stage for Victoria's most talked-about relationship.

John Brown was a Highland ghillie, a Balmoral outdoor servant, blunt to the point of rudeness with everyone including the Queen. From 1865 she made him her permanent personal attendant, and for the next eighteen years he was the closest person to her — organising her days, bullying her gently, going everywhere with her. The court, appalled, called her "Mrs Brown" behind her back. Her children loathed him. And when he died in March 1883 she grieved with an openness that unsettled everyone: she commissioned a life-sized statue of him, wrote warmly of him in the published *More Leaves from the Journal of a Life in the Highlands*, drafted a laudatory memoir of him that her household persuaded her to suppress, and asked to be buried with mementoes of him, including, it is recorded, a lock of his hair and his photograph placed in her hand.

Then the rumours: that they had secretly married, that there was even a child. This is where a popular-history book has to decide whether it is honest, so let us be. What is documented is the extraordinary closeness, the public grief, the memorials, the suppressed memoir. What is not documented — anywhere — is a marriage.

The single load-bearing piece of "evidence" for a wedding is a diary entry by Lewis Harcourt, later Viscount Harcourt, for 17 February 1885. Harcourt recorded that Lady Ponsonby, wife of the Queen's private secretary, had told his father, the Home Secretary, that a Miss Macleod said her brother — the Reverend Norman Macleod, one of Victoria's chaplains — had confessed on his deathbed that he had married the Queen to John Brown, and had "always bitterly regretted it." Count the handoffs. Macleod died in 1872; Harcourt was nine at the time and never heard the man; the story reaches him fourth-hand, from the sister, to Lady Ponsonby, to his father, to his diary, thirteen years later. Even Harcourt hedged: Miss Macleod "could have no object in inventing such a story, so that one is almost inclined to believe it, improbable as it sounds." That is not a witness. That is a man writing down a rumour and admitting he half-believes it. Historians treat it accordingly.

The secret-child story is thinner still, resting on a Victorian pamphleteer, and has no evidence behind it at all. Raymond Lamont-Brown's biography and later television documentaries have combed the same ground and turned up devotion in abundance and a marriage certificate never.

But here is the part that makes the story genuinely about power rather than gossip. After Victoria died in 1901, her son Edward VII — who had detested Brown all his life — set about destroying everything connected with him: busts smashed, photographs removed, and a file of Brown's letters to the Queen burned. Edward VII also had Victoria and Albert's private rooms at Osborne sealed. So the reason we cannot settle the John Brown question is not that nothing was written down. It is that two people, Beatrice and Edward, took it upon themselves to make the record go quiet, exactly as they made it go quiet elsewhere.

Set the two houses side by side and you have the whole argument of this book in one reign. Osborne is a queen who enjoyed sex within marriage and said so, in her own hand, in ink her daughter later burned. Balmoral is a widow whose intimacy with a servant the establishment could not file under any acceptable heading, and therefore tried to erase. In both cases the desire was ordinary and human. In both cases somebody with a poker and a grate decided we shouldn't know.



## 7. The House Where a King Gave It All Up

*Fort Belvedere, Windsor Great Park, Surrey, England · 1929–1936*


> **Fort Belvedere (Edward VIII & Wallis Simpson)** — Sunningdale, Surrey, England
> 51.40560° N, 0.62220° W · confidence 4/5


> `[PLATE 5 — Fort Belvedere (Edward VIII & Wallis Simpson) · 51.40560° N, 0.62220° W]`


Fort Belvedere is not open to the public, has never been open to the public, and — since it is a private lease on the Crown Estate, currently a family home — is not going to be open to the public. You cannot visit. This is worth stating plainly at the outset, because it is the one house in this book where the single most consequential act of British erotic history took place, and you will have to imagine it from the gates.

The act was a signature. On the evening of 10 December 1936, in a house he loved more than any other, King Edward VIII signed the Instrument of Abdication, renouncing the throne "for Myself and for My descendants," so that he could marry a twice-married American named Wallis Simpson. His three younger brothers witnessed it. The document survives at The National Archives; it is short, and it is the only time in British history a monarch has voluntarily walked away from the crown. He did it for a woman the establishment had decided was impossible, and he did it in his own drawing room.

To understand why the house matters, you have to understand what Edward had made of it. Fort Belvedere began as an eighteenth-century folly, a mock-castle built for the Duke of Cumberland around 1750 and remodelled by Jeffry Wyatville for George IV in the 1820s — battlements with no battle, a toy fort on Shrub's Hill overlooking Virginia Water. When it was offered to Edward, then Prince of Wales, in 1929, his father George V was baffled: "What could you possibly want that queer old place for? Those damn weekends I suppose." He signed it over anyway.

Edward adored it. "I came to love the Fort," he wrote later in his memoir *A King's Story*, "as I loved no other material thing — perhaps because it was so much my own creation." He tore out the undergrowth with his own hands, roped weekend guests into the gardening, put in a swimming pool where a lily pond had been, added a tennis court and a steam bath and central heating and en-suite bathrooms. He mixed the cocktails himself. Guests came for the weekend and found not a stiff royal household but a country-house party that ran late, with the heir to the throne in a kilt or a dressing gown doing needlepoint. To the old court it was scandalous informality. To Edward it was the only place he could breathe.

Into this circle, around 1931, came Wallis Simpson and her husband Ernest, introduced through Edward's then-mistress, Thelma, Viscountess Furness. For a while the two women were friends. Then, in January 1934, Thelma sailed for New York and — by her own rueful account in her memoirs — asked Wallis to look after the Prince while she was away. Wallis, she wrote drily, "looked after him exceedingly well." Thelma returned that spring to a cooling lover and a private joke she wasn't part of; at dinner she watched Wallis playfully slap the Prince's hand and understood the whole thing in a single cold glance. She left the next morning. The displacement was complete.

What actually passed between Edward and Wallis is the part where a responsible book slows down, because the lurid version is enormous and the evidence is small. For decades the relationship has been narrated through a fog of sexual speculation: that Wallis had learned exotic techniques in a Shanghai brothel, that she held some carnal power over a sexually inadequate prince, that a document called the "China Dossier" catalogued her depravities for a horrified Prime Minister. Almost none of this survives contact with a fact. The China Dossier has never been produced; no one has ever seen it; the historian Paul French, who went looking, concluded it never existed and that the stories in it were real scandals about other people, transposed onto Wallis by an intelligence officer to break up the romance. The "Shanghai grip" is a saloon-bar legend. What we actually have are two memoirs — his *A King's Story*, her *The Heart Has Its Reasons* — and a mass of letters showing an infatuation that ran, on his side, close to dependence. Their contemporaries and later biographers describe his need for her as obsessive. That is a real and documented dynamic, and it is far stranger and sadder than the brothel stories, which were largely hostile invention aimed at explaining why a king would do something so mad.

And it was, constitutionally, mad. Edward became king on his father's death in January 1936. Wallis was still married; her second divorce was heard at Ipswich that October, timed to clear the way. As king, Edward was Supreme Governor of a Church of England that did not then remarry divorced people while a former spouse lived, and Wallis had two living former husbands. Prime Minister Stanley Baldwin told him plainly that the country and the Dominions would not accept her as queen. The British press, by a gentlemen's agreement extraordinary to modern eyes, said nothing at all for months while the American and Continental papers roared; the silence broke only in early December, and then the crisis ran in days. Offered a choice between the throne and the woman, Edward chose the woman, signed the paper at the Fort, and broadcast to the nation the following night that he could not do the job "without the help and support of the woman I love." He had reigned 325 days.

Stand back and a pattern from earlier in this book snaps into focus. A century and a bit before, another Prince of Wales — the future George IV — had fallen for another unacceptable twice-married woman, Maria Fitzherbert, and had actually gone through a secret wedding with her in 1785. That marriage was void under the Royal Marriages Act 1772, which required the sovereign's consent, and George went on to his throne and his official, catastrophic marriage to Caroline of Brunswick, keeping Maria at the edge of his life. Two princes, two forbidden, twice-married women, the same 1772 Act hanging over both. The difference is the outcome. George IV kept the crown and sacrificed the woman. Edward VIII kept the woman and sacrificed the crown. You may decide for yourself which was the more honest, but only one of them signed away a throne for it, and he did it here, in a folly on a hill you cannot visit.

The Fort's later life is an odd coda. It was used by Crown Lands offices in the war, leased to a grandson of George V in the 1950s, briefly held by the son of the ruler of Dubai, and has for decades been a private home. The swimming pool Edward built is reportedly still there. The rest you must picture from the road.



\newpage

# Chapter 2 · The Scandal Sheets

*What the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries did to people who were caught*


> `[PLATE 6 — chapter frontispiece]`


Getting caught is not a constant. It is a technology, and in the eighteenth century it improved dramatically.

What changes between this chapter and the last is not that people started behaving worse. It is that a commercial press arrived, discovered that other people's marriages sold papers, and set about industrialising the business of exposure. Print runs rose; caricature became a national art form; the satirical print shops of St James's put engravings in their windows within days of a rumour reaching the clubs. By Amelia Rauser's count, at least eighty-nine different political prints survive from April 1784 alone, the month the Duchess of Devonshire canvassed for Charles James Fox in the Westminster election; many turned her into a duchess trading kisses for votes. The story was manufactured, distributed and believed at a speed that would have been impossible thirty years earlier.

The result is a strange asymmetry, and it is the engine of this chapter. The behaviour on display here is not obviously more scandalous than what came before. A ménage à trois ran openly at Chatsworth for twenty-five years — the Duke, the Duchess, and her closest friend Bess Foster, all under one roof, with children by both women in the nursery. Nelson, the nation's hero, lived at Merton Place with another man's wife and their daughter, and the entire country agreed, more or less politely, not to notice. What varies is not the conduct but the exposure: who gets written about, in what tone, and who is protected.

Protection, as ever, tracked money and sex in combination. Nelson could keep Emma Hamilton in Surrey because he had won the Nile; Emma, once he was dead and his codicil ignored, died in Calais with nothing. Georgiana was sent abroad to give birth in France and the child was raised elsewhere; the Duke's illegitimate children by Bess were simply absorbed into the household. Mary Robinson, the Maid of Buttermere, was famous for a fortnight, and the man who married her bigamously was hanged for forgery, which tells you what the eighteenth century actually valued.

And there is a second, quieter theme running underneath the noise, which is the sheer scale of what did not get written about at all. For every ducal ménage that generated a hundred prints there were thousands of arrangements — servants, tenants, the woman who kept house for a widower for thirty years — that generated nothing whatever. This chapter's Edinburgh brothel-keeper was a public figure in her own city, prosecuted repeatedly, entirely tolerated in practice, and yet the exact prices she charged do not survive in any open source. Fame is a preservative; obscurity is not.

So the scandal sheets should be read the way one reads any hostile witness: carefully, and with attention to what the witness wanted. They are frequently the only record we have. They are also, almost without exception, produced by men with something to sell, aimed at women with something to lose. Georgiana's kisses, whether or not she ever gave one, were a political attack dressed as gossip. That the attack still shapes how she is remembered, two and a half centuries on, is a fair measure of how well this technology worked.



## 8. Georgiana, Bess and the Duke

*Chatsworth House, Derbyshire, England · 1774–1811*


> **Chatsworth (Georgiana's ménage à trois)** — Bakewell, Derbyshire, England
> 53.22750° N, 1.61070° W · confidence 5/5


A duke, his duchess, and the duchess's best friend, all under one enormous roof for the better part of twenty-five years. The duke and the best friend are lovers, and have two children together. The duchess, meanwhile, has a daughter by a different man entirely, borne in secret in France and given away. Everybody more or less knows. The arrangement holds, through gossip and gambling debts and a revolution across the Channel, because at this level of society the rules about who slept with whom were startlingly elastic — provided you did not get caught in a way that could not be smoothed over, and provided you were a man.

Georgiana Spencer was seventeen when she married William Cavendish, fifth Duke of Devonshire, on her birthday in June 1774. He was the most eligible bachelor in England, master of Chatsworth in Derbyshire and Devonshire House in London, and about as warm as the marble he owned. She was dazzling — a fashion leader before the phrase existed, a political hostess who could fill a room, a genuine celebrity in the first modern sense, mobbed in the street and reported in the papers for the height of her hair and the droop of her ostrich feathers. The marriage was a mismatch of temperature from the start. He wanted an heir and his clubs; she wanted to be loved and, failing that, to be adored by everyone else.

She threw herself into Whig politics, and here we meet the first of this chapter's cases of the lurid version outrunning the evidence. In the Westminster election of 1784 Georgiana campaigned hard for Charles James Fox, going out into the streets to canvass tradesmen — extraordinary conduct for a duchess. The Tory press destroyed her for it. According to the art historian Amelia Rauser, at least eighty-nine different political prints survive from the month of April 1784 alone, many of them showing her trading kisses for votes, kissing butchers, selling herself for Fox. The image has stuck for two centuries. What is documented underneath it is real but far smaller: she certainly canvassed, certainly charmed, and there is a letter, quoted by her biographer Amanda Foreman, in which Georgiana herself refers ruefully to the kissing. But the butcher-kissing duchess of the caricatures is largely a construction of misogynist print culture, a way of punishing a woman for doing politics in public by recasting it as prostitution. The kiss became, as one scholar puts it, political theatre. Fox won his seat. Georgiana paid for it in reputation.

The debts were real and enormous. Georgiana gambled catastrophically — faro, mostly — and concealed the scale of it from her husband for years, borrowing to cover losses and losing to cover borrowing, in a spiral that would have ruined a lesser house. This mattered more than it should because of the other pressure on her: the duke needed a son, and for a long time she produced daughters and miscarriages. "Little G" arrived in 1783, "Harryo" in 1785, and only in 1790 the longed-for heir, William. Aristocratic wives were valued for fertility as much as dowry, and Georgiana felt every year of the wait.

Into the middle of this, in 1782, walked Lady Elizabeth Foster — "Bess" — separated from her husband, cut off from her children, clever, charming and effectively penniless. Georgiana adored her on sight and brought her into the household. Bess proceeded to make herself indispensable to both Devonshires: bosom friend to the wife, and mistress to the husband. The ménage à trois that resulted was singular even by Georgian standards and lasted, in one form or another, until Georgiana's death. Bess bore the duke two illegitimate children, Caroline St Jules and Augustus Clifford, who were folded into the Chatsworth nursery alongside the legitimate ones. Whether Georgiana tolerated this out of love for Bess, or dependence, or because Bess had leverage over her debts, her contemporaries could not agree and neither can we; her own mother thought Bess a con artist of the first order.

The double standard arrives with brutal clarity when Georgiana finally takes a lover of her own. Having secured the succession, she fell for Charles Grey, the young, rising Whig — the same Grey who would decades later, as Earl Grey and Prime Minister, carry the Great Reform Act, and who has a blend of tea named after him. In 1791 she became pregnant by him. The duke's response tells you everything about whose desires were permitted. A mistress in the house was tolerable; a straying duchess was not. He gave her a choice: divorce and disgrace, or exile. To keep contact with her legitimate children she chose exile, and in February 1792, in Aix-en-Provence, she gave birth to Grey's daughter, Eliza Courtney. The baby was handed to Grey's parents to raise as their own. Georgiana was allowed to know Eliza only as a kind of family friend, a godmother; Eliza learned who her mother really was only after Georgiana died. Grey, for his part, married someone else in 1794. He kept his career. Georgiana kept her guilt.
