She came home in 1793 on the duke's terms, having promised to withdraw from the fray, and lived out her remaining years in the ménage that everyone had by now stopped pretending not to see, her health wrecked by illness and by the medicines and drink she used to get through the days. She died in 1806, aged forty-eight. With what looks like her blessing, Bess became the next Duchess of Devonshire three years later, marrying the duke at last in 1809 — the mistress promoted to wife over the body of the friend who had loved them both.

What survives of Georgiana at Chatsworth is worth being precise about, because the house is run by the Devonshire family still and the archive is a real one. The Devonshire Collection holds her papers — notebooks, correspondence, the material historians actually work from — and the house shows portraits, including Gainsborough's, and personal relics down to a lock of her hair. It is important to separate that documentary base from the two things that made Georgiana famous again in our own time: Amanda Foreman's 1998 biography, which is superb and which mined the family papers properly, and the 2008 film *The Duchess*, which mined Foreman and Keira Knightley's cheekbones and which pressed hard on the parallel with Diana, Princess of Wales — Georgiana's own descendant, and another woman who observed that there were three people in her marriage. The parallel is genuine but it flattens Georgiana into a victim, and she was a great deal more than that: a political operator, a writer, a scientist of sorts, and a woman who ran, and was run by, one of the most openly unconventional households in England.

That openness is the point. The Devonshire ménage worked precisely because it belonged to a class that could afford not to care, at the exact moment when the middle class beneath it was busy inventing the idea that such things were unthinkable. Chatsworth in the 1780s and the moralising drawing rooms of the 1830s are only a few decades apart. What changed was not human desire. What changed was who got to be exempt from the rules about it.



## 9. The Arrangement the Nation Agreed Not to See

*Merton Place, Merton, Surrey (now London Borough of Merton), England · 1801–1815*


> **Merton Place (Nelson & Emma Hamilton)** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.41417° N, 0.19417° W · confidence 2/5


> `[PLATE 7 — Merton Place (Nelson & Emma Hamilton) · 51.41417° N, 0.19417° W]`


Emma Hamilton was handed from one man to another to pay off a debt, and the paperwork survives. That sentence ought to be shocking, and the fact that it usually isn't — that Emma is remembered as a scandal, a schemer, a fallen woman — is itself the scandal this chapter is about.

She was born Amy Lyon in 1765 at Ness in Cheshire, the daughter of a blacksmith who died when she was two months old. Raised by her mother and grandmother in relative poverty across the Welsh border, she went into service young, drifted to London, and by her mid-teens was surviving on her looks and her wits in a city that had very few honest uses for a poor and beautiful girl. She modelled; she was mistress to a baronet who threw her out when she fell pregnant; she became the mistress of the Hon. Charles Greville, who set her up, renamed her Emma Hart, and had her painted again and again by George Romney until her face was famous across the country.

Then Greville needed money and a rich wife, and Emma was in the way of both. So in 1786 he shipped her to Naples, to his widowed uncle Sir William Hamilton, the British envoy — telling Emma it was a holiday and that he would follow. He never followed. The arrangement between the two men was, in effect, a transaction: Sir William would take Emma off Greville's hands, and Greville would remain his uncle's heir, undisturbed by any late remarriage. Emma worked it out slowly, in a foreign city, and her letters as the truth dawned are unbearable. "I love you," she wrote to Greville; and, realising: "if I was with you I would murder you and myself both." She was twenty-one, and she had been sold to clear another man's accounts.

Here is where the ordinary story would have her disappear. Instead Emma did something remarkable: she became, on her own terms, one of the most celebrated women in Europe. She learned Italian and French, sang well enough to be offered an opera contract, befriended the Queen of Naples, and invented a performance art all her own — her "Attitudes," in which, draped in shawls, she moved through a sequence of poses from classical sculpture and painting so vividly that Goethe travelled to see them and wrote about them. Sir William, to his credit, fell genuinely in love with the woman he had been handed, and in 1791 he married her. The blacksmith's daughter became Lady Hamilton.

And then, in 1798, Horatio Nelson came to Naples after the Battle of the Nile, and the most famous love affair in British naval history began under the roof, and with the knowledge, of Emma's husband. It is easy to be arch about this, and pointless. The three of them lived together and defended the arrangement in public as a pure friendship — Emma liked to call the household *tria juncta in uno*, "three joined in one," which is the motto of the Order of the Bath both men happened to belong to. Whether Sir William believed his own line or simply loved them both enough to look away, the trio held together until his death in April 1803, and he died holding Emma's hand with Nelson in the room.

Their daughter arrived in the middle of it. Horatia was born in late January 1801, and the machinery of concealment around her is a small masterpiece of Georgian hypocrisy: Nelson wrote to Emma about the baby in code, casting himself and Emma as a sailor named "Thomson" and his wife, so that letters could be read aloud or intercepted without exposing them. The child was farmed out to a nurse in Marylebone and only later brought into the household as an "adopted" ward. Everyone who mattered understood exactly what was going on, and everyone agreed, for the sake of the man winning Britain's war at sea, not to say so out loud.

The house at the centre of all this was Merton Place, in what was then rural Surrey. Nelson bought it in 1801 for £9,000 — a substantial sum, well into the low millions in today's money — while he was away at sea, which meant Emma chose it, inspected it, and furnished it on his behalf. He never haggled; against his solicitor's advice he refused to offer less. It became, in his own worn phrase, "dear, dear Merton," the only house he ever owned and the nearest thing to a home he ever had. Emma filled it with guests and dinners and improvements, dug a branch of water in the grounds that the household nicknamed "the Nile," and made it a paradise the three of them shared until Sir William died and, after him, the two of them and their small daughter.

In August 1805 Nelson came home to Merton for twenty-five days, his last spell of happiness, and then left it "to go to serve my King and Country." On 21 October, in sight of the combined fleets off Cape Trafalgar, he wrote the codicil that is the moral heart of this story. Reciting Emma's services to the nation, he wrote: "I leave Emma Lady Hamilton, therefore, a legacy to my King and Country, that they will give her an ample provision to maintain her rank in life." He commended their daughter Horatia to the country's care too. "These," he added, "are the only favours I ask of my King and Country, at this moment when I am going to fight their battle." Hours later he was dead.

The nation took the victory and ignored the codicil entirely. Nelson's clergyman brother William was created Earl Nelson and granted a perpetual pension of £5,000 a year; Parliament later voted £90,000 to buy him an estate, which became Trafalgar Park in Wiltshire. His sisters were handsomely provided for; the state paid for a funeral of imperial splendour at St Paul's, from which Emma — as a mistress — was excluded. Emma herself received nothing from the Crown. She had money from Sir William and from Nelson and the house at Merton, and with care it might have lasted; but she had never been taught care, and grief and generosity and debt did the rest. She was forced to try to sell Merton, could not find a buyer, and it passed out of her hands. She was twice arrested for debt. In 1814, broken and drinking, she crossed to Calais with Horatia to escape her creditors, and there, on 15 January 1815, she died in poverty. She was forty-nine. The daughter Nelson had "left to his country" was raised by his relations and went to her own grave still denying that Emma had been her mother.

Merton Place itself is gone — demolished by 1823, the estate broken into building lots, the "Nile" filled in, the paradise buried under Victorian terraces and, later, twentieth-century council housing and lock-ups. This is why the Marquis's camera has to work harder here than anywhere. What survives is a scatter of names and markers: streets called Nelson, Trafalgar, Victory, Hardy and Hamilton laid over the old grounds; a small Lord Nelson memorial with two cannon beside the church of St John the Divine, near South Wimbledon; Nelson Gardens, where local schoolchildren long raised flags each Trafalgar Day; a plaque near Merton High Street marking where the house stood; and the Merton council's Nelson Trail stitching the fragments together. It is a curiously honest kind of ruin — a great man's happiness reduced to street signs, and beside it, unmarked, the erasure of the woman who made the happiness and was thrown away the moment its guarantor was gone.



## 10. Lakeland Notoriety: The Maid of Buttermere and Wicked Jimmy

*The Fish Inn, Buttermere & Lowther Castle, near Penrith, Cumbria (historically Cumberland and Westmorland), England · 1792–1814*


> **The Maid of Buttermere & the Bigamist 'Colonel'** — Buttermere, Cumbria (Cumberland), England
> 54.54130° N, 3.27660° W · confidence 4/5
>
> **Wicked Jimmy — the Bad Earl of Lonsdale** — Lowther (near Penrith), Cumbria (Westmorland), England
> 54.60100° N, 2.72200° W · confidence 4/5


Mary Robinson's misfortune was to be beautiful in the exact decade that beauty in a remote place became a tourist attraction. In 1792 a retired army captain named Joseph Budworth published *A Fortnight's Ramble to the Lakes*, one of the first guidebooks to the newly fashionable Lake District, and in it he devoted an embarrassing quantity of prose to the teenage daughter of the innkeeper at Buttermere in Cumberland — her dark hair, her fine oval face, her lips "as red as vermilion," crowning her the beauty of the valley. It was, in effect, a review, and it worked as reviews do. Sightseers began arriving at the Fish Inn specifically to look at Mary, who had to go on carrying their ale while they gawped and the Lake Poets composed remarks about her. She had become a destination before she had done anything at all.

Ten years of this had passed when, in the summer of 1802, the trap arrived in the shape of a charming stranger. He called himself the Hon. Colonel Alexander Augustus Hope, brother of the Earl of Hopetoun and a Member of Parliament, and he courted Mary with the confidence of a man who was none of those things. He was in fact John Hatfield, a serial impostor and undischarged bankrupt with a string of abandoned wives and forged bills behind him, working the Lakes for whatever credit a borrowed title could raise. He married Mary at Lorton church on 2 October 1802.

And here the story turns on a piece of writing, which is why it belongs in a book about how the record is made. The Keswick correspondent of the *Morning Post* filed a charmed little item about the match — a real colonel's brother marrying a humble Lakeland beauty — under the heading "Romantic Marriage." The correspondent was Samuel Taylor Coleridge. His report, published on 11 October, was meant as a piece of picturesque human interest; instead it detonated the fraud, because people who knew the genuine Colonel Hope was abroad read it and asked how he could be honeymooning in Cumberland. Coleridge, realising what he had set off, followed up with further accounts as the impostor was unmasked. Hatfield fled, was hunted down near Swansea, and was brought back for trial.

He was hanged at Carlisle on 3 September 1803 — and here is a detail that says everything about the age's priorities. Hatfield was not executed for the bigamy that made him a monster in the public mind, nor for breaking Mary's heart. He was hanged for forgery, then a capital offence, for faking bills and franking letters with an MP's signature. The state did not much care whom he had deceived into bed; it cared that he had defrauded the mail and the banks. Mary, pregnant by him, bore a child that was stillborn.

She survived it. Wordsworth, who had known of her before the scandal, wrote her into Book Seven of *The Prelude* as the "Maid of Buttermere," an emblem of natural virtue wronged by metropolitan vice, and the public, having gawped at her beauty and then at her ruin, raised subscriptions for her. In 1808 she married a Caldbeck farmer, Richard Harrison, had four children, and lived quietly until her death in 1837. She is buried at St Kentigern's, Caldbeck. Melvyn Bragg, a Cumbrian himself, novelised the whole affair in 1987. The Fish Inn is still there, still a working inn at the foot of Honister Pass, still trading gently on the legend of the maid who once carried the drinks.

Twenty miles east, and cut from entirely different cloth, sat the man who casts the longest shadow over the Lakeland of this period: Sir James Lowther, first Earl of Lonsdale, known the length of two counties as "Wicked Jimmy" and "Jemmy Grasp-all." He was, by broad agreement, the richest and most detested man in the north of England, a coal and land magnate who controlled a bloc of parliamentary seats so completely that his tame Members were nicknamed his "Ninepins." The Dictionary of National Biography, no scandal sheet, records Alexander Carlyle's verdict that Lowther was "more detested than any man alive" and, in Carlyle's phrase, "truly a madman, though too rich to be confined."

He touches this book, and the Wordsworth circle, through money and through a corpse. The money first, because it is solid. Lowther employed John Wordsworth — the poet's father — as his law-agent, and over two decades ran up a debt to him of some £4,000 in expenses which he simply declined to pay. When John Wordsworth died in 1783, the debt died unpaid with him, and the five orphaned Wordsworth children, William among them, grew up in reduced and anxious circumstances directly because the wealthiest man in the region would not settle his bill. Only after Wicked Jimmy's death in 1802 did his heir pay the sum, with interest, freeing William to marry and to write. The poet's whole early life was shaped by one rich man's meanness.

Now the corpse, and here we must attribute rather than assert, because the story is famous and the evidence is thin and late. The tale runs that Lowther loved the daughter of one of his tenant farmers, made her his mistress, and, when she died young, could not bear to bury her — that he kept the body until it could no longer be kept, and finally had it embalmed under glass. Its earliest respectable source is Thomas De Quincey, who set it down in his *Recollections of the Lakes and the Lake Poets*, not as fact but expressly as Cumberland hearsay: it was, he wrote, a story "I have often heard repeated by the country people of Cumberland." And De Quincey's own version is markedly gentler than the one that circulates now. He says only that Lowther loved a young woman of humble parentage, that she died young, and that in his grief he had her embalmed with a glass placed over her features, which he visited. The lurid accretions — that he refused burial outright, dined beside a rotting body, drove the servants out with the smell, kept her seven weeks, stowed her in a cupboard — appear nowhere in De Quincey and nowhere in any reputable biography. The Dictionary of National Biography, which cheerfully records his tyranny and his madness, does not mention the woman at all. She is never reliably named; the "Betsy" of some modern retellings has no source behind it. So: an early, named witness reporting a genuine piece of local folklore about an embalming; and, layered on top over two centuries, a horror story that owes more to the tellers than to Lowther. The truth is grim enough without the cupboard.

The castle you can actually visit belongs to the aftermath, not to Wicked Jimmy. He died in 1802 leaving no legitimate son; his heir, William Lowther, pulled down the old hall and had Robert Smirke build a vast Gothic pile between 1806 and 1814. That is Lowther Castle — and it too came to grief, abandoned and unroofed in the twentieth century, so that what stands today is a magnificent shell, a ruined stone face fronting restored gardens, open to visitors who wander through rooms that have no ceilings.

Put the two Lakeland stories together and the same landscape holds both. The same hills that made Mary Robinson a tourist attraction made the Lake Poets who wrote her down and who suffered under Lowther's thumb; Coleridge exposed the impostor who ruined the innkeeper's daughter, and Wordsworth's childhood was mortgaged to the tyrant up the road. Beauty and money, the powerless and the powerful, and running between them a thread of poets who turned all of it into literature — and who return, later in this book, to Dove Cottage and Greta Hall to do it again.



## 11. A YMCA With Extras

*17 Danube Street · Stockbridge, Edinburgh, Scotland · c.1946–1977*


> **17 Danube Street (Dora Noyce's establishment)** — Edinburgh, Midlothian, Scotland
> 55.95850° N, 3.20780° W · confidence 4/5


> `[PLATE 8 — 17 Danube Street (Dora Noyce's establishment) · 55.95850° N, 3.20780° W]`


She wore a fur coat, a twinset and pearls, kept a blue rinse, spoke in an accent she had manufactured herself, and when the police arrived to raid her brothel she is said to have opened the door and asked, sweetly, "Business or pleasure, gentlemen?" Dora Noyce was the most famous madam in Scotland, and almost nothing about her was quite what it appeared, beginning with the name.

She was born Georgina — "Georgie" — Hunter Rae in 1900, in Rose Street, then one of the poorer and rougher lanes tucked behind the douce Georgian frontages of Edinburgh's New Town. Her father was a cutler. She went into prostitution young, before she was twenty, and somewhere along the way acquired both a daughter, Violet, born in 1923, and the surname of the child's father, Ernest Noyce, whom she seems to have regarded chiefly as a friend. She also acquired the plummy vowels of Morningside, Edinburgh's most self-consciously refined suburb, on the sound commercial principle that a better accent brought a better class of customer. Her first conviction for living on immoral earnings came in 1934.

The house at 17 Danube Street opened for business shortly after the Second World War and ran until Dora's death, an elegant terraced property on the northern edge of the New Town, a few minutes' walk from the water of Leith. She owned two floors. According to Tom Wood, later Deputy Chief Constable of Lothian and Borders Police, who walked the Stockbridge beat as a young constable in Dora's final years, she kept the upstairs flat for herself and her guests and sublet the basement to her women, where it was divided into small cubicles. Peter Ritchie, a former detective superintendent, recalled the arrangement more bluntly: the top floor for the "high heid-yins", the bottom for "you and me".

What the record documents about the interior is modest and, refreshingly, unglamorous. Customers arriving upstairs were offered coffee, sandwiches and biscuits while they decided which woman took their fancy — this from David McLean's account in The Scotsman. The writer Roddy Martine, who was taken there on a stag night in the early 1970s and used no services, remembered it as "rather scruffy": a big television, women on sofas, drunken men, and Dora circulating with glasses of dry white wine, asking the suddenly sobered party whether they were sailors. That is the closest we have to an eyewitness inventory, and it is worth holding on to, because the estate-agent language of "lavishly furnished A-listed rooms" attached to the house belongs to its 2020 sale particulars, not to Dora's tenure.

She employed fifteen women in permanent residence and could draw on up to twenty-five more at short notice — arriving, the story goes, by taxi from Glasgow — when trade was brisk. The women had regular medical checks, a point Dora made much of. She hated the word "brothel" and preferred "house of leisure and pleasure", or, in her single most quoted line, "a YMCA — with the one little difference". She called the women her "business girls". Her busiest fortnights, she liked to tell reporters with a straight face, were the Edinburgh Festival and the annual General Assembly of the Church of Scotland.

The prices are where the record goes frustratingly quiet. No tariff or price list for Dora's survives in any openly available source; the fullest scholarly treatment, Roger Davidson's chapter "'An Open and Notorious House of Lewdness'" in the Edinburgh University Press volume Illicit and Unnatural Practices (2018), promises detail on "sexual transactions" but sits behind a paywall, and the exact figures charged remain, for now, undocumented in public. What we do have are the folkloric takings. When the American aircraft carrier USS John F. Kennedy was in the Firth of Forth in 1970, the house is said, in the words repeated by Wikipedia and the Edinburgh Evening News, to have done "£4,000 of business in one night" before "the ship's captain declared the house off-limits" — something like £60,000 in today's money — the queue reputedly stretching round to neighbouring Ann Street. Every source hedges this with "reportedly" and "it is said", and so should we; it is a very good story that has been polished by long handling. A word of caution on one further claim: the connection sometimes made to the 1961 arrival of the US Polaris fleet at the Holy Loch is not supported by any source I could find — the loch is on the west coast, a long way from Leith, and the only documented US Navy episode is the Kennedy.

The law's relationship with Dora was a study in pragmatism. She was charged exactly forty-seven times for living off immoral earnings across her career, mostly paying fines — up to £250, roughly £2,500 today — and lying low for a few days before resuming. Genuine prison was rare; her last sentence, four months, came in 1972 when she was seventy-one. Tom Wood explained the arrangement without embarrassment: you can ignore the sex trade, which is foolish, or try to suppress it, which is impossible, or you can regulate it and at least know who the players are. Danube Street would be raided roughly every six months; in return Dora passed the police useful information about stolen goods and the wider underworld. It was toleration by handshake. Tellingly, when she was jailed in 1972 the council received more complaints about the house, not fewer — because it was run less well without her.

She was, incongruously, a Conservative, and hung party posters in her windows at election time, an act of respectability-by-association that nobody has ever satisfactorily explained. She went to Kirk. She embarrassed her MP by turning up at garden fêtes. And she understood publicity better than most: leaving court, she would remind the reporters to print the correct address, "because in my profession there is no such thing as bad publicity".

Dora Noyce died in 1977, aged seventy-seven. The business limped on briefly without her and closed; the house was divided into flats and, as the Scottish Daily Express records, sold for £1.57 million in August 2020 — against a street average of £1,449,000. Half a century on, the New Town's most notorious address is worth almost exactly what its respectable neighbours are, which is a joke Dora, connoisseur of Edinburgh's talent for keeping its appetites and its appearances in separate rooms, would have appreciated. She belongs to a longer line of British brothel-keepers who prospered precisely by looking like the last people on earth who would; the London madams of later chapters ran the same trick with more velvet and worse manners.



## 12. Respectable Façades

*Castle Ward, Strangford, Co. Down · The Crown Liquor Saloon, Belfast · Tiger Bay, Butetown, Cardiff · Llandudno, Conwy · c.1762–1901*


> **Castle Ward (the marriage-split house)** — Strangford, County Down, Northern Ireland
> 54.36722° N, 5.57194° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **The Crown Liquor Saloon** — Belfast, County Antrim, Northern Ireland
> 54.59473° N, 5.93430° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Tiger Bay (Cardiff's dockland demi-monde)** — Cardiff, Cardiff, Wales
> 51.46694° N, 3.16611° W · confidence 2/5
>
> **The Llandudno Mixed-Bathing Moral Panic** — Llandudno, Conwy, Wales
> 53.32500° N, 3.82600° W · confidence 3/5


Walk up to Castle Ward today from the landscaped side and you are looking at a sober, correct, Palladian country house: a pediment, four engaged Ionic columns, the architecture of reason and restraint. Walk round to the front facing Strangford Lough and you are looking at a different building entirely — pointed Gothic windows, battlements, pinnacles, the whole Strawberry Hill fancy-dress. It is the same house. It was built that way on purpose, in County Down in the early 1760s, and the interiors keep up the quarrel room by room: classical on his side, Gothic on hers, the split running down the middle of the plan like a line drawn on a map by two people who have stopped speaking.

The couple were Bernard Ward, later 1st Viscount Bangor, and his wife Lady Anne, daughter of the 1st Earl of Darnley. He liked the classical; she preferred, in the withering word of their contemporary Mrs Delany, the "whimsical" Gothic. Delany, watching the project rise, doubted both their judgements — "he wants taste, and Lady Anne is so whimsical that I doubt her judgment" — and a compromise was struck: his taste for the entrance front, hers for the garden front. It is the most photogenic marital disagreement in the National Trust's portfolio, and the temptation to read the architecture as a diagram of a failing marriage is almost irresistible. Lady Anne did leave Bernard, in 1770, shortly after the house was finished, and never came back.

Here honesty is required, because the story has hardened into something the evidence does not quite support. The Wards were married in 1747 and had, by most counts, seven or eight children together; Anne stayed a great many years before she left. Whether the divided house caused the separation, expressed it, or simply coincided with a couple who wanted a fashionable home incorporating both of the age's competing styles, we cannot say. The National Trust's own historians now lean towards the less dramatic reading — two tastes under one roof — while acknowledging how much later storytelling has done to marry the bricks to the breakup. What is genuinely documented, and more interesting, is Lady Anne herself: an independent-minded woman whose family were thought eccentric to the point of derangement, and who, before her marriage, had a years-long and tenderly written attachment to another woman, Letitia Bushe. The house divided is a wonderful metaphor. It is just not quite a proven one.

Fifty-odd miles north, in Belfast, stands a building whose reputation runs the opposite way — a place where the salacious story is the invention and the truth is quieter. The Crown Liquor Saloon on Great Victoria Street, refitted to its present High Victorian glory in 1885 by the Flanagan family, is the finest surviving gin palace in these islands: polychrome tiles, stained glass full of fairies and pineapples and clowns, a red granite altar of a bar, gaslight, and ten carved wooden snugs. The snugs are the thing everyone wants to talk about. Each has a door that latches, a gunmetal plate for striking matches, and a bell-push that once summoned a waiter without the occupants having to show their faces. And the tourism copy, with a nudge, will tell you they were "rumoured" to be used by prostitutes and their clients.

They were not — or at least, nothing whatever supports it. The claim appears nowhere in the statutory listing or in the National Trust's own account of the building; it lives only in the hedged, winking language of guidebooks. What the snugs were actually for is both documented and, if you think about it, more human: privacy. They let respectable Victorians — women of standing, courting couples, clergy, anyone who wanted a drink unobserved in an age that policed being seen far more fiercely than being — enjoy themselves out of the public eye. The doors, the frosted glass, the bell that meant you never had to stand at the bar: this is the architecture of discretion, not of vice. The Crown is a monument to the Victorian genius for keeping the appearance of propriety intact while quietly doing as one pleased, which is a subtler and truer erotic history than the one the postcards are selling.

Cardiff's Tiger Bay demands a different register altogether, because here the moral panic did real harm to real people. Butetown grew up from the 1830s around the coal docks built by the Marquess of Bute, and by the late nineteenth century it was one of Britain's first genuinely multi-ethnic communities — seafarers and their families from Somalia, Yemen, the Caribbean, West Africa, China, Greece and dozens of other places, settled among the boarding houses, pubs and dance halls, with Loudoun Square as its heart. By 1894, according to Cardiff's maritime records, there were 178 lodging houses in Butetown, four times as many as in any other British seaport, and a quarter of them were kept by foreigners — some of them "crimps" who ensnared sailors into debt. There was a dockside sex trade, as there is in every great port. But what the Cardiff and national press fixed upon, obsessively, was not the trade so much as the sight of interracial relationships — white women with Black and Arab men — which contemporaries wrote about in language of open disgust.

That framing turned lethal in June 1919, when returning white servicemen and local crowds attacked Butetown's minority households over four days of rioting. Boarding houses were besieged and burned; families armed themselves and barricaded their doors; broken glass covered Bute Street; and the violence and nearly all the arrests fell on the very residents who had been attacked. Official accounts recorded three deaths across the South Wales disturbances, though eyewitnesses in Butetown put the number higher, at six. The trigger, tellingly, was reported as an incident involving a white woman and a Black man. The lesson the authorities drew was not to protect the community but to restrict it. Loudoun Square's fine Victorian houses, which could have been saved, were swept away in the slum clearances of the 1960s and replaced with tower blocks. The racism belonged to Tiger Bay's accusers, never to Tiger Bay; the district's own memory of itself, preserved now by the Heritage and Cultural Exchange, is of a place its people called home.

And then, for light relief that is also entirely serious, there is Llandudno and the great Victorian panic about mixed bathing. On the beaches of the Conwy resort, as at scores of others, men and women were kept strictly apart in the water by byelaw — separate ladies' and gentlemen's bathing grounds, and the wheeled bathing machines that trundled the bather down to the sea so that no stretch of costumed body need be glimpsed crossing open sand. The costumes themselves, it should be said, covered practically everything: neck-to-knee flannel for women, drawers-and-vest for men, garments that grew heavier and more concealing the wetter they got. What the rules governed, in other words, was not nakedness. It was proximity — the mere possibility that a clothed man and a clothed woman might enter the same water within sight of one another.

The absurdity is best captured by a case the Yorkshire Evening Post reported in 1896: a man visiting Llandudno was summonsed for "unlawfully bathing within 200 yards of the ladies' bathing ground" — the ladies in question being his own three sisters. He had gone for a swim with his family and committed a crime by doing so. Legal segregation of Britain's beaches ended in 1901, and the bathing machines rattled into obsolescence within a couple of decades. The panic connects to a much wider nineteenth-century project of policing the body, which runs from these beaches to the surveillance of the poor and the sex worker; and it makes the argument that binds these four places together. From a divided Irish house to a Belfast snug, from a slandered Cardiff dockland to a Welsh beach, the nineteenth century reserved its heaviest punishments not for the act but for the appearance of impropriety — and, as ever, the punishment fell hardest on women and the poor.



\newpage

# Chapter 3 · Rakes, Monks and Pleasure Gardens

*The organised pursuit of pleasure, 1660–1930*


> `[PLATE 9 — chapter frontispiece]`


Somewhere around the Restoration, the English worked out that pleasure could be organised — that it could be built, staffed, advertised, timetabled and sold by the ticket. This chapter is about what they built, and about the persistent gap between the brochure and the premises.

The gap is the point, and the Hellfire Club is the purest demonstration of it. Sir Francis Dashwood's friends met at Medmenham Abbey and later in the chalk caves at West Wycombe, dressed as monks, drank a great deal of claret, and called the women they brought with them nuns. That much is solid. Almost everything else — the black masses, the Satanism, the orgiastic rites in the inner chamber — descends from a hostile novel of the 1760s and from the political vendetta of a former member. The caves themselves were dug as famine relief, providing paid work for local labourers. The most notorious sex club in English history was, in its origins, a public works scheme with a fancy-dress theme, and it has been dining out on the rumour ever since.

Vauxhall was the honest version, and considerably cleverer. For a shilling the visitor got twelve acres of designed possibility: supper boxes hung with paintings, an orchestra, triumphal arches, a thousand oil lamps lit simultaneously at a whistle — and, deliberately retained among all that illumination, a set of unlit walks. Jonathan Tyers installed the lights to make the gardens respectable and kept the shadows because respectability alone did not pay. That double instinct, selling the light and the dark in the same enclosure at the same price, is the commercial logic of the entire chapter.

What such places really sold was not sex but proximity and deniability — the chance to be somewhere other people were, in conditions that permitted a great deal to happen and nothing to be admitted. The shilling was the control mechanism as much as the price. Half a crown at Ranelagh bought a better class of company; a guinea at a masquerade bought anonymity along with it.

The private houses in this chapter ran on the same principle at a smaller scale. Byron kept a skull cup at Newstead and dressed his friends as monks, in what looks like deliberate homage to Dashwood, which is theatre borrowed from theatre. The Delavals of Northumberland spent a fortune on practical jokes and amateur dramatics. Bloomsbury, arriving a century and a half later, dispensed with the costumes and kept the essential arrangement: a house, a set of friends, and a private understanding that the ordinary rules were suspended within its walls.

The through-line from Medmenham to Charleston is not licentiousness. It is the discovery that if you assemble the right group of people in the right building and agree on the rules in advance, you can construct a small temporary jurisdiction in which different things are permitted. That is what a pleasure garden is, what a club is, and what a country house full of painters is. This chapter is about people who understood that the surest way round a rule is to build somewhere it does not apply.



## 13. The Dark Walks

*Vauxhall Pleasure Gardens · Lambeth, London · 1661–1859*


> **Vauxhall Pleasure Gardens (the Dark Walks)** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.48750° N, 0.12083° W · confidence 4/5


> `[PLATE 10 — Vauxhall Pleasure Gardens (the Dark Walks) · 51.48750° N, 0.12083° W]`


Fanny Burney knew exactly what happened at the far end of the gardens, and in 1778 she put it in a novel. In Evelina, her heroine strays with the wrong companions into Vauxhall's unlit avenues and is set upon, seized and very nearly assaulted before she can struggle free. Burney's readers did not need the geography explained. Everyone knew about the Dark Walks. They were the whole reason a certain sort of person went to Vauxhall, and the whole reason a certain sort of parent forbade it.

For most of two centuries Vauxhall was the most famous pleasure garden in the world. It opened as the New Spring Gardens before the Restoration — Samuel Pepys was there in 1662 — and became something extraordinary after 1729, when the entrepreneur Jonathan Tyers took the lease and reinvented it. His grand opening, a masked ridotto al fresco, was held in June 1732 in the presence of Frederick, Prince of Wales. Over the following decades Tyers filled twelve acres on the south bank of the Thames with supper boxes decorated by Francis Hayman, a great Rotunda, an orchestra, triumphal arches, a statue of Handel, thousands upon thousands of oil lamps, and — the young William Wordsworth, visiting at eighteen, was struck by it — a "wilderness of lamps, dimming the stars".

The genius of the place, and the point of it for this book, was the mixing. Admission was a single shilling for most of the eighteenth century, and a shilling bought entry to everyone: royal dukes, duchesses, penurious servants, apprentices on an illicit night out, merchants, soldiers, pickpockets and prostitutes, all promenading the same gravel walks under the same lamps. That was genuinely novel. Nowhere else in Georgian London did the classes rub shoulders so freely, and the erotic charge of the gardens came partly from exactly that — from the possibility of an encounter, romantic or transactional or somewhere between, with someone you would never meet in your ordinary walled-off life. The London Museum's Beatrice Behlen offers a useful yardstick: in the 1760s a housemaid earned about two shillings a week, so a shilling at the gate was half her wages and no small outlay — but it was payable, and pay it she might.

The food was famously terrible and expensive, which only added to the raffish glamour. The ham was the standing joke: sliced, it was said, so thin you could read a newspaper through it, and a single ham was reckoned enough to cover the whole site. In 1817 a small dish of that ham and two tiny chickens cost eleven shillings, with tarts and custards another four. The wine, at least, was good, and the arrack punch — arrack, rum and sugar — was potent enough to explain a good deal of what happened later in the evening.

And then there were the Dark Walks. Tyers, who otherwise lit his gardens like a fairground, deliberately left certain tree-lined avenues at the far edge of the site unlit — the Close Walks, the Lovers' Walk, the Druids' Walk, known collectively as the Dark Walks. This was not an oversight. Darkness was an amenity. Here is where the documentary difficulty begins, and where honesty matters, because the Dark Walks are one of those places whose reputation vastly outruns what can be firmly proved. That prostitutes worked the gardens is not in doubt; the fullest modern account, David Coke and Alan Borg's Vauxhall Gardens: A History (Yale, 2011), treats the erotic possibilities of the place as central to its appeal, and Tyers's very decision to charge a shilling was, in part, an attempt to keep pickpockets and prostitutes out — with, as one historian dryly notes, varying degrees of success. What is harder to document is precisely who did precisely what among the trees, because the vivid scenes we have tend to be either fiction, like Burney's, or the outraged testimony of visitors.

The best of that testimony is genuinely first-hand. The German traveller Karl Philipp Moritz, writing of his supper at Vauxhall in the 1780s, was startled less by any furtive activity in the shadows than by the sheer front of the women working the open tables: he described "the boldness of the women of the town, who often rushed in upon us by half dozens" and importuned the diners for wine. This is the documented voice of the gardens' sexual commerce — not a whisper in the dark but a brisk, public solicitation over supper. The management's own struggle tells the same story. By the later 1750s there were complaints of "dangerous terrors", shouts and screams, coming from the Dark Walks; and around 1764 the proprietors introduced a modest amount of lighting into the main Lovers' Walk. The lamps were resisted and, by some accounts, torn down — because a great many visitors had paid their shilling precisely for the dark, and did not thank Tyers for shining a light on it.

The famous rakes belong in this story, but they must be placed carefully. James Boswell, whose London Journal of 1762–63 is the most candid sexual diary of the age, certainly frequented Vauxhall — he records "parading" there among the aristocracy — and his journal is thick with casual encounters with prostitutes across the city, in the Strand, in St James's Park, on Westminster Bridge. Giacomo Casanova was in London in 1763–64 and knew the pleasure gardens well. But the specific, cinematic image of either man picking up a woman in Vauxhall's Dark Walks is not something the record I consulted actually pins down; their documented adventures happen elsewhere in London, and the Dark Walks' fame rests more on Burney's fiction, Moritz's supper and the management's lamp wars than on a signed confession from a famous libertine. It is a distinction worth keeping, because the whole argument of this chapter is that the pursuit of pleasure in Georgian England was half theatre — and the Dark Walks were, above all, a stage. Their reputation for transgression was itself an attraction, marketed by rumour, sustained by novels, and quite possibly more titillating than the fumbling reality among the damp trees, which — a detail the guidebooks omit — also served the crowd as an open-air latrine.

Vauxhall drew towards its close through the early nineteenth century, undercut by newer, brasher entertainments, and shut for good in 1859. The site was built over. But a corner survives: a public park in Lambeth called Spring Gardens keeps the old name and something of the old ground, and if you stand in it on a summer evening you are standing where duchess and apprentice and business-girl once queued at the same gate for the same shilling. Vauxhall's descendants are the subject of later chapters — the Covent Garden bagnios and the printed catalogue of women that was Harris's List — where the transaction the Dark Walks only hinted at was conducted with a frankness, and a price list, that Jonathan Tyers would never have permitted at his gate.



## 14. Do What Thou Wilt

*Hellfire Caves, West Wycombe & Medmenham Abbey · Buckinghamshire · c.1748–1766*


> **The Hellfire Caves, West Wycombe** — West Wycombe, Buckinghamshire, England
> 51.64740° N, 0.80533° W · confidence 4/5
>
> **Medmenham Abbey (the Monks of Medmenham)** — Medmenham, Buckinghamshire, England
> 51.55460° N, 0.85667° W · confidence 3/5


The source of almost everything you think you know about the Hellfire Club is a novel narrated by a coin, written by a man with a grudge and repeated ever since by people who would rather it were true. That is the correction this piece exists to make, and making it is more fun than the legend, because the legend — Satanism, orgies, black masses in a chalk cave — turns out to be a masterclass in how eighteenth-century England manufactured a scandal.

The facts first. Sir Francis Dashwood, born in 1708 and later 11th Baron le Despencer, was a wealthy, well-travelled, genuinely irreverent man who around 1750 founded a private club he variously called the Order of the Friars of St Francis of Wycombe and the Monks of Medmenham. The name "Hellfire Club" was pinned on it later by outsiders. From 1751 the friars met at Medmenham Abbey, a ruined Cistercian house on the Thames in Buckinghamshire that Dashwood leased and rebuilt in fashionable Gothic, with the Rabelaisian motto Fais ce que tu voudras — "Do what thou wilt" — set in stained glass over the door. The membership was a roll-call of Georgian power and wit: John Wilkes, the radical journalist; John Montagu, 4th Earl of Sandwich; Thomas Potter, son of an Archbishop of Canterbury; the poet Paul Whitehead as steward. Benjamin Franklin, a friend of Dashwood's from their days as the two nations' postmasters, is recorded staying at West Wycombe, though almost certainly as a guest rather than a member.

What did they actually do? On the honest, documented evidence: they dressed as monks, addressed each other as "brother", drank a great deal of very good claret, dined enormously, made mock-monastic toasts to Bacchus and Venus, and enjoyed the company of women — sometimes wives, sometimes courtesans, some of whom the members playfully styled "nuns". They satirised the Catholic Church, which Dashwood had come to despise on his travels, and they satirised monastic piety by inverting it. The History of Parliament's own historians, weighing what can be proved, land on something like posh, clever men having elaborately themed boozy weekends by the river, with a little light paganism for atmosphere. Sex was undoubtedly part of it. Human sacrifice, devil-worship and orgiastic black masses were not.

So where did all that come from? From two directions, both traceable. The first is Charles Johnstone's Chrysal, or the Adventures of a Guinea, a sprawling satirical novel published between 1760 and 1765 in which a magic coin passes from hand to hand narrating the vices of its owners. Chrysal contained thinly veiled, luridly embroidered episodes set unmistakably at Medmenham — including the story that Lord Sandwich, during some mock ritual, mistook a baboon for the Devil and was terrified out of his wits. Readers took the fiction for reportage, and the Medmenham legend was born. The second direction is pure politics. When Dashwood became a spectacularly incompetent Chancellor of the Exchequer in 1762 and pushed through a hated tax on cider, his former friend John Wilkes — denied office and out for revenge — used his anti-government paper the North Briton to leak, embroider and weaponise stories about the club and its now-respectable members. The baboon prank is itself usually attributed to Wilkes, who is said to have smuggled a dressed-up baboon into a ceremony to leap out at Sandwich. Whether Wilkes really did it or merely told everyone he had is, fittingly, impossible to establish. The reputation was, in the most literal sense, propaganda.

The caves are the other half of the myth, and they too dissolve under examination. Beneath West Wycombe Hill, Dashwood had a network of chalk and flint tunnels excavated between 1748 and 1752 — a quarter of a mile of chambers running deep under the hill, entered through a mock-Gothic church front. The legend says they were dug as a subterranean temple for unspeakable rites. The documented reason is almost aggressively wholesome: three successive harvest failures in 1748, 1749 and 1750 had thrown the local labourers out of work, and Dashwood, who owned most of West Wycombe, paid the destitute a shilling a day to dig, using the excavated chalk to build a badly needed new straight road from West Wycombe to High Wycombe. It was a Georgian public-works relief scheme. The club may have held a meeting or two underground; the elaborate association of caves and orgies came later, much of it invented or embroidered by the guides who ran the caves as a tourist attraction from 1951, because ghost stories and Black Masses earn better tips than road-building.

And here is the part the legend cannot accommodate at all: the same Sir Francis Dashwood who supposedly presided over Satanic rites spent his last years, in 1773, producing on his own press an abridged Book of Common Prayer, in collaboration with Benjamin Franklin — a sincere, rather Enlightenment-minded effort to shorten Anglican services so that busy or elderly worshippers might actually get through them. Franklin trimmed the catechism; Dashwood did the heavy cutting, excising repetitive Psalms and, with a certain dark comedy given his reputation, the line "Cursed is he that lieth with his neighbour's wife". He also helped fund London's first Unitarian chapel. The founder of the Hellfire Club, in other words, ended up as a liturgical reformer. It is the least demonic biography imaginable, and it is the true one.

That is really the whole lesson of the place, and of this chapter's argument. The organised pursuit of pleasure in Georgian England was theatre, and the Hellfire Club was its most accomplished production — a troupe of powerful men playing at wickedness in monks' habits, whose performance of transgression was so much more elaborate than the transgression itself that two and a half centuries of visitors have preferred the costume to the man inside it. Fifty years later a young poet would restage almost exactly the same play, in monks' habits, at another ruined abbey a hundred miles north; and both were, at heart, dressing up as the Restoration rakes of Charles II's court, who had at least had the decency to mean it.



## 15. Byron at Home

*Newstead Abbey · Ravenshead, Nottinghamshire · 1798–1818*


> **Newstead Abbey (Lord Byron)** — Ravenshead, Nottinghamshire, England
> 53.07900° N, 1.19200° W · confidence 5/5


> `[PLATE 11 — Newstead Abbey (Lord Byron) · 53.07900° N, 1.19200° W]`


The object was a human skull. A gardener turned it up while digging in the grounds of Newstead — the bones, Byron guessed, of "some jolly friar or monk of the Abbey" from the days before Henry VIII emptied the place — and the young lord, seized by what he called "a strange fancy", sent it to a jeweller in Nottingham to be cleaned, polished and mounted in silver as a drinking cup. It came back, he wrote, "a mottled colour like a tortoiseshell". At his parties it was filled with claret and passed around, and he liked it well enough to write a poem about it, the Lines Inscribed upon a Cup Formed from a Skull, which cheerfully advises the reader that it is "Better to hold the sparkling grape / Than nurse the earth-worm's slimy brood." A skull cup, an inheritance he could not afford, and a genius for turning his own scandals into literature: this is Byron at home.

George Gordon, 6th Baron Byron, inherited Newstead Abbey in 1798, aged ten, from his great-uncle the "Wicked Lord". It was a ruined Augustinian priory converted after the Dissolution into a family seat, and by the time it came to Byron it was falling down — the roof leaking, whole wings uninhabitable, the estate mortgaged to the hilt. He could never afford to restore it properly. Using borrowed money he made a few rooms habitable, hung velvet curtains, and left the rest semi-ruinous, which suited both his purse and his taste for Gothic gloom. He kept a menagerie worthy of the setting: a tame bear he had installed at Cambridge (there being, he noted, no college rule against bears), and at Newstead a wolf and assorted other animals. He used one hall as a pistol gallery and flooded a cloister cell to make a plunge bath.

The parties are documented, and they are exactly as theatrical as they sound. In 1809, before leaving on his travels, Byron gathered his Cambridge friends — John Cam Hobhouse, Charles Skinner Matthews, Scrope Davies — at Newstead, where, in the words of a 1939 county history drawing on their own accounts, "they found amusement in dressing up as monks". They wore habits and cowls, drank the cellar dry of claret and burgundy, and passed the skull cup round after dinner, playing at being the very monks whose home they had inherited and whose bones they were drinking from. It is the same performance the Hellfire Club had staged fifty years earlier and a hundred miles south: aristocrats in monastic fancy-dress, pastiching the piety their ancestors had swept away. Byron even knew it was a performance; the pose was half the pleasure.

His actual sexual life, as opposed to the costume drama, is unusually well documented, because Byron wrote about it himself in letters that survive in many volumes and are long out of copyright. He desired women and men, and said so — to his confidante Lady Melbourne and others — in terms his correspondents understood perfectly, even when the law of the day made the male half of it a hanging matter. Modern scholarship reads him plainly as bisexual. He was frank, too, about the women who worked at Newstead: the servants were not off-limits in his letters, and he wrote about them with a candour that later editors did their best to soften. It is a part of the story that has to be handled without either prurience or false delicacy. These were women in his employ, and the power ran all one way; Byron's own breeziness about it should not be mistaken for their consent to being written about.

Then came 1812, and Byron woke, as he put it, to find himself famous — the first two cantos of Childe Harold's Pilgrimage had made him the sensation of the London season. Into the sensation walked Lady Caroline Lamb, married, clever, reckless, who conducted an affair with him so public it appalled even a society that thrived on affairs. It is Caroline who gave him his epitaph, writing in her journal after their first meeting that he was "mad, bad, and dangerous to know" — a verdict she then spent years proving on herself rather than him. When he cooled and withdrew, as he always did, she pursued him, and is documented to have disguised herself as a page boy to get past his servants and into his rooms. The cross-dressing was not merely a stratagem; it was of a piece with the whole theatrical register of Byron's world, where transgression was performed as much as committed.

The rumour that has clung hardest concerns his half-sister, Augusta Leigh. It must be stated as precisely what it is: a persistent, contemporaneously believed, never-proven allegation that Byron and Augusta were lovers, and that Augusta's daughter Elizabeth Medora, born in 1814, was his. Byron's own letters flirt with it darkly — of the baby he wrote to Lady Melbourne a line that has haunted the question ever since, that "it is not an Ape, and if it is, that must be my fault". His wife came to believe it, his enemies broadcast it, and it did more than anything else to destroy him socially. But it was never established, and honest history leaves it where the evidence leaves it: unproven, and central precisely because it was believed.

The marriage that the rumour helped to wreck was brief and wretched. In January 1815 Byron married Annabella Milbanke — intelligent, mathematical, earnest, whom he had nicknamed the "princess of parallelograms" — and within the year it was over. Their daughter Ada, the future computer pioneer Ada Lovelace, was born in December 1815; weeks later Annabella left, taking the baby, and never returned. In the separation proceedings she gave voice to her suspicions of incest and of sodomy, and the scandal — debts, rumours, the whispers of Augusta — made England impossible for him. In April 1816 Byron left the country for good. He sold Newstead in 1818, to a school friend, Colonel Thomas Wildman, and never saw it again. He died in 1824, aged thirty-six, at Missolonghi, having gone to fight for Greek independence.

Newstead today belongs to Nottingham City Council, and you can walk through the rooms Byron made habitable, see his private apartments and the gilded bed he brought from Cambridge, and wander the gardens laid out among the old monastic ruins. There is one memorial there he built himself, and it is not to a lover or a friend. In the garden stands a monument to his Newfoundland dog, Boatswain, over an inscription mourning an animal who possessed, Byron wrote, "all the Virtues of Man without his Vices". Of all the epitaphs at Newstead — the skull's, Caroline's, his own — it is the only one he wrote without a trace of performance.



## 16. The Gay Delavals: Northumberland's most exhausting family

*Seaton Delaval Hall · Seaton Sluice, Northumberland, England · 1718–1822*


> **Seaton Delaval Hall (the Gay Delavals)** — Seaton Sluice, Northumberland, England
> 55.07417° N, 1.52889° W · confidence 5/5


Fifteen hundred pounds. That is what Sir Francis Blake Delaval spent, in a single evening, to hire the Theatre Royal, Drury Lane, so that he and his brothers and sisters could act out Shakespeare's *Othello* to a fashionable London audience — with themselves in every leading part. In the money of the day it was a small fortune; today the sum runs into six figures. The House of Commons adjourned early to let members attend. The footmen's gallery, Horace Walpole recorded, was "strung with blue ribands." The Prince of Wales came. Sir Francis played the Moor; his brother John played Iago; a sister, Mrs Quon, played Desdemona. Walpole, who watched the whole thing with the delight of a man collecting other people's absurdities, wrote that they acted so well "that it is astonishing they should not have had sense enough not to act at all."

That was the Delavals. They did nothing by halves, and they did almost nothing sensibly.

Their house still stands on the Northumberland coast a mile inland from Seaton Sluice, and it is one of the most theatrical buildings in England. Sir John Vanbrugh — playwright turned architect, the man behind Blenheim and Castle Howard — designed it between 1718 and 1728 for Admiral George Delaval, a naval officer who had made his money capturing enemy ships. Neither man lived to see it finished. The Admiral fell from his horse in 1723; Vanbrugh died in 1726. The estate passed to a nephew, and it was the nephew's children who turned the place into a byword for extravagance and earned the family its nickname: the Gay Delavals, gay in the eighteenth-century sense of merry, spendthrift and scandalous.

The stories that cling to the house are mostly about beds. Guests, it is said, were shown to bedrooms whose walls could be hauled up on pulleys in the small hours, exposing a sleeper to the room next door; or whose four-poster could be winched aloft and lowered, occupant and all, into a tank of cold water; or whose entire contents had been turned upside down and fixed to the ceiling. Geese were hidden in the bedclothes. These tales are wonderful and they are told everywhere, so it is worth being clear about the evidence. The National Trust, which owns the Hall, is candid: the family's reputation for pranks is genuine and contemporary, but the mechanical beds and sliding walls belong largely to Victorian retelling, much of it traceable to the *Monthly Chronicle of North Country Lore and Legend* in the 1880s. The Trust's own displays include an upside-down room, described as "inspired by one of the most well known anecdotes" — which is a heritage body's honest way of saying: lovely story, don't take it to the bank. What is solidly documented is the appetite for spectacle. They took acting lessons from the professionals Charles Macklin and Samuel Foote. They staged plays and pantomimes at home. They hired a London theatre. The practical jokes are the froth on top; the theatre is the real thing.

And under the froth is a good deal of ordinary human wreckage. Sir Francis (1727–1771) is described in the *Oxford Dictionary of National Biography*, flatly, as a rake. He kept a long-term mistress, an illegitimate cousin named Betty Roach, who bore him two children; the National Trust reckons he left at least five illegitimate children in all. To clear his debts the family arranged, in March 1750, his marriage to a wealthy widow, Lady Isabella, daughter of the Earl of Thanet — a woman decades his senior whose fortune turned out to be smaller than advertised, and whose money he promptly spent on Betty Roach anyway. The marriage was a transaction to keep the estate afloat, and everyone at the time understood it as one. Sir Francis died in 1771, aged forty-four, an alcoholic and, by most accounts, largely alone.

The building, too, came to grief. In 1822 fire tore through the central block; the heat was so fierce that glass ran like water. The core of the house has stood as a roofless shell ever since, statues of the Muses still ranged along bare interior walls open to the Northumberland sky. The National Trust acquired the Hall in 2009 and opened it the following year, choosing — rightly — not to pretend the fire never happened. The shell stays a shell.

Set the Delavals beside the other pleasure-seekers of this chapter and something clarifies. The Hellfire men dressed as monks and told each other they were blaspheming; Byron kept a bear at Cambridge and a skull for a wine cup at Newstead. All of it was performance, and the performance was almost always more elaborate than the sin underneath. The Delavals are that principle in its purest, silliest, most domestic form. Their transgression was, in the end, a cold bath and a goose in the bed. They spent themselves into ruin staging plays about themselves, and they built a house so theatrical it eventually caught fire and became the best set of all. Nobody was corrupted. Everybody was exhausted. It is the most English scandal imaginable: enormous effort, enormous expense, and the joke, when you get to the end of it, is a wet aristocrat climbing out of a tank in his nightshirt.



## 17. Bloomsbury: they stopped performing

*46 Gordon Square & Charleston Farmhouse · London WC1 and Firle, East Sussex · 1904–1978*


> **46 Gordon Square (Bloomsbury HQ)** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.52444° N, 0.13056° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Charleston Farmhouse (Bloomsbury Group)** — Firle, East Sussex, England
> 50.85400° N, 0.10600° E · confidence 5/5


> `[PLATE 12 — 46 Gordon Square (Bloomsbury HQ) · 51.52444° N, 0.13056° W]`


A spring evening, a drawing room in Bloomsbury, two young women sitting after dinner. The door opens and Lytton Strachey — long, cadaverous, unmistakable — stands on the threshold. He points at a stain on Vanessa Bell's dress and says a single word: "Semen?"

Virginia Woolf set the scene down decades later in a paper called "Old Bloomsbury," read to friends and never meant for the public. "With that one word," she wrote, "all barriers of reticence and reserve went down." It has become the founding anecdote of the whole group, and like most founding anecdotes it has been polished by retelling. It comes to us only through Woolf, only in memoir, only in old age; the exact year is uncertain. But the point she was making is the real one. She was describing the moment when a group of clever, well-bred Victorians decided that sex was a thing you could simply talk about at the dinner table, in mixed company, without dying of it.

The room was 46 Gordon Square. The four Stephen siblings — Vanessa, Virginia, their brothers Thoby and Adrian — had moved there in 1904 after the death of their father, Sir Leslie Stephen, escaping the heavy respectability of Kensington for the unfashionable squares near the British Museum. Thoby's Cambridge friends began coming on Thursday evenings: Strachey, Clive Bell, Leonard Woolf, the economist Maynard Keynes, the painter Duncan Grant. They talked about art and the nature of the good and, eventually, about who was sleeping with whom, which in this company was a large subject.

Because the striking thing about Bloomsbury is not that they had affairs. Everyone in this book has affairs. It is that they arranged their actual households around desire, in the open, and kept doing so for decades. The clearest case is a farmhouse under the South Downs.

In 1916 Vanessa Bell — married to Clive Bell, though the marriage had quietly become a friendship — took the lease of Charleston, near Firle in East Sussex, from the Gage estate. She was in love with Duncan Grant. Grant was a conscientious objector, and so was his lover, the writer David Garnett, always called Bunny; under the terms of Grant's exemption the two men needed farm work, and Charleston was near a farm. So the household that moved in consisted of Vanessa, her two young sons by Clive, the man Vanessa loved, and the man that man loved. Clive came at weekends. Everyone painted.

Set out plainly it sounds like arithmetic, and Bloomsbury has a famous piece of arithmetic attached to it: they "lived in squares and loved in triangles." The line is nearly always credited to the American wit Dorothy Parker, but the attribution is shaky — scholars tracing it have found no solid source in Parker and an earlier candidate in the novelist Margaret Irwin, writing in 1928. Best to enjoy it as an anonymous summary rather than anyone's coinage.

On Christmas Day 1918 Vanessa gave birth at Charleston to a daughter, Angelica, whose father was Duncan Grant. Maynard Keynes was staying in the house at the time; so was Bunny Garnett. Within weeks Garnett wrote to Lytton Strachey about the newborn a sentence that has never stopped being quoted with a shudder: he thought he might marry her when she grew up. He was twenty-six. She was days old. In 1942 he did exactly that. He was fifty; she was twenty-three; her parents were appalled and did not attend the wedding — and neither Vanessa nor Duncan told Angelica the other half of it, which was that her new husband had once been her father's lover.

That is the part of the story that stops the laughter. Angelica was raised believing Clive Bell was her father, and was told the truth only in 1937, when she was eighteen. She wrote about all of it in a memoir, *Deceived with Kindness* (1984), and the title is the whole argument. The adults around her were tolerant, artistic, unshockable, kind — and they organised her childhood around a set of secrets that were an open joke to everyone except the child at the centre of them. Bloomsbury's freedom was real. The bill for it was itemised, and a good deal of it was sent to Angelica.

The men were freer still, and better documented, because Keynes wrote everything down. He kept, alongside his ledgers of expenditure and his golf scores, a private tally of his sexual partners — some by initials (GLS for Strachey, DG for Grant), some by nickname, some, in the anonymous years around 1911, by pure category: "Lift boy of Vauxhall." He counted, quarter by quarter, as methodically as he counted anything. This was a man who would later shape the economies of nations, keeping a spreadsheet of his lovers before spreadsheets existed, at a time when what he was recording could have sent him to prison. He married the Russian ballerina Lydia Lopokova in 1925, to the disappointment of Grant and the astonishment of nearly everyone, and by all accounts loved her.

Charleston is now a museum, its walls and doors and furniture painted all over in the Omega Workshops manner, the red dog Duncan painted still guarding the head of Vanessa's bed. Angelica Garnett helped found the trust that saved it. Bloomsbury's other threads run on from here — Vanessa's friend Virginia would fall, later, for Vita Sackville-West, which is a story for the queer chapter and another set of great houses. But Charleston is where the argument of this chapter turns. Every rake and monk and clubman before them performed transgression and went home to a conventional bed. The Bloomsbury household stopped performing. They simply rebuilt the bed to the shape they wanted, and lived in it in the daylight, and let a child grow up inside the consequences.



## 18. The salon and the saloon bar

*Garsington Manor & the Fitzroy Tavern · Oxfordshire and Fitzrovia, London · 1915–1950s*


> **Garsington Manor (Lady Ottoline Morrell's set)** — Garsington, Oxfordshire, England
> 51.71611° N, 1.16278° W · confidence 3/5
>
> **The Fitzroy Tavern** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.51806° N, 0.13472° W · confidence 5/5


An ordinary corner house on Charlotte Street gave a whole London district its name, and what went on inside it between the wars is now almost impossible to picture from the pavement. To understand the Fitzroy Tavern at all you have to start forty miles away, in an Oxfordshire manor painted the colours of a peacock.

Garsington Manor was bought in 1915 by Lady Ottoline Morrell and her husband Philip, a Liberal MP. Ottoline was six feet tall, dressed like a stained-glass window, and had a genius for gathering interesting people and almost no talent for keeping their loyalty. She had the Tudor house done out in her own extraordinary taste — peacock-blue panelling, red lacquer, Spanish leather on the walls — laid out an Italianate pond and a loggia, and during the First World War threw the place open as a refuge for conscientious objectors, many of them from Bloomsbury. They ate her food, worked notionally on her land, and repaid her by turning her into a figure of fun the moment they were out of earshot.

Her private life was as unhidden as anything in this chapter. From 1911 to 1916 she conducted a passionate, non-exclusive affair with the philosopher Bertrand Russell; it began one night in 1911 when Philip was away. The two were prodigious correspondents even by the standards of the age — the Harry Ransom Center in Texas holds over 1,900 letters from Russell to Ottoline, and the Bertrand Russell Archives at McMaster University in Canada hold roughly 1,500 the other way. Later, in 1920, a young village stonemason named Lionel Gomme came to Garsington to cut plinths for her garden statues; she called him "Tiger," and by 1921, in her late forties, she had — her biographers agree — found real physical happiness for the first time, with a working man half her class and age. Some critics have suggested Gomme fed into the gamekeeper Mellors in D. H. Lawrence's *Lady Chatterley's Lover*, though that is literary speculation rather than fact.

Philip was no better behaved, and considerably clumsier about it. In 1917 the Morrells' marriage hit a genuine crisis when it emerged that he had got two women pregnant at once — his wife's maid and his own secretary. Ottoline, who tolerated a great deal, was for once badly shaken.

The cruelty came from the guests. Lawrence, who had sheltered under her roof, put her into *Women in Love* (1920) as the awful Hermione Roddice; Aldous Huxley, another beneficiary, satirised her in *Crome Yellow* (1921). Both portraits were recognisable and both wounded her deeply, which tells you something about the etiquette of the clever set: you accepted the woman's hospitality and then sold her to your publisher. Garsington is a private house now, and the opera company that borrowed its name has long since moved elsewhere.

The Fitzroy was the other end of the same world — bohemia with the country-house filter removed. The building had been the Fitzroy Coffee House in 1883 and a pub called the Hundred Marks by 1887; a former Savile Row tailor named Judah Kleinfeld took over the licence and, in March 1919, renamed it the Fitzroy Tavern. Everyone called him Pop. He devised the "pennies from heaven" trick — customers threw coins wrapped on darts up into the ceiling for a children's charity — and he presided over the most mixed room in London. Augustus John called it the Clapham Junction of the world, meaning everyone passed through sooner or later. Dylan Thomas scribbled poems on beer mats and gave them to women at the bar; Aleister Crowley mixed cocktails; the painter Nina Hamnett held court; Betty May, the "Tiger Woman," lapped drink from a saucer on all fours.

It was also, crucially, one of the places where gay and bisexual Londoners could drink in relative peace decades before the law allowed it. Sex between men remained a criminal offence until 1967, and the period's raids were real and ruinous — a queer gathering in nearby Fitzroy Square was raided in 1927, the Caravan Club off Covent Garden in 1934, its members photographed and prosecuted. The Fitzroy escaped that fate for years, largely, one regular reckoned, because it was too well known and too public to raid quietly. Tolerance here was not a right; it was a fragile local arrangement that could have collapsed at any time, and everyone drinking there knew it.

Nina Hamnett is the figure who binds the two halves together, because she moved through both and paid for it. A gifted painter from Tenby, the self-styled Queen of Bohemia, she published a memoir, *Laughing Torso*, in 1932. In it she wrote that Crowley had practised black magic at his "abbey" in Sicily. Crowley — who had discovered that suing people was a living — took her and her publisher to court for libel in 1934. He lost, spectacularly. The judge, Mr Justice Swift, told the jury he had never in over forty years on the bench heard "such dreadful, horrible, blasphemous and abominable stuff" as Crowley's evidence. Hamnett won and was ruined anyway; drink took the rest of her, and she died in 1956 after a fall from her window.

The salon and the saloon bar look like opposites — the peacock panelling and the spit-and-sawdust. They were the same impulse, at different altitudes: rooms where the ordinary rules were suspended, at a price, and where the people who made them possible were usually the ones who suffered for it.



## 19. Coteries at the edge: Mount Stewart and Portmeirion

*Mount Stewart & Portmeirion · County Down and Gwynedd · 1915–1941*


> **Mount Stewart (Lady Londonderry & 'The Ark')** — Newtownards, County Down, Northern Ireland
> 54.55500° N, 5.60800° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Portmeirion (Noël Coward writes Blithe Spirit)** — Penrhyndeudraeth, Gwynedd, Wales
> 52.91361° N, 4.09778° W · confidence 5/5


> `[PLATE 13 — Mount Stewart (Lady Londonderry & 'The Ark') · 54.55500° N, 5.60800° W]`


The oddest thing in the gardens of Mount Stewart is a terrace populated by concrete animals — dodos, a griffin, a boar, apes holding pots, a whole ark of creatures moulded by a local craftsman, Thomas Beattie, around the late 1920s. They are not decoration. They are a guest list. Each beast stands for a member of the Ark, the private club that Edith, Marchioness of Londonderry, ran through and after the First World War, and whose members took animal nicknames, usually a pun on their own. Winston Churchill was Winston the Warlock. Edith's husband Charles, whose infidelities were an open secret, was Charley the Cheetah — tall, sleek and prowling. Ramsay MacDonald, the first Labour Prime Minister, was Hamish the Hart. And Edith herself, the woman who summoned them all and held them in place, was Circe the Sorceress, the enchantress from Homer who turned men into swine.

Edith was one of the great political hostesses of the age, and her club — founded in 1915 and meeting at Londonderry House on Park Lane — was where the powerful were charmed into her orbit. The most discussed of her enchantments was MacDonald. A Tory marchioness and a Labour prime minister formed, across the 1920s and especially after his break with his own party in 1931, an intense friendship that scandalised both their camps and generated a large and affectionate correspondence. Here it is worth being honest about the evidence, because the temptation to tidy it into a love affair is strong. What the record supports is deep emotional intimacy and real political influence. The sexual question is unresolved; it is generally held to have been platonic, and gossip is not proof. Edith mattered to MacDonald enormously, and he to her, and beyond that the sources go quiet.

She was also, it turns out, tattooed. Beneath the marchioness there was a serpent: a snake coiling up her left leg from the ankle, hidden for decades and, according to the tattoo historian Matthew Lodder, exposed to general view around 1938 when hemlines rose, causing something of a sensation and prompting some to conclude she was "a fast woman." The National Trust, which now cares for Mount Stewart, records the tattoo too. It was not a family eccentricity but a fashion — the late-Victorian and Edwardian aristocracy went in for tattoos, Lady Randolph Churchill among them with a serpent round her wrist. Where and by whom Edith's snake was done is not documented; the suggestion that she was tattooed in Japan around 1900 rests on a single scholarly source and should be taken lightly. The gardens she made from 1921, of which the Dodo Terrace is one corner, she gave to the nation in 1957.

Four hundred miles south-west, on a wooded Welsh estuary, another powerful and unconventional person was building a private world with its own rules. Portmeirion is the fantasy Italianate village that the architect Clough Williams-Ellis began in 1925 on the coast of Gwynedd, and it is where, in the spring of 1941, Noël Coward wrote the play that would become his greatest hit.

He had good reason to be out of London. The Blitz had destroyed his office and his flat. He arrived at Portmeirion in early May and stayed, by the best account — his own — in the Fountain suite, though the village's folklore also attaches the story to the Watch House, and it is one of those pleasant local disputes that will never be settled. What is certain is the speed. Coward sat down on the morning of the second day and wrote for six days flat, morning and afternoon, propping up a wobbling table leg with a wedge, and by the evening of 9 May 1941 he had finished *Blithe Spirit* — the ghost comedy about a novelist haunted by his dead first wife. He took the title from Shelley. He made almost no corrections; only two lines of the original script were ever cut. It opened at the Piccadilly Theatre that July and ran for 1,997 performances, a record for a non-musical play in the West End that stood until Agatha Christie's *The Mousetrap* overtook it in 1957.

The thing the play could not say out loud is the thing that shaped Coward's whole career. He was homosexual, at a time when that was a criminal matter throughout his working life, and he never publicly acknowledged it. His discretion was total and it was armour: the wit, the dressing gown, the clipped delivery, the drawing-room comedies about heterosexual couples behaving badly, all of it built a public Noël Coward so polished that no interviewer could find the private one. There is a real joke buried in *Blithe Spirit*, and it is not on any of the characters. It is on a legal system so blunt that it drove one of the most celebrated Englishmen of the century to hide in plain sight, and never noticed him doing it. Portmeirion went on to a second fame in the 1960s as the set of *The Prisoner*, and now draws well over two hundred thousand visitors a year, most of whom have no idea a masterpiece was hammered out here in under a week.

Two places, then, at the geographical edges of these islands, made by two people who could not or would not live by the centre's rules — and who each built, out of concrete animals or Italianate towers, a world in which they got to set the terms. Circe kept her sorcery to the end. Coward kept his secret. The buildings kept both.



## 20. De Quincey's opium marriage
