*Dove Cottage & Nab Cottage · Grasmere and Rydal, Cumbria · 1802–1859*


> **De Quincey's Opium Marriage at Dove Cottage** — Grasmere / Rydal, Cumbria (Westmorland), England
> 54.45500° N, 3.01670° W · confidence 4/5


Consider first the girl, because he did. In the winter of 1802 a runaway schoolboy of seventeen was starving on the streets of London, sleeping in doorways and empty houses, and the person who kept him alive was a teenage prostitute he calls only Ann. She was about sixteen. One night, when he fainted from hunger on Oxford Street, she spent her own small money on hot spiced wine to revive him — a poor girl feeding a poorer boy. He never forgot it. Years later, in his *Confessions of an English Opium-Eater*, Thomas De Quincey wrote of "this poor friendless girl" with a tenderness he gave almost nothing else, and refused to file her among the "outcasts": "let me not class thee, oh! noble-minded Ann." When his luck turned and he came back to find her, she had vanished, and though he searched Oxford Street for years he never saw her again. It is one of the most humane passages any Englishman wrote in that century, and it is about a sixteen-year-old sex worker who once bought a hungry boy a drink and disappeared.

De Quincey (1785–1859) is remembered for the drug and ought to be remembered for the honesty. Born in Manchester, he ran from his school, wandered Wales, and fetched up destitute in London before his family recovered him and sent him to Oxford. He worshipped the poet William Wordsworth from a distance, so violently that when he first travelled to the Lakes to meet his hero he lost his nerve on the ridge above the house and turned back. He got there in the end, in 1807, and in 1809 — the year after the Wordsworths outgrew their famous little house at Town End and moved on — he took over the tenancy of Dove Cottage in Grasmere and stayed for eleven years.

The opium had started in 1804. He had crippling pains in the face and head — neuralgia, a bad tooth — and a fellow student recommended laudanum, the ordinary tincture of opium in alcohol that any apothecary sold across the counter, no questions asked. It worked; and then it kept working, and then he could not stop. He measured it, as addicts do, and the figures climb through his own account and his biographers': from drops, to glasses, to a reported ten wineglasses of laudanum a day by around 1813, with a peak that later writers put as high as some twelve thousand drops daily during his worst years — a figure to treat with caution, but a horrifying one even discounted. His great book turns on the split he knew from the inside: "The Pleasures of Opium" and, then, "The Pains." He called it, in one of the phrases everyone remembers, "just, subtle, and mighty opium."

The marriage sits right in the middle of the habit. Near Rydal Water stood a small farm called the Nab, and there lived Margaret Simpson, the daughter of an independent farmer — a "statesman," in the local word. De Quincey courted her, walking out to see her late at night, and Dorothy Wordsworth, who missed nothing and forgave less, noted it with a spinster's tartness: "At the up-rousing of the Bats and the Owls he regularly went thither." The consequence, she added, was that "Peggy Simpson presented him with a son ten weeks ago." The son, William, was born in November 1816; De Quincey married Margaret at Grasmere church in February 1817. The Wordsworths disapproved, and the disapproval was about class — Margaret was a farmer's daughter, beneath the poet's exalted circle — and it was cold enough to help end the friendship. That, and the fact that De Quincey had taken to ripping out the cottage garden his hero had planted.

They should be allowed their own verdict on the marriage, not the Wordsworths'. By every honest account it was happy. Margaret bore him eight children and nursed him through the opium nights, and he wrote of her as his comfort in the worst of them — the wife who sat up with the addict while the visions came. She died in 1837, worn out, and he outlived her by more than twenty years.

The *Confessions* came out first in the *London Magazine* in 1821 and made him famous almost overnight. It is, by common consent, the first modern drug memoir — the book that taught Baudelaire and half the writers who came after that you could turn addiction itself into literature. It did not cure him and it did not make him solvent; he spent his later years dodging creditors, at one point taking refuge in the debtors' sanctuary at Holyrood, and he died in Edinburgh in December 1859.

What survives is the building. Dove Cottage at Grasmere, where Wordsworth wrote his best poetry and De Quincey ate his opium, is now cared for by Wordsworth Grasmere and open to visitors; the Nab, Margaret's family farm at Rydal, still stands as Nab Cottage. Readers of this book will have met the Lake District already — the Maid of Buttermere fending off her seducer a valley away, Wicked Jimmy Lowther terrorising the same landscape a generation earlier — and will meet Dove Cottage again in the chapter on the Lakes poets, because it is the same small whitewashed house. Two poets, one drug, one kind farmer's daughter and one nameless girl on Oxford Street: the erotic map does not always lead to a scandal. Sometimes it leads to a man being decent about the two women who saved him, in a century that told him not to mention either.



\newpage

# Chapter 4 · The Old Gods

*Chalk, stone, water, and the rites we kept — or invented in 1873*


> `[PLATE 14 — chapter frontispiece]`


The chalk giant above Cerne Abbas, in Dorset, is fifty-five metres tall, carries a club, and has an erection of some thirty-five feet. He is also, on the best current evidence, medieval — dated by optically stimulated luminescence to a range of AD 700 to 1100, with the deepest sample from his elbow pointing to a date around 905, which is neither the Iron Age fertility god of the guidebooks nor the seventeenth-century joke about Cromwell that replaced him. Most tellingly, no surviving document securely mentions him before 1694, when the churchwardens recorded three shillings for repairing him. A colossal naked man on a hillside is not easy to overlook. The silence of everyone who should have seen him is the loudest evidence we have.

This chapter is about that kind of silence, and about what got poured into it later.

Britain's ancient fertility rites are, to a degree that becomes comic once you start counting, a Victorian invention laid over genuinely old things. Castlerigg is a real Neolithic circle, but its grove and apparent alignment were altered during the Romantic era. The Long Man of Wilmington, that immemorial pagan colossus, first appears in a drawing of 1710 and was dated by excavation to the sixteenth or seventeenth century. The Padstow 'Obby 'Oss is first recorded in 1803, and the pagan reading was fixed on it by Frazerian folklorists around 1913 and then absorbed by the town itself, so that visiting scholars are now told their own theory back. The Green Man was named and defined in an article published in 1939.

The temptation is to treat this as debunking, and to feel superior about it. That would be a mistake and, worse, dull. The customs are genuinely old and genuinely marvellous. Abbots Bromley dances with reindeer antlers radiocarbon-dated to around 1065, in a country where reindeer had been extinct for centuries — a set of horns carried from Scandinavia and kept in one Staffordshire village for the better part of a millennium. Nobody invented that. What the antiquarians invented was the meaning.

And the human impulse underneath is neither invented nor recent. People genuinely did pass sick children through the holed stone at Mên-an-Tol. Women genuinely did take their hopes of conception to a chalk figure, a well, a monolith in a Yorkshire churchyard. That such practices are often documented no earlier than the nineteenth century tells us about the history of record-keeping, not about the history of longing.

So the argument of this chapter is not that the old gods are fake. It is that Britain has repeatedly required its landscape to be more anciently and more explicitly sexual than the evidence supports, and has been willing to move stones, plant trees and rewrite customs to make it so. The joke lands on the antiquarian with his Golden Bough and his theory, never on the villagers dancing at dawn. They knew what they were doing. It was the scholars who needed it to mean something.



## 21. The Rude Man of Cerne

*Cerne Abbas Giant · Cerne Abbas, Dorset, England · c. AD 700–1100 to present*


> **Cerne Abbas Giant** — Cerne Abbas, Dorset, England
> 50.81306° N, 2.47472° W · confidence 5/5


> `[PLATE 15 — Cerne Abbas Giant · 50.81306° N, 2.47472° W]`


There is a photograph the English Post Office would not, for many decades, allow through the mail. It was not smuggled contraband or a saucy French postcard. It was a picture postcard of a Dorset hillside. The offending item was the Cerne Abbas Giant, and the trouble was the thing you cannot help noticing about him: a chalk-cut naked man, 55 metres from heel to head, brandishing a knobbled club in his right hand and, below a set of neatly drawn ribs, an erection some 11 metres — around 36 feet — long, nearly the length of his own head. He has been called, with some justice, Britain's most famous phallus.

Start with the anatomy, because the anatomy turns out to be the whole argument in miniature. The Giant we photograph today is not quite the Giant our great-great-grandparents photographed. Somewhere in the figure's long history of being neglected, grassed over and re-cut, his navel and his penis were joined into a single feature, and the length of the latter increased. Leslie Grinsell's 1980 paper in the journal *Antiquity* drew attention to this, comparing the earliest illustration of 1764 with the modern outline, and concluded that the navel had "become added to the penis, increasing the length of the latter by some 5 or 6 feet". A navel that appears clearly on that eighteenth-century drawing, and still on a picture postcard from the very end of the nineteenth century, had vanished by the time the twentieth was under way, absorbed upwards during a re-cut around 1908. Whether that was clumsiness or a private joke by the workmen we shall never know. But it means the single rudest thing about the rudest figure in England is, in its present dimensions, younger than the motor car.

For most of the last three hundred years the argument about the Giant was a two-horse race. Either he was ancient — Iron Age, Romano-British, a god of the Durotriges, a chalk Hercules — or he was a joke, a seventeenth-century lampoon of Oliver Cromwell cut by a bored landowner, the club a dig at Puritan rule and the phallus a comment on Puritan hypocrisy. In 1996 the two theories were put on trial, literally, in a public and televised debate in Cerne Abbas village hall, published as *The Cerne Giant: An Antiquity on Trial*. The jury could not decide, because there was nothing to decide with. That is the crux of the whole thing, and it deserves to be said plainly: there is no record of the Cerne Giant before 1694. None. No medieval chronicler, no monk of the abbey that sat at the foot of his hill from 987, no Tudor surveyor, no antiquarian, no map-maker mentions a colossal naked man on the hillside above the village. The first documentary trace of him is an entry in the Cerne Abbas churchwardens' accounts recording three shillings spent on the re-cutting of the figure. A giant with a 36-foot erection is not an easy thing to miss, and the silence of everyone who ought to have seen him is not a gap in the evidence. It is the evidence.

Then, in 2020, the National Trust — which has owned the Giant since the Pitt-Rivers family gave him over in July 1920 — decided to settle the matter for the centenary. Working with the geoarchaeologist Mike Allen and Professor Phillip Toms of the University of Gloucestershire, Trust archaeologists led by Martin Papworth cut four small trenches into the elbows and feet and took samples for Optically Stimulated Luminescence dating, a technique that reveals when grains of sand last saw daylight. The samples then sat in a locked laboratory through the first Covid lockdown before anyone could read them. When the results came, in 2021, they surprised everyone. The two earliest samples ranged between AD 650 and 1110, with a tenth-century median date — placing the Giant's making startlingly close to the founding of Cerne Abbey in 987. Not Iron Age. Not Cromwell. Medieval. "Everyone was wrong," said Allen, cheerfully, "and that makes these results even more exciting." His environmental work turned up a supporting witness in the soil: microscopic snail shells of species that only arrived in Britain in the medieval period, probably as stowaways in imported hay.

If the date was a surprise, it also opened a door. In 2024, in the medieval studies journal *Speculum*, Thomas Morcom of the University of Oslo and Helen Gittos of the University of Oxford made the case that the Giant was cut in the ninth or early tenth century as an image of Hercules, and that he stood where he stands because his hillside was a muster station — an unmistakable landmark where West Saxon armies could gather at a time when Dorset was being raided by Viking fleets. The club and the outstretched arm fit Hercules and his lion-skin, they argue; a resistivity survey back in 1980 had already suggested the ghost of a cloak or skin over the left arm. And here the story folds neatly back on itself, because by the eleventh century the monks in the abbey below appear to have quietly re-branded their embarrassingly nude neighbour as an image of their own hermit-saint, Eadwold — nakedness, after all, being a recognised sign of holy poverty. From Hercules to hermit, and eventually to fertility god: the Giant has been re-imagined by every century that has looked at him. He is less an ancient monument than a mirror.

Which brings us, at last, to the sex. The Cerne Giant is famous today as a fertility figure — the place childless couples come to conceive. What is remarkable, given the fame of the belief, is how young it is. The fertility folklore is first recorded in the Victorian era, not before. The specific practices are these: a woman who wished to conceive would spend a night alone on the figure, most efficaciously within the outline of the phallus itself; and couples hoping for a child would have intercourse on him. Just above his head, on the crest of the hill, sits a small Iron Age earthwork called the Trendle, or the Frying Pan, and in this ring the village raised a maypole, around which — the former sexton of Cerne told a folklorist in 1901 — young people danced on May morning. Dancing round a maypole above a giant phallus does rather suggest itself as fertility magic, and the antiquarians of the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries, steeped in Frazer's *The Golden Bough*, duly wrote it up as the survival of an immemorial pagan rite. But the maypole memory reaches back no further than living recollection, and the earliest firm record of any of it is Victorian. The belief is real and moving; it is simply not old.

Does it work? A local report noted that women in North Dorset average close to double the national birthrate, which proves nothing except that Dorset is good at statistics as well as at chalk. The most quoted testimonial belongs to the sixth Marquess of Bath, who visited in the 1980s having struggled to conceive; he later told the *New Yorker* that he "explained the problem and sat on him", and that a daughter arrived some ten months afterwards. One sits with that image for a moment, and moves on.

The Giant is now a scheduled monument, protected by the highest grade of archaeological law, and the Trust asks people to stay off him — every footfall speeds his erosion, and the seventeen tonnes of fresh chalk hammered in by hand every decade or so cannot keep pace with enthusiasm. He has, over the years, been fitted with a giant condom by a family-planning charity, given the word "Theresa" spelled out along his member, worn a Borat mankini, and been recreated on a Sussex school playing field by the girls of Roedean. We came looking for a prehistoric god of fertility and found, instead, a medieval war-marker with a Victorian sex life and a modern comedy career. Yet the detail that stays with you is quieter: through every theory, re-cut and publicity stunt, he has kept the same expression he has worn since a Saxon turf-cutter first gave him a face.



## 22. Chalk and Desire: the Long Man and the White Horse

*Long Man of Wilmington, East Sussex; Uffington White Horse, Oxfordshire, England · c. 1380–550 BC and c. 16th–17th century AD*


> **The Long Man of Wilmington** — Wilmington, East Sussex, England
> 50.81000° N, 0.18800° E · confidence 5/5
>
> **Uffington White Horse & the Manger** — Uffington, Oxfordshire, England
> 51.58194° N, 1.56667° W · confidence 5/5


Two figures are cut into the chalk of southern England, and between them they contain the entire argument of this chapter. One is genuinely, provably ancient and has almost no erotic folklore attached to it at all. The other is early modern, a relative newcomer, and has attracted fertility legends, neo-pagan dawn rituals and a persistent rumour about a missing penis. Guess which one the public thinks is the older.

Begin on the steep north face of Windover Hill, above the village of Wilmington in East Sussex, where the Long Man stands 235 feet — around 72 metres — tall, a lean outline of a man holding a long staff in each raised hand. He is the tallest representation of the human form in Europe, and for a long time everyone assumed he was prehistoric: an Iron Age god, perhaps, or Neolithic, keeping company with the flint mines and long barrows nearby. The trouble is the paperwork. The earliest known depiction of the Long Man is a drawing made by the surveyor John Rowley in 1710 — a drawing not rediscovered until 1993 — and it shows something rather different from the crisp figure of today: a shadowy indentation in the grass, with facial features, and a head shaped like a helmet. There is no earlier record of him at all.

In 2003 the matter was settled, or as near settled as chalk ever gets. Professor Martin Bell of the University of Reading excavated the slope for Aubrey Manning's Open University series *Landscape Mysteries* and found that the hillside had been stable for a very long time until a sudden episode of instability — a wash of chalk rubble down the slope — in the sixteenth or seventeenth century, exactly what you would expect from the fresh cutting of a giant figure. A species of land snail in the relevant soil layer had not reached England until the sixteenth century. The Long Man, in other words, is early modern. Ronald Hutton, the Bristol historian who is the indispensable guide to all of this, observed that we could at least "celebrate the fact that we have our first, apparently unequivocally, Early Modern hill figure". He may be a Tudor or Stuart creation, possibly a Reformation religious image, possibly a piece of political satire in the manner now proposed for Cerne. Nobody knows. What he is not is ancient.

Two things follow, both instructive. The first is that the Long Man you see is barely a lifetime old in its physical fabric. Until the nineteenth century he was visible only in certain lights, or under a dusting of snow, which is why the locals sometimes called him the Green Man. In 1874 the Reverend W. de St Croix had his outline marked out in yellow bricks, and since 1969 the figure has been rendered in white-painted breeze blocks and lime mortar. You are looking at a concrete diagram of a Tudor shadow.

The second is the penis. There is a stubborn, much-loved belief that the Long Man was once ithyphallic — proudly endowed like his Dorset cousin — and that prudish Victorians unmanned him. It is a lovely story and there is no evidence for it whatsoever. The Sussex Archaeological Society, which has cared for the figure since the Duke of Devonshire gave it to them in 1925, states flatly that there is no archaeological or historical evidence that the Victorians robbed him of anything. The 1710 drawing shows no such feature. He probably never had one. That has not stopped modern hands: pranksters gave him a twenty-foot appendage at the 2010 summer solstice, and a television programme once added breasts and pigtails, to the fury of neo-Druids who regard the "ancient" figure as sacred.

And here is the delicious part. The Long Man has a thriving pagan ritual life, and it is younger than the Ford Cortina. The Long Man Morris Men dance at dawn at the foot of the figure every May morning — a genuinely lovely thing to witness, the sun coming up over the Weald, the bells and the hankies, the old man on the hill above. The side was formed in Eastbourne in 1978. The tradition they perpetuate is, at most, a little older than the average British marriage. This is no criticism of the dancers, who are keeping something warm and good alive. It is only that the "ancient rite" is a modern kindness, and the joke is entirely on the antiquarian who assumed otherwise. The Long Man stands, as it happens, within sight of the Downs above Charleston, where Bloomsbury painted and loved a few miles off; the same chalk landscape has served as a backdrop for both pagan revival and modern bohemia, and both were making it up as they went along, beautifully.

Now travel north-west to the Berkshire Downs — administratively Oxfordshire now — and everything inverts. On the flank of White Horse Hill above Uffington runs a horse, 110 metres of sleek, abstract, almost modern-looking line: a streaming back, disjointed legs, a beaked bird-like head. And this one is the real thing. In the 1990s Julie Rees-Jones and Mike Tite, at Oxford's Research Laboratory for Archaeology, used Optically Stimulated Luminescence on the soil in the lowest layers of the figure's trenches — among the first such dates ever obtained from a British archaeological site — and dated its creation to between about 1380 and 550 BC, the late Bronze Age or early Iron Age. Roughly three thousand years old. The oldest chalk hill figure in Britain by a distance, and one of only a handful anywhere that is genuinely prehistoric.

The Horse survived not by luck but by love. Left alone for a decade, it would vanish under the grass; it has lasted a hundred generations because people kept coming back to clean it. The scouring, held roughly every seven years, was the occasion for a great fair on the hill — cheese rolling down the dramatic dry valley called the Manger, wrestling, backsword play (in which you won by drawing an inch of blood from your opponent's scalp), a greased pole, and a very great deal of beer. Thomas Hughes, the Rugby-educated author of *Tom Brown's Schooldays*, attended the scouring of September 1857 and turned it into a book, *The Scouring of the White Horse* (1859), our best eyewitness account of the festive machinery that kept a Bronze Age drawing alive. Below the Horse sits the flat-topped mound of Dragon Hill, where — the story goes — St George killed the dragon, and where nothing grows on the bare patch because dragon's blood poisoned the ground.

Here is the point of putting the two together. The Uffington Horse is three thousand years old and has almost no erotic or fertility folklore worth the name — a sun-horse, a tribal emblem, a goddess perhaps, but not a bedroom aid. The Long Man is perhaps four hundred years old and has collected fertility rumours, a phantom penis and a dawn fertility dance. Age and sauce, it turns out, have nothing to do with each other. The customs gather where the customs gather, and the antiquarian who reads longevity into rudeness, or rudeness into longevity, has simply mistaken one for the other.



## 23. The Woman on the Wall at Kilpeck

*Kilpeck sheela-na-gig, Church of St Mary and St David · Kilpeck, Herefordshire, England · c. 1140*


> **Kilpeck Church sheela-na-gig** — Kilpeck, Herefordshire, England
> 51.97056° N, 2.80611° W · confidence 5/5


> `[PLATE 16 — Kilpeck Church sheela-na-gig · 51.97056° N, 2.80611° W]`


About nine miles south-west of Hereford, in a village of a couple of hundred souls, stands what many people who know about these things consider the most perfect small Norman church in England. The Church of St Mary and St David at Kilpeck was built around 1140 of the local red sandstone, and it has survived, astonishingly, more or less as its builders left it — nave, chancel, apse, and a riot of carving by the group of twelfth-century masons known to art historians as the Herefordshire School. Around the outside, under the eaves, runs a corbel table: a row of some eighty-five little carved brackets, each one worth stopping for. There are musicians and wrestlers, a dog and a rabbit, a pig, dragons eating their own tails, and assorted grotesques. And, on the south-west, there is a naked woman holding her vulva open with both hands.

She is a sheela-na-gig, and there is no delicate way to describe her, so let us not try. She squats, she stares out with a large round head, and she pulls apart an exaggerated, gaping vulva — plainly, deliberately, unmistakably. The Kilpeck figure is the best-known and most-photographed sheela in Britain, partly because she is so well preserved and partly because, unlike most, she is not a stone imported from some older building but an original, integral part of the 1140s church. Somebody who built one of England's loveliest churches decided, as part of the design, to put an explicit carving of female genitalia on the outside wall, and nobody has ever been entirely sure why.

What is a sheela-na-gig? The name is Irish, of uncertain meaning and first written down in the 1840s, applied to a class of carved figures — naked women displaying an outsized vulva — found across the British Isles and, crucially, across France and Spain too. Counting them is a fool's errand, because scholars disagree about what qualifies, but the researchers Joanne McMahon and Jack Roberts tallied 124 in Ireland and 45 in Britain, with more turning up as people look. Ireland has by far the greatest concentration, which is one reason the interpretations differ so violently.

And the interpretations are the whole battlefield. There are, roughly, four. The first, and by far the most popular, is that she is a survival of a pre-Christian fertility cult — a pagan mother-goddess, the Cailleach, smuggled onto the church wall by masons who still half-believed in her, or tolerated by a Church willing to let the old goddess sit quietly among the saints. This was the reading championed in the 1930s by Margaret Murray, the same Egyptologist who did so much to convince the twentieth century that the witch trials had persecuted a surviving pagan religion. The second reading is the opposite: she is a Christian warning against the sin of lust, *luxuria*, her ugliness and exposure designed to repel rather than entice, a sermon in stone for a congregation that could not read. The third is that she is apotropaic — a charm against evil, genitalia exposed to frighten off the devil, in the same family as the gargoyle and the grotesque. And the fourth, the one that has quietly won most of the academic argument, is that she is not a survival of anything: she is a Romanesque import, part of a new fashion in church decoration that travelled up from the pilgrimage churches of France and Spain in the eleventh and twelfth centuries and arrived in Britain and Ireland with the Normans.

This last case is worth stating because it is the least romantic and the best supported. Jørgen Andersen, in *The Witch on the Wall* (1977), first traced the figure to the Continent; Anthony Weir and James Jerman, in *Images of Lust* (1986), followed the motif along the pilgrimage routes and argued the warning-against-lust reading. The clinching detail belongs to Eamonn Kelly of the National Museum of Ireland, who pointed out that almost every sheela still in its original place in Ireland stands in an area of Anglo-Norman conquest, and hardly any in the areas that stayed under native Irish control. If the sheela were a survival of native Irish paganism, you would expect the reverse. As Ronald Hutton puts it, the pagan-survival reading — the popular one — is precisely the one the evidence least supports. The woman at Kilpeck sits on a Christian church, built by a Norman lord, carved in a French-influenced style, around 1140. She is medieval to her bones.

None of which makes her less extraordinary, and none of which has dented her modern life. She has been claimed by the women's movement as an image of female power and sacred sexuality, adopted by neo-pagans as a goddess of the crone-cycle, and set to music: the English musician PJ Harvey called a fierce 1992 song after her, which introduced the word "sheela-na-gig" to a great many people who had never seen a corbel table. The lyrics need not detain us; the point is only that a twelfth-century Herefordshire carving found a second career on the indie charts, which is not a sentence one gets to write often.

There is a darker footnote, and it concerns her neighbours. Kilpeck kept its carvings when so many churches lost theirs, but not quite all of them. The folklorist Roy Palmer, in *Herefordshire Folklore* (2002), recorded the recollection of an old man who claimed to have been present, in the 1920s, at the destruction of a number of "obscene" figures at the church, on the orders of an offended lady of the parish — "I never could suffer that un," he remembered, "so I got her a pole and pothered un off." Palmer's careful guess is that the destroyed corbels were male exhibitionist figures, possibly aroused ones, which the Victorian and Edwardian sensibility found intolerable in a way it apparently did not find the sheela — perhaps because the men were unambiguous, and nobody was quite sure what the woman was. It is a grimly funny thought: she may have survived precisely because the person with the pole did not understand what she was looking at. The artist George Lewis, who drew the church in 1842, described her not as a sheela at all but as "a fool" opening his heart to the devil. Sometimes the best protection is to be misread.

The church is Grade I listed, still in use, and open to anyone. Go and look. She has been holding herself open on that wall for nearly nine hundred years, outlasting the men who offended the lady with the pole, outlasting the antiquarians who called her a goddess and the ones who called her a warning, and she will very likely outlast this book.



## 24. Stones That Grant Children

*Mên-an-Tol, Cornwall; Rudston Monolith, East Yorkshire; the Giant's Ring, County Down; Castlerigg, Cumbria · Neolithic and Bronze Age to present*


> **Mên-an-Tol** — Madron, Cornwall, England
> 50.16220° N, 5.60420° W · confidence 4/5
>
> **Rudston Monolith** — Rudston, East Riding of Yorkshire, England
> 54.09388° N, 0.32257° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **The Giant's Ring (Neolithic ritual site)** — Belfast, County Down, Northern Ireland
> 54.54028° N, 5.95000° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Castlerigg — the Invented Fertility Circle** — Keswick, Cumbria (Cumberland), England
> 54.60285° N, 3.09838° W · confidence 5/5


The wish to bargain with a stone for a child is very old and very human, and you find it wherever there are large old stones and people who want babies. What you do not always find — indeed, what you almost never find intact — is the actual ancient rite. Again and again the specific practice turns out to be younger than advertised, or attached to a monument that has been shuffled about since the folklore first fastened on it. Four stones make the case.

Begin in the far west of Cornwall, on the moor between Madron and Morvah, at the Mên-an-Tol — the name means, straightforwardly, "the holed stone". It is a strange and lovely little monument: a round granite stone with a clean circular hole bored through its middle, flanked by two uprights, so that from the right angle the three stones read as the numerals "101". It is also known as the Crick Stone, and its folklore is medical before it is sexual. For generations, children suffering from rickets or scrofula were passed naked through the hole — three times, or nine, and against the sun — to be cured. That much is well documented. The fertility version — that a woman who passes through the hole seven times backwards at the full moon will conceive — is later and more thinly evidenced, the sort of claim that accretes onto a suggestive shape.

And the shape may not even be the original one. In 1993 the archaeologist Ann Preston-Jones published a reassessment of the site in *Cornish Archaeology*, following survey work by the Cornwall Archaeological Unit, and concluded that the stones have been rearranged — probably in the early nineteenth century — and that the monument may originally have been part of a stone circle, with the holed stone standing elsewhere within it. The westernmost stone was moved into line with the others some time after 1815. So the monument we crawl through and photograph is, in its present arrangement, a couple of centuries old at most. The hole is genuinely ancient; the tidy little "101" through which the folklore supposedly flowed is a Georgian rearrangement. The wish is old. The stage set is recent.

Travel to the other end of England, to the village of Rudston in the East Riding of Yorkshire, and find the second stone standing — improbably — in a Christian churchyard. The Rudston Monolith is the tallest standing stone in Britain: about 7.6 metres, some 25 feet, of gritstone, weighing perhaps forty tonnes, dragged or carried by ice from at least ten miles away and raised in the late Neolithic or early Bronze Age. The eighteenth-century antiquarian Sir William Strickland is said to have dug around its base and concluded that there was as much stone again below ground as above. The church of All Saints was planted right beside it — almost certainly a deliberate Christian claim on a site already sacred, the same instinct that built churches on so many old stones — and the very name of the village records the takeover: Rudston, from the Old English *rood-stane*, the cross-stone, a pagan monolith rebranded with a crucifix on top. The fertility and pagan-worship claims made for it are the usual antiquarian speculation; what is solid is the stone itself, its Neolithic date, and the eloquent fact of a church built to stand next to it and, in a sense, to tame it.

Cross the Irish Sea to County Down, a few miles south of Belfast, for the third: the Giant's Ring at Ballynahatty, a colossal Neolithic henge some 180 metres across, its bank still standing four metres high, with a small dolmen — a passage tomb — at its centre. It was built around 2700 BC, before the pyramids, and it is genuinely a place of the dead: excavation nearby by Barrie Hartwell of Queen's University Belfast in the early 1990s revealed timber enclosures that seem to have been part of the funerary ritual, where bodies may have been left to decay before burial. The antiquarian imagination, naturally, reached for druids and sun-worship and fertility. But the Giant's Ring offers a wonderful corrective to all that solemnity, because we know exactly what the enclosure was used for in the eighteenth century, and it was not worship. It was horse-racing. The great bank made a natural grandstand, and the horses ran six circuits to the heat, which the locals reckoned made two miles. A five-thousand-year-old temple of the ancestors, repurposed as a racecourse, with the mourners' amphitheatre turned over to punters. There is, tellingly, almost no genuine old folklore attached to the Ring at all — no fertility rite, no druidic legend that can be traced back — which is itself instructive. Sometimes the stones stay silent, and honest.

Which brings us to the fourth stone, and the best debunk of the lot: Castlerigg, in Cumbria, on its hilltop platform east of Keswick with the Lakeland fells ringed around it like a stone audience. This one is the real thing — a genuine Neolithic circle of some thirty-eight stones, built around 3000 BC, one of the earliest in Britain. And it is here that we can catch the romantic imagination red-handed. English Heritage, which cares for the site, records that thirteen of the stones were re-erected or shifted between about 1794 and 1848, that these adjustments conveniently created an alignment towards the midwinter sunset, and that a stand of larch trees — a fake "druidical grove" — was planted at the centre to improve the atmosphere. This was not vandalism; it was set-dressing, done in the great age of Lakeland tourism to make the circle feel more primevally mystical than it already did. And we have a witness. When Samuel Taylor Coleridge climbed up to the circle in 1799, in the company of Wordsworth, he noted in his characteristic way that "the Keswickians have been playing Tricks with the stones." The Druid associations, the fertility mysticism, the solstice alignment — Romantic and Victorian invention, laid over a genuinely ancient monument by people who found the truth insufficiently picturesque. Coleridge, who knew the Lake District intimately — he would settle at Greta Hall in Keswick, in the same landscape that gave us the Maid of Buttermere and the appalling Wicked Jimmy Lowther — saw the trick being played and wrote it down.

Bind the four together and the pattern is plain. At Mên-an-Tol the stones were shuffled; at Rudston the Church built alongside to claim the ground; at the Giant's Ring the temple became a racetrack; at Castlerigg the Georgians moved the stones and planted a grove and invented the druids. The human wish to pass a sick child through a hole, or to ask a great stone for a baby, is old and real and worth honouring. Almost every specific "ancient rite" attached to these particular stones is not. The impulse is genuine. The tradition is usually about as old as your grandmother, and sometimes a good deal younger.



## 25. Rites Still Danced

*Abbots Bromley Horn Dance, Staffordshire; Padstow 'Obby 'Oss, Cornwall; Beltane Fire Festival, Edinburgh · AD 1065 antlers to a 1988 revival*


> **Abbots Bromley Horn Dance** — Abbots Bromley, Staffordshire, England
> 52.81722° N, 1.87861° W · confidence 4/5
>
> **Padstow 'Obby 'Oss May Day Festival** — Padstow, Cornwall, England
> 50.54120° N, 4.93670° W · confidence 4/5
>
> **Beltane Fire Festival, Calton Hill** — Edinburgh, Midlothian, Scotland
> 55.95528° N, 3.18222° W · confidence 5/5


> `[PLATE 17 — Abbots Bromley Horn Dance · 52.81722° N, 1.87861° W]`


Some of the customs in this chapter are dead, recorded and gone. Three of them are emphatically alive, danced every year by people who will happily buy you a drink afterwards, and they make the best possible ending, because they show the whole tangle at its most enjoyable: genuinely old things wrapped in genuinely recent meanings, and nobody the poorer for it.

Start in Staffordshire, at Abbots Bromley, on the Monday after the first Sunday after the fourth of September — Wakes Monday — when the Horn Dance is performed. Twelve people process ten or twelve miles around the parish all day: six dancers carrying sets of reindeer antlers mounted on carved wooden heads, three painted white and three a dark blue-brown, plus a Fool, a hobby horse, a Maid Marian (traditionally a man in a woman's dress), a boy with a bow and arrow, a triangle player and a musician. They fetch the horns from St Nicholas's church first thing, dance them round the parish, and return them at dusk. And the extraordinary thing — the thing that makes this the strangest fact in the whole chapter — is the antlers. In 1976 a splinter from one of them was radiocarbon-dated, and it came back at around AD 1065, give or take eighty years. Reindeer had been extinct in Britain for centuries by then. Which means the antlers were brought here, most likely from Scandinavia, nearly a thousand years ago, and have been kept and danced in this one village ever since. That part is real, and astonishing.

The dance itself, though, is a subtler case. The earliest written record of it is Robert Plot's *The Natural History of Staffordshire* of 1686, and Plot — writing beautifully — calls it a "Hobby-horse dance", performed "within memory" at Christmas and New Year, in which "6 others, carrying on their shoulders as many Rain deers heads" danced the hays and country dances while a man worked a hobby-horse with a snapping bow. An earlier mention of a hobby horse at Abbots Bromley in 1532 makes no mention of horns at all. So the custom is genuinely old — comfortably medieval in its bones, with millennium-old antlers — but the antlers may have joined a pre-existing hobby-horse dance at some point, and the confident modern reading of the whole thing as a pagan fertility rite is exactly that: a modern reading. There is, as the folklorists honestly admit, no evidence for it. Even the "medieval" costumes are Victorian; the current designs replaced earlier ones, and the splendid outfits worn today were made around the turn of the millennium. What you are watching is authentically strange and genuinely continuous. It is just not a fertility rite, whatever the guidebooks tell you.

Now to Cornwall, and the loudest, wildest, most physical of the three: the Padstow 'Obby 'Oss on May Day. It begins at midnight on the thirtieth of April, when the town gathers outside the Golden Lion to sing the Night Song, and by morning Padstow is dressed in greenery and flowers around its maypole. Then the 'Osses come out — there are two, the Old (or Red) 'Oss and the Blue Ribbon (or "Peace") 'Oss, the latter introduced in the nineteenth century — each a man inside a black hooped frame six feet across, hung with a skirt of black tarpaulin and topped with a snapping, masked head. Each 'Oss is goaded through the streets by a capering Teaser with a padded club, followed by the Mayers in white with red or blue sashes, the accordions and the drums, and the whole town singing. And every so often the 'Oss lunges and catches a young woman under its black frame. The documented belief is that a woman caught under the 'Oss will be married or pregnant within the year; in the older, rougher version, there was tar or blacking under there, and she came out marked. It is the frankest surviving fertility gesture in the English calendar, and it is thrilling to watch.

Here is where Ronald Hutton earns his place as the presiding spirit of this chapter. The 'Obby 'Oss feels immemorial, and Padstonians will tell you it is thousands of years old. But the first written record of it is 1803 — an account by the antiquarian Richard Polwhele of a man dressed in a horse's skin, led through the streets, ducking his head in every dirty pool and throwing the water over the crowd. There is no proof it is older than the eighteenth century. The reading of it as an ancient pagan fertility rite was not handed down by the townspeople at all; it was fixed onto the custom by outside folklorists in the early twentieth century — first Francis Etherington in 1907, then, decisively, Thurstan Peter in 1913, who, marinated in Frazer's *The Golden Bough*, argued that the 'Oss "might have once been" a pre-Christian fertility ritual. That reading then percolated back into Padstow itself, so thoroughly that when Hutton visited in 1985 he found the locals confidently describing their own custom to him in Frazerian terms the Frazerians had supplied. It is a perfect little loop: the experts told the town its tradition was ancient and pagan, and the town believed them, and now tells the experts. None of which, and this matters, makes the 'Obby 'Oss any less real, any less moving, or any less Padstow's own. Hutton himself calls it one of the most dramatic folk customs of modern Britain and "a tremendous reaffirmation of communal pride and solidarity". The custom is authentic. The pagan pedigree is imported. Both things are true, and the town dances anyway.

Which leaves the last fire, and the honest one. Every year on the evening of the thirtieth of April, thousands of people climb Calton Hill in the middle of Edinburgh for the Beltane Fire Festival: a torchlit procession led by the May Queen and the Green Man, drummers, acrobats, fire-sculptures, semi-nude and body-painted performers in red and white, and a bonfire, all telling the story of the death of winter and the rebirth of summer. It looks, and feels, like something dragged up from the Iron Age Celtic dark. It is not. It was started in 1988 — within living memory, more recent than the compact disc — by a small group including Angus Farquhar of the industrial music collective Test Dept, with academic support from folklorists at the University of Edinburgh's School of Scottish Studies. The first one drew a few hundred; from that core of a dozen performers it had grown, by 1999, to several hundred performers and over ten thousand spectators, and today it draws crowds of many thousands (around 6,800 attended in 2025), run by the Beltane Fire Society, a registered charity of elected volunteers. They chose Calton Hill partly to reclaim a space that then had a reputation for drugs and anonymous sex, and turn it back over to the community.

And here is why Beltane is the right note to end on. The organisers are completely honest about what it is. They do not claim an unbroken line back to the druids. They say plainly that it is a modern celebration, a deliberate reinvention, drawing on old material to make something new and meaningful now. It is an invented tradition that is truthful about being invented — and it is, by any measure, a real one, freighted with genuine feeling, welcomed into thousands of lives. Which turns the chapter's whole argument on its head at the last minute, and gently. The Victorian antiquarian took a young custom and lied that it was ancient. The Edinburgh Beltane took a young custom and told the truth — that it is ours, and now, and made on purpose — and it works just as well. Perhaps better. The old gods, it turns out, do not mind being freshly invented, so long as the dancing is good and nobody pretends otherwise.



## 26. Water Will Take Anything You Bring It

*Ffynnon Elian · Ffynnon Fair · St Winefride's Well · Ynys Llanddwyn · North Wales · c.5th century–present*


> **Ffynnon Elian — the Cursing Well** — Llanelian-yn-Rhos, Conwy, Wales
> 53.27698° N, 3.71009° W · confidence 3/5
>
> **Ffynnon Fair — the Chapel of Secret Weddings** — Cefn Meiriadog (near St Asaph), Denbighshire, Wales
> 53.22777° N, 3.45585° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **St Winefride's Well** — Holywell, Flintshire, Wales
> 53.27650° N, 3.22530° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **St Dwynwen's Island — the Welsh Isle of Love** — Newborough (Niwbwrch), Anglesey (Ynys Môn), Wales
> 53.14090° N, 4.41390° W · confidence 4/5


In 1925, someone clearing the site of a demolished well near Llanelian-yn-Rhos, Conwy, turned up a small piece of slate about three inches long. Scratched around its border were crossed lines; in the centre, the initials R.F.; in the corners, more letters. Pinned to the middle of the slate was a little wax figure — head, body, legs, one arm, the left arm broken off. Nobody made this to be found. Somebody made it to kill a neighbour.

This was Ffynnon Elian, the most feared well in Wales, and for the better part of a century it did a brisk trade in hatred. Like almost every holy well in the country it had begun respectably, a healing spring supposedly called forth by a sixth-century saint, visited by the sick who left a groat or its worth in bread and tried, as part of the ritual, to empty the well three times. Then, in the last quarter of the eighteenth century, the reputation curdled. Thomas Pennant, whose *Tours in Wales* recorded the well around this time, noted drily that people came "to imprecate their neighbours, and to request the saint to afflict with sudden death, or with some great misfortune, any persons who may have offended them." Pennant added, with the air of a man who had learned to keep his opinions to himself, that a local had once threatened him with the vengeance of St Elian.

The mechanism was precise and it was not free. To curse someone, you gave the keeper the victim's name; it was written in a register and scratched onto slate, or on parchment folded into lead, and dropped into the water while imprecations were spoken. Sometimes a wax effigy was stuck with pins, sometimes a frog was skewered and floated, corks and pins bobbing on the surface. The curser was given well-water to drink and to pour over his head, three times. A shilling laid the curse. Lifting one cost considerably more — which was, of course, where the real money lay, because the cursed came too, sick with fright, and paid to be released. One keeper was reckoned to be earning close to £300 a year, a startling sum, from selling misfortune and then selling the cure for it.

The keepers are the human comedy here. The most notorious was a tailor, John Evans, who styled himself Jac Ffynnon Elian. When the local Methodists, appalled, had the well destroyed around 1829, Evans simply diverted the spring onto his own land and carried on. He was jailed twice for fraud and eventually confessed that the whole business was bogus — that he had invented the terror and sold it — before, in the neat manner of the reformed, joining the Baptists. By about 1850 the well was finished as a going concern. It survives today only as a filled-in site on private farmland with no public access; a slate plaque marks it, and a spring feeds a house. What you can visit is the church of St Elian nearby, whose late-medieval painted rood-screen panels — now fixed to the north wall of the nave — are among the best surviving church decoration in this part of Wales. The best modern account is Richard Suggett's *A History of Witchcraft and Magic in Wales*.

Twenty-odd miles south-west, near St Asaph in Denbighshire, is the opposite of a cursing well: a well people married at. Ffynnon Fair — St Mary's Well — sits beside the ruined, cruciform chapel on the banks of the River Elwy, its spring rising into a beautiful eight-pointed star-shaped basin, closely modelled on the one at Holywell. It is often called "the Welsh Gretna Green," and this needs stamping on. The label rests almost entirely on a single manuscript, *Y Cwtta Cyfarwydd*, the chronicle kept by a local notary, Peter Roberts, between 1607 and 1646. Roberts records seven marriages at the chapel between 1611 and 1640, two of them described as "clandestine" — the first, in February 1611, being that of his own brother-in-law. The romantic cluster people point to is really four sisters of the Lloyd family of nearby Wigfair marrying at the family's convenient local chapel over some years; only one of those, Elizabeth Lloyd's in 1626, is recorded as clandestine. As the well historians who have gone back to the source point out, clandestine marriages happened all over the place, in private houses as much as chapels, and seven weddings in thirty years does not make a wedding factory. The "Welsh Gretna Green" is Victorian romance laid over an ordinary parish record. The Victorians did rather more than romanticise it: they rebuilt the site as a landscaped picnic walk, and the little bath by the spring is a Victorian addition, an architectural flourish rather than anything a pilgrim used. The poet Felicia Hemans, who lived near St Asaph, wrote her "Our Lady's Well" about the place. Like Ffynnon Elian, it is on private farmland; permission is needed to visit.

The star-shaped basin at Ffynnon Fair copies the one at Holywell because Holywell is the mother of them all. St Winefride's Well in Flintshire — the "Lourdes of Wales" — has been a place of pilgrimage for something like 1,300 years, the only site in Britain with an unbroken tradition of public pilgrimage across the Reformation. Its founding story must be handled with care, because it is a story of attempted rape and a beheading, and it should be read as documented medieval hagiography rather than anything else. According to the two twelfth-century Latin Lives — one written by Robert, prior of Shrewsbury, some five hundred years after the events they describe — Winefride (Gwenffrewi) was a seventh-century noblewoman who refused the advances of a chieftain's son, Caradog. Enraged, he cut off her head; where it came to rest a spring burst from the ground. Her uncle, St Beuno, replaced the head and prayed, and she was restored to life, marked forever by a thin white scar. Caradog, in the telling, was swallowed by the earth and effectively vanishes from the story, while Winefride goes on to become an abbess, a saint, and the patron invoked against unwanted advances. It is worth noticing what the medieval church did with this: it turned a woman's refusal, and her violent punishment for it, into a cult of healing and holiness, and quietly disposed of the man. She becomes everything; he becomes a hole in the ground.

The physical practice is unbroken and specific. The shrine building, a superb Perpendicular Gothic structure over the spring, was built around 1500 and is usually credited to Margaret Beaufort, mother of Henry VII, which is very likely why the shrine escaped the worst of the Reformation. The spring rises into a star-shaped inner pool. The traditional method of bathing, still followed, is to pass three times through this inner pool while reciting a decade of the Rosary, then to move to the outer pool and kneel on a submerged stone — St Beuno's Stone — for as long as the prayer takes. The triple immersion comes straight out of Robert of Shrewsbury's Life, in which Beuno prophesies that whoever asks Winefride's aid will obtain his wish "at the first, or the second, or certainly the third petition." The well is Grade I listed and a Scheduled Monument; in November 2023 the Catholic Bishops' Conference of England and Wales elevated it to National Shrine status, and it attracts around 30,000 visitors a year. It is run by the Roman Catholic Diocese of Wrexham and welcomes anyone. Pilgrims still line up to kiss a fragment of the saint's finger bone.

The fourth well is the tenderest. Off the south-west corner of Anglesey lies Ynys Llanddwyn (Llanddwyn Island), a tidal islet named for St Dwynwen, the Welsh patron saint of lovers, who according to legend lived here in the fifth century. The story exists in several versions but runs, broadly, like this: Dwynwen, a daughter of the prince Brychan Brycheiniog, loved a young man called Maelon Dafodrill; the match was refused, or she rejected him, and in her distress she prayed to be released from her love. An angel brought a potion that turned Maelon to ice. God then granted her three wishes: that Maelon be thawed, that all true lovers should find happiness through her, and that she herself should never marry. She became a hermit and died here around AD 460. On the island are the ruins of a sixteenth-century chapel, built on the site of her original one, and Ffynnon Dwynwen, her well. The divination practised there is charming and precise: a woman would scatter breadcrumbs on the surface of the water and lay her handkerchief on top, and if the sacred eels living in the well disturbed the cloth, her lover would be faithful. So many pilgrims came, and left so much, that in Tudor times this was among the richest parishes in the region — pilgrim offerings supplied the great bulk of the church's income in 1535. Dwynwen's feast, Dydd Santes Dwynwen on 25 January, is the Welsh St Valentine's Day, and unlike a great deal of "ancient" custom it is genuinely being revived, with cards, gifts and a busy modern trade in Welsh romance.

Four wells, four uses. The Welsh took their marriages to the water, and their curses, and their divinations, and their broken hearts, and the church absorbed the lot — building chapels over springs, appointing saints to eels, charging a groat here and a shilling there. Water was one of the two great folk technologies of desire in Wales; the standing stones of an earlier chapter were the other. What is striking, looking at all four together, is how little the difference between blessing and cursing really was. The same triple immersion cured a child at Holywell and killed a neighbour at Llanelian. It depended entirely on what you brought to the water — and, as Jac Ffynnon Elian understood perfectly, on who was standing beside it holding out his hand.



## 27. Caru yn y Gwely, and a Job Application in Wood

*Bundling and the love spoon · rural Wales · 17th–19th century*


> **Caru yn y Gwely — Bundling & the Love Spoon** — St Fagans, Cardiff (museum); custom pan-North-Wales, Denbighshire/Gwynedd (custom); Cardiff (collection), Wales
> 51.48690° N, 3.27180° W · confidence 4/5


> `[PLATE 18 — Caru yn y Gwely — Bundling & the Love Spoon · 51.48690° N, 3.27180° W]`


Almost everything you have read about Welsh bundling is from Massachusetts. The board down the middle of the bed, the girl sewn into a bundling sack, her legs bound so that nothing could happen below the waist — these are the details that make the custom sound both hilarious and faintly sinister, and most of them come from New England, or from English travel-writers who had crossed into Wales specifically to be scandalised. If you are going to write about a chapter's worth of invented tradition, as this one is, it will not do to invent a composite Welsh custom out of American parts. So let us be careful, and separate what is actually evidenced for Wales from what is merely famous.

The Welsh custom had a Welsh name: *caru yn y gwely*, "loving in the bed," though the strictly correct form was *caru ar y gwely*, "loving *on* the bed," and it was also called *caru'r nos*, the night-courting. The practicalities that are genuinely documented for Wales are modest. A young man, usually from another farm, would come after dark and knock at the young woman's window with small stones — a practice called *cnoco*, knocking. If admitted, a couple might sit up in the kitchen, or they might go up to the woman's bedroom and spend the night together, clothed, sometimes with a blanket or a bolster between them. The Welsh definitions of the custom are insistent on one point: the couple lay *on* the bed, dressed, and as long as they kept talking, those downstairs assumed nothing improper was happening. The bundling stocking and the plank are much harder to pin to Wales specifically; the most vivid tie-them-up accounts belong to the American tradition catalogued in Henry Reed Stiles's 1871 book on bundling, and to scandalised English visitors like William Bingley, whose *North Wales* gave readers exactly the frisson they wanted.

Why did it exist at all? The dull, sensible answer is distance, cold and poverty. Farms were far apart, houses were small and had no private room, courting happened after a full day's work when there was nowhere warm and unwatched to do it, and — as one contemporary account of the northern English version cheerfully admitted — it saved time, candles and fuel. It was a courtship conducted under the parental roof, with the parents a wall away, and it was widely regarded, by the people who practised it, as neither immoral nor indecent. An official Poor Law inquiry of 1834 recorded that in parts of Wales this was simply "the way in which all courtships amongst the lower class have been carried on from time immemorial."

And then the chapel came for it. Welsh Nonconformity, gathering force through the nineteenth century, disapproved thoroughly, and the seiat — the chapel society — kept discipline, with members hauled up to confess fornication and be punished. The great set-piece of outrage was the 1847 *Reports of the Commissioners of Inquiry into the State of Education in Wales*, the notorious "Blue Books," compiled by three English commissioners — R. R. W. Lingen, Jellynger C. Symons and H. R. Vaughan Johnson — who spoke no Welsh and leaned heavily on Anglican clergy; their report was presented on 1 July 1847. They declared pre-marital unchastity "the scandal of the Principality" and claimed the majority of Welsh women were pregnant before marriage. This was a moral assertion, not a measured one, and it was furiously rebutted at the time — the Welsh minister Ieuan Gwynedd demonstrated that Welsh illegitimacy ran only fractionally above the English rate.

Here is where the temptation to reach for a startling statistic must be resisted, because the honest position is more interesting than a number. There is no reliable figure for how many Welsh brides were pregnant at the altar. The leading modern historian of Welsh illegitimacy, Angela Muir, has shown that the standard demographic technique for calculating prenuptial pregnancy simply cannot be applied to Welsh parish registers — the pool of Welsh surnames is so small, thanks to the patronymic naming system, that you cannot reliably match a baptism to a marriage. The famous "about a third of all brides were pregnant" figure is English (from P. E. H. Hair's studies of English registers) and American (rural New England after independence); it is not a Welsh measurement, and it should never be presented as one. What can be said about Wales is narrower and better sourced: illegitimacy *ratios* in mid-Wales rose across the eighteenth century, and by the nineteenth, Cardiganshire had among the highest illegitimacy rates in England and Wales. Bundling frequently ended, in other words, exactly as anyone would expect a night in bed to end — but the lurid national statistic that everyone quotes was borrowed from somewhere else. The custom got the blame; the numbers were imported. The standard scholarly authority remains Trefor M. Owen's *Welsh Folk Customs*, with Catrin Stevens's *Welsh Courting Customs* the fullest modern treatment.

If bundling is the courtship that has been over-decorated, the love spoon is the courtship object that has been decorated for real. The *llwy garu* is a wooden spoon, carved from a single piece of wood and given by a young man to the woman he hoped to court. The oldest dated Welsh example, from 1667, is at St Fagans National Museum of History near Cardiff, and the tradition is certainly older than that. What matters is what the spoons were *for*. A young man in a poor rural society had no money for jewellery; what he had was long winter evenings, a pocket-knife and something to prove. So he proved it. The symbols accumulated meanings — hearts for love, keys and keyholes for a home and security, links of chain for binding faithfulness, a wheel for the promise to work, an anchor for a sailor, and, most tellingly, wooden balls carved loose inside a pierced cage, one for each child the carver hoped they might have. To carve a chain, or a ball in a cage, from a single unbroken piece of wood takes patience, skill and an extremely steady hand — which was precisely the point. The spoon was not really a love letter. It was a job application in wood: proof that this man had the patience and the fine motor control to make a good and careful husband, submitted for inspection to the girl and, crucially, to her father.

One last thing, because it is nearly always got wrong. The word "spooning," for canoodling, is very widely said to come from the love spoon, and it is a lovely story, and it is not true. The *Oxford English Dictionary* traces "spoon" as a verb meaning to court, sentimentally and foolishly, to 1831, and it derives from an older sense of "spoon" as a simpleton — a shallow, silly person, like the shallow bowl of the utensil. The cuddling sense, lying together "spoon-fashion," is later still, from the 1880s. The Welsh love spoon and the English verb "to spoon" are, disappointingly, unrelated; the connection is folk etymology, which is itself a very good example of exactly the kind of invented tradition this chapter is about.

Keep the love spoon and the bundling bed together in your mind, though, because they are two ends of the same thing: a poor, cold, closely watched society working out how young people could get close enough to marry. And keep them in mind for one more reason, because the story runs straight on into the next chapter. A Warwickshire girl called Anne Hathaway was three months pregnant when she married an eighteen-year-old glover's son in the winter of 1582, and everything the Welsh understood about courtship — that sex came first, that the wedding regularised it, and that this was normal — was understood in Shottery too.



## 28. The Green Men Who Were Named in 1939

*Rosslyn Chapel (Collegiate Chapel of St Matthew) · Roslin, Midlothian · founded 1446*


> **Rosslyn Chapel (the Green Men)** — Roslin, Midlothian, Scotland
> 55.85528° N, 3.16028° W · confidence 5/5


On any given morning now, the small car park at Roslin fills with people who have come to a fifteenth-century Scottish chapel because of a twenty-first-century American thriller. In the year before Dan Brown published *The Da Vinci Code*, Rosslyn Chapel received 38,141 visitors. The book came out in 2003, ends here, and the following year the figure had risen to 79,916. The film followed in 2006, and that year 175,074 people came through the door; the chapel would later record 181,700 paying visitors in 2017. The money paid for a badly needed conservation programme, which is the chapel's own dry, forgiving verdict on the whole business: the nonsense saved the building. It is a useful place to end a chapter about invented tradition, because Rosslyn is where two entirely different inventions — one recent and commercial, one genteel and academic — sit side by side on the same carved stone.

The chapel itself needs no invention. It was founded in 1446 by William Sinclair (or St Clair), 3rd Earl of Orkney, as a collegiate church to pray for his family's souls; building seems to have got properly under way around 1456, and stopped when Sinclair died in 1484, leaving only the choir of what was planned as a far larger cruciform church. What was finished is one of the most densely carved interiors in Europe — every surface worked into foliage, figures, biblical scenes and geometric patterns of bewildering intricacy. Its showpiece is the Apprentice Pillar, four vines spiralling up out of dragons' mouths, around which clings a legend of the kind that attaches itself to any object too good to be explained: the master mason went abroad to study the design, the apprentice carved it perfectly in his absence, and the master, consumed by jealousy, killed the boy with a blow of his mallet. Two carved heads are pointed out as the pair, condemned to stare at each other across the chapel forever. It is a wonderful story and there is no evidence for a word of it.

But the carvings that concern us are the Green Men. Rosslyn has an extraordinary density of them — foliate heads, human faces with vegetation issuing from the mouth, and sometimes the eyes and ears. The chapel's own fact sheet states there are "over 100," and the Trust's guides cite more than 110 throughout the building; the visitor is invited to hunt them out and to notice that they are said to progress in age around the chapel, young in the east and old in the west, as if aging with the seasons. They are genuinely medieval, genuinely mysterious, and genuinely beautiful.

They are also not called "Green Men" by anyone before 1939. This is the point on which the whole chapter turns. Until the twentieth century these carvings were simply "foliate heads," and no medieval source anywhere explains what they were for or what they were called. Then, in March 1939, an amateur folklorist named Julia Somerset, Lady Raglan, published a short article in the journal *Folklore* titled "The 'Green Man' in Church Architecture." She had been struck by a foliate head in a church at Llangwm in Monmouthshire, and she proposed that these heads were the survival of a pagan vegetation deity — the same figure, she argued, as the Jack-in-the-Green, the May King and Robin Hood, a nature-god worshipped for fertility and hiding in plain sight on Christian walls. It was a bold, romantic idea, arriving at the high tide of Frazer's *Golden Bough* and Margaret Murray's theories of a surviving pagan witch-cult, and it was enormously influential. It is also almost certainly wrong.

The historian Ronald Hutton has been the most careful critic of the fertility-god reading, and the case against it is simple and devastating. There is no medieval evidence that the foliate heads were pagan, or fertility symbols, or worshipped, or even named. Worse, several of the folk figures Lady Raglan lassoed to her theory postdate the carvings by centuries: the Jack-in-the-Green, her key witness, is an eighteenth-century invention — there is no trace of him before 1795. In other words, a genteel folklorist looked at genuinely ancient carvings, connected them to genuinely modern customs, and produced a "pagan survival" that was younger than the carvings by four or five hundred years. To their considerable credit, Rosslyn Chapel's own custodians say so plainly in their published material, conceding that Lady Raglan "had not quite got her facts right" and that many of the folk figures she invoked were invented long after the leaf mask appeared. A heritage body debunking the myth on which half its gift-shop merchandise depends is a rarer and finer thing than any Templar treasure.

Which brings us back to the treasure, and the maize. Rosslyn is the beating heart of a whole industry of invented significance — Knights Templar, Freemasonry, the Holy Grail hidden in a vault, secret musical scores encoded in the carved cubes, and, most enjoyably, the claim that certain carvings depict New World maize and aloe, plants supposedly unknown in Europe when the chapel was built, and therefore proof that a Sinclair sailed to America before Columbus. Botanists are unconvinced that the carvings are maize at all; the "pre-Columbian voyage" rests on the "if such it is" of a stone that might just be stylised foliage. None of it is any older than the enthusiasm that needs it to be true.

What Rosslyn actually is, when you strip the layers away, is a working Episcopal church, still holding services, cared for by the Rosslyn Chapel Trust, containing some of the finest late-medieval carving in Britain — carving whose meaning we honestly do not know. The Green Men peer down from the vaulting, sprouting their leaves, aging from east to west, keeping their secret. And that is the joke of the whole chapter, made in stone: the carvings are five hundred years old, and their famous pagan meaning is younger than the electric light bulb.



\newpage

# Chapter 5 · Love in the Library

*The houses, moors and harbours behind the books*


> `[PLATE 19 — chapter frontispiece]`


Three sisters lived in a parsonage on the edge of a Yorkshire moor with their father, their brother and a graveyard at the door. Between them they had almost no romantic experience whatsoever. Within roughly two years they produced Jane Eyre, Wuthering Heights and The Tenant of Wildfell Hall, and reviewers refused to believe women had written them.

That is the argument of this chapter, stated in one household. English literature's most powerful writing about desire was produced, again and again, by people with restricted, thwarted, censored or entirely absent sexual lives — and the constraint was not an unfortunate background condition. It was the engine.

The mechanism is not mysterious. Where the thing itself is unavailable, the imagination does the work, and the prohibition supplies the pressure. Austen's restraint is not a Victorian evasion of sex — she predates the Victorians — but a technique, and the charge lives precisely in the negative space where the kiss does not happen.

There is a second, harder theme running through these houses, and it concerns who controlled the record afterwards. Cassandra Austen burned the bulk of her sister's letters. Wordsworth's family concealed his French daughter for over a century, until an American scholar reconstructed Annette Vallon from Dorothy's correspondence and published in 1916. Coleridge cut Sara Hutchinson out of his own greatest poem, replacing her name with "Lady" and publishing the remnant on Wordsworth's wedding day. A passage of Dorothy's Grasmere journal was scored out by an unknown hand. Hardy is said to have read his dead wife's account of their marriage and destroyed it, then wrote her the tenderest elegies in the language.

So the literary chapter is also a chapter about editing — about sisters, widowers, executors and descendants deciding what posterity would be permitted to know. What we inherit is a cleaned version of some extremely complicated households, and the cleaning was usually done by people who loved the dead and thought they were being kind.

The houses themselves survive, and they are smaller than the work suggests. Austen wrote at a twelve-sided walnut table barely larger than a dinner plate, in a room with no privacy, with a creaking door left un-oiled as an early warning system. Keats had half a villa and a plum tree. Dylan Thomas had a shed on a cliff. It is worth standing in these rooms and registering the disproportion: the enormous interior weather, and the very small space it was made in.



## 29. The Parsonage Full of Desire

*Haworth Parsonage · Top Withens · Haworth, West Yorkshire · 1820–1855*


> **Haworth Parsonage (the Brontës)** — Haworth, West Yorkshire, England
> 53.83139° N, 1.95722° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Top Withens (Wuthering Heights inspiration)** — Haworth, West Yorkshire, England
> 53.81481° N, 2.02981° W · confidence 5/5


> `[PLATE 20 — Haworth Parsonage (the Brontës) · 53.83139° N, 1.95722° W]`


A woman in Brussels took the pieces of a torn-up letter out of a wastepaper basket and sewed them back together with a needle and thread. Her husband had ripped it up; she mended it, in case a scandal ever needed proving. Four of the letters Charlotte Brontë wrote to Constantin Heger survive because of that, and you can still see the stitching. They are the best-documented erotic fact in the whole Brontë story — and they were written by a woman whose life, viewed from outside, appeared to contain nothing of the kind at all.

The Brontës came to Haworth Parsonage in 1820, when Patrick Brontë was appointed perpetual curate. There were six children. The two eldest, Maria and Elizabeth, died in 1825 of the consequences of a brutal school, a loss that shadowed everything after and fed directly into the Lowood chapters of *Jane Eyre*. That left Charlotte, Branwell, Emily and Anne, who grew up inventing elaborate imaginary kingdoms — Angria, Gondal — and filling the loneliness of the moor with worlds of their own making.

Emily's single novel is the strange, dark heart of the family. *Wuthering Heights*, published in 1847 under the name Ellis Bell, is not a love story in any comfortable sense; it is a book about obsession that survives death. Heathcliff's passion for Catherine does not end when she dies — it runs on for eighteen years, and culminates in one of the most disturbing scenes in the English novel, when he bribes the sexton to open her grave so that he can see her face again and arranges for his own coffin to be laid against hers with the adjoining sides removed. Contemporary reviewers were appalled and impressed in roughly equal measure. They called it coarse and brutal; one American magazine wondered how any human being "could have attempted such a book… without committing suicide," and another dismissed it as "a compound of vulgar depravity and unnatural horrors." Running under all the disgust was disbelief: surely a retiring, consumptive parson's daughter could not have imagined this. When Charlotte later revealed that Ellis, Currer and Acton Bell were three sisters, the shock was genuine and, for some, faintly indecent.

But the best-documented erotic fact in the entire Brontë story is not Emily's fiction. It is Charlotte's heartbreak, and it survives on paper. In 1842 Charlotte and Emily went to Brussels to study at the Pensionnat Heger, and Charlotte fell in love with Constantin Heger, the school's brilliant, exacting director of studies — a married man with children, the first person outside her family to take her seriously as an intellect. She returned to Haworth in 1844 and wrote him letters of extraordinary, naked longing. He did not reply. He tore them up. And here the story becomes almost unbearably poignant, because his wife, Zoë Heger, retrieved the pieces from the wastepaper basket and stitched them back together with needle and thread, mending the torn paper so that a scandal could be proved if one were ever needed. Four of those repaired letters survive, and you can see the stitching. They were donated to the British Library in 1913 by Heger's son, Dr Paul Heger, and his sisters, and printed in *The Times* on 29 July 1913 — described and translated by Marion H. Spielmann — and they are the raw material out of which Charlotte built her fiction: Heger is written across Rochester in *Jane Eyre*, and openly across Paul Emanuel in *Villette*, the novel she set explicitly in Brussels. In one of the letters she tells him, plainly, that if he withholds his friendship entirely she will be "altogether without hope." He had, at least once, scribbled a Brussels shoemaker's address in the margin of her anguish.

Charlotte did marry, eventually, and late. In June 1854 she married her father's curate, Arthur Bell Nicholls — a steady, unglamorous man her father initially opposed. She was happy, by the surviving accounts, for a few months. She died in March 1855, at thirty-eight, in the early stages of pregnancy. It is a bleak arithmetic: the woman who wrote the most famous marriage plot in English literature got less than a year of her own.

Three miles across Haworth Moor stands the ruin that the world insists on calling Wuthering Heights, and it is the perfect place to end, because the people who look after it tell the truth about it. Top Withens is a roofless farmhouse in a superbly bleak position, long associated with Emily's novel. In 1964 the Brontë Society fixed a plaque to its wall which is a small masterpiece of honesty. The building, it says, "even when complete, bore no resemblance to the house she described, but the situation may have been in her mind when she wrote of the moorland setting of the Heights." And then, drily: "This plaque has been placed here in response to many inquiries." The association was never Emily's; it seems to have come from her sister's friend Ellen Nussey, who suggested Top Withens to a publisher's artist illustrating the novels in the 1870s, and the illustration did the rest. Scholars think the actual building described in the novel owes more to the now-demolished High Sunderland Hall near Halifax. Top Withens is not the house. But the walk out to it — along the Pennine Way and the Brontë Way, across the moor the sisters actually walked, with the wind doing precisely what "wuthering" means — gives you the one thing that really was Emily's, which was never a building at all.

You can still climb up there, past a few wind-bent trees, to a ruin that was never the thing it is famous for. It is oddly fitting. The Brontës' whole achievement was to build enormous interior worlds out of almost nothing — out of a parsonage, a graveyard, a moor and a great deal of longing that had nowhere else to go.
