The plaque tells the truth, and the truth is better than the legend: the house was never real, and it never needed to be.



## 30. The Bond at Worcester

*Anne Hathaway's Cottage · Shottery, Warwickshire · 1556–1623 (marriage 1582)*


> **Anne Hathaway's Cottage (Shakespeare's courtship)** — Stratford-upon-Avon, Warwickshire, England
> 52.18694° N, 1.72278° W · confidence 5/5


On 28 November 1582, two Warwickshire farmers named Fulk Sandells and John Richardson rode to Worcester and put their names to a bond for £40 — a serious sum, the price of guaranteeing that a marriage could go ahead. The bond indemnified the Bishop of Worcester against any legal trouble arising from the union of "William Shagspere" and "Anne Hathwey of Stratford… maiden." It permitted the banns to be read only once, rather than the usual three times. This document, dry as a mortgage, is very nearly all we have of the beginning of the most famous marriage in English literature, and almost everything people think they know about it is either an insult or a mistake.

Start with the mistake, because it is a good one. The day before the bond, on 27 November, the Bishop's register recorded a licence issued for the marriage of "Willelmum Shaxpere" and — here is the problem — "Annam Whateley de Temple Grafton." Anne Whateley, of a different village, on a different day. For more than a century this has fuelled a small romantic industry: that Shakespeare loved one Anne and was forced to marry another, that Anne Whateley was the true beloved, the Dark Lady, the road not taken. It makes a lovely story and the scholarship is firmly against it. The overwhelming consensus, following the analysis of J. W. Gray, is that "Whateley" is a clerical error — the register clerk was demonstrably careless, a Whateley appears elsewhere on his pages, and the surnames are close enough to muddle. No independent evidence has ever been found that an Anne Whateley of Temple Grafton existed at all. She is, in one biographer's lovely phrase, a ghost haunting the edges of Shakespeare's story. The likelier truth is duller and warmer: William, aged eighteen and a minor, and Anne, aged twenty-six, needed to marry quickly and quietly, before the Advent season closed the church to weddings, and the single reading of the banns bought them the speed.

Why the hurry? Because Anne was pregnant. Their daughter Susanna was baptised on 26 May 1583, roughly six months after the wedding, which puts Anne about three months gone at the altar. And here the Shakespeare story runs straight back into the Welsh hills of the previous chapter, because this was entirely ordinary. In Elizabethan Warwickshire, as in rural Wales, sex commonly came before the wedding and the wedding regularised it. Betrothal — the troth-plight, the handfast — was itself a binding promise, and a couple who had pledged themselves and then slept together were not thought to have done anything scandalous, provided they married before the child arrived. Anne Hathaway pregnant at her wedding is not evidence of disgrace, or of a reluctant boy trapped by an older woman. It is evidence that she and William did what courting couples across Britain did, from the bundling beds of Cardiganshire to the farmhouses of Shottery.

Anne had grown up at that farmhouse — Hewlands Farm, in the hamlet of Shottery a mile west of Stratford, the twelve-roomed house now known to the world as Anne Hathaway's Cottage. Her father Richard was a prosperous yeoman farmer; the oldest timbers of the house have been dated to 1463, and thirteen generations of Hathaways lived in it. It is where William courted her, and it is where, presumably, Susanna was conceived. Two years after the marriage, in 1585, came the twins, Hamnet and Judith. And then Shakespeare went to London, and largely stayed there, for the better part of two decades, coming home to a wife who kept his household in Stratford while he built a career and a reputation a hundred miles away.

Which brings us to the insult — the second-best bed. Shakespeare's will of 1616 leaves Anne, almost as an afterthought interlined into the document, "my second best bed with the furniture" — the hangings and linen that went with it. For centuries this was read as a deliberate snub, a husband's final cold shoulder to a wife he had come to despise. The best modern reading is very nearly the opposite. In a well-appointed Elizabethan house, the best bed was the guest bed, kept for show; the second-best bed was the marital bed, the one the couple actually slept in, in which their children had been born. To leave Anne that bed was to leave her the most intimate object in the house. And she was in any case entitled by common law to a widow's third of the estate, so the will's silence about the rest is not the abandonment it looks like. Germaine Greer built her 2007 book *Shakespeare's Wife* around exactly this rehabilitation — arguing that Anne was a capable, independent woman who ran a business and a household, not the dreary millstone that four centuries of male biographers, irritated that Shakespeare should have a wife at all, had made of her.

The cottage itself is now the gentlest of the Shakespeare Birthplace Trust's properties, which has owned it since 1892. Its famous garden is a 1920s creation, laid out on the advice of the horticulturist Ellen Willmott; the real Elizabethan plot would have been herbs, vegetables, pigs and mud. In 1969 a fire badly damaged the building, and the Trust restored it. Inside are genuine Hathaway heirlooms, including an ornately carved oak bed whose frame dates to Anne's lifetime — and which visitors, and generations of guides, have longed to identify as the second-best bed. The Trust is honest enough to say there is no evidence for it. Which leaves Anne Hathaway roughly where the documents leave her: pregnant at her wedding like many a bride across Britain, married in a hurry to a teenager who became the greatest writer in the language, left behind while he went to work, and remembered, unfairly, for a bed. She outlived him by seven years, and was buried beside him in Holy Trinity in 1623, under a stone that asks, in Latin, for her not to be moved.



## 31. Medieval Bawdy: Chaucer's Tabard and Gwerful Mechain

*Tabard Inn, Southwark, London & Llanfechain, Powys, Wales · c.1387–1502*


> **The Tabard Inn site (Chaucer's Wife of Bath)** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.50417° N, 0.09000° W · confidence 3/5
>
> **Gwerful Mechain — Wales's Bawdiest Poet** — Llanfechain, Powys, Wales
> 52.79800° N, 3.21600° W · confidence 2/5


> `[PLATE 21 — The Tabard Inn site (Chaucer's Wife of Bath) · 51.50417° N, 0.09000° W]`


The best-known pub in English literature does not exist. It stopped existing in 1873, when the demolition men pulled down a dilapidated galleried coaching inn on the east side of Borough High Street in Southwark, a building that had by then spent two centuries being called the Talbot because a signwriter, at some point after the great Southwark fire of 1676, had confused a tabard (a herald's sleeveless coat) with a talbot (a breed of hunting dog) and nobody had ever quite got round to fixing it.

The original Tabard was old even in Chaucer's day. It had been built for the Abbot of Hyde around 1307 as a place for him and his fellow churchmen to stay when business brought them to London, and by 1381 the tax rolls record an innkeeper living there — one Henry Bailly, with his wife Christian. That name matters. When Geoffrey Chaucer sat down around 1387 to write the frame for *The Canterbury Tales*, he set his pilgrims' departure at the Tabard and made their genial, bullying master of ceremonies a man called Harry Bailly. He was using the name of the real landlord of the real pub. It is as if a modern novelist set a story in a named Wetherspoon's and gave the manager his actual name.

Chaucer was not a full-time poet. Almost nobody was. He was a civil servant, and from 1374 he held one of the more thankless jobs in the medieval bureaucracy: Controller of Customs for the port of London, checking that the correct duty was levied on the wool, sheepskins and leather leaving the country. This was no minor sideline of the exchequer. The historian Mark Ormrod called Edward III's wool taxes "the single most important weapon in the financial armoury of the late medieval state," the custom-plus-subsidy on a sack of wool exceeding thirty per cent of its value — which made Chaucer's a serious, numerate, document-heavy post. He held it for twelve years while living rent-free in an apartment over Aldgate. The *Tales* were written in the margins of a working life.

And what he wrote, in those margins, is the filthiest great poem in English before the modern era. Not all of it — there are saints' lives and sermons and a knightly romance — but Chaucer understood that a pilgrimage is a cross-section of society and that a cross-section of society, given a few drinks, tells dirty jokes. The Miller's Tale is the purest of these: a randy Oxford lodger named Nicholas seduces his landlord's teenage wife Alison, persuades the gullible old carpenter that a second Noah's flood is coming so he'll sleep in a tub hung from the rafters, and — the detail every schoolchild remembers — a rejected suitor, Absolon, comes to the window in the dark begging a kiss, and Alison offers him not her lips. The Reeve's Tale answers it with two Cambridge students taking their revenge on a thieving miller by bedding his wife and daughter in a single dark night of misdirected beds and moved cradles. These are *fabliaux* — short, obscene comic tales — and Chaucer tells them with a technical brilliance that has kept them on syllabuses precisely because teachers cannot pretend they are about anything other than what they are about.

But the truly startling voice belongs to a woman. The Wife of Bath — Alison of Bath, a prosperous cloth-maker — is given the longest prologue of any pilgrim, and she uses it to talk, with tremendous relish and not a shred of shame, about sex, marriage and power. She has buried five husbands. She married the first three, who were old and rich, for their money, and cheerfully describes withholding sex until they signed over their land; she loved the fifth, who was young and hit her, best of all. She discusses her own appetite as a simple fact of life. Chaucer even lets her make a joke that turns on the word "queynte" — a pun hovering between "a curious thing" and the plainest available Anglo-Saxon word for the female anatomy — and she deploys it more than once. The astonishing thing is not the smut. The astonishing thing is the argument underneath it: that what women most desire is "sovereynetee," mastery, sovereignty over their own husbands and their own lives. A fourteenth-century male poet put that thesis in the mouth of a woman and gave her the wit to defend it. It would be centuries before English literature was that frank about a woman's desires again — and when D. H. Lawrence tried, they took him to the Old Bailey.

Southwark, it should be said, was the right postcode for all this. It lay outside the City of London's jurisdiction, under the more relaxed authority of the Bishop of Winchester, whose Bankside "stews" employed the Winchester Geese, whose licensed trade this book returns to in Chapter 10. Chaucer's pilgrims set out for Canterbury from the exact edge of medieval London's red-light district.

Two centuries and the better part of two hundred miles away, in the Powys parish of Llanfechain in mid-Wales, a woman was writing something even blunter. Gwerful Mechain (flourished c.1460–1502) is the only medieval Welsh woman poet from whom a substantial body of work survives — the daughter of Hywel Fychan of Mechain and a woman named Gwenhwyfar, married to John ap Llywelyn Fychan, mother of a daughter called Mawd. She wrote devotional verse of real seriousness ("Christ's Passion," "Death and Judgement") in the fiendishly demanding strict metres of Welsh bardic poetry, all internal rhyme and consonantal chiming, called *cynghanedd*. And she wrote, in exactly the same exacting metre, a poem called "Cywydd y Cedor" — "Ode to the Vulva."

It is not a euphemism-laden thing. It opens by scolding male poets — the great professionals — for lavishing praise on a woman's eyes, her hair, her hands, her elegant throat, and then stopping, cowards that they are, at exactly the point that matters, ignoring "the best bit." And then Gwerful does what they would not: she praises the vulva directly, at length, ingeniously, in the high poetic manner usually reserved for God or a patron. Her modern translator, Professor Katie Gramich of Cardiff University, whose bilingual edition *The Works of Gwerful Mechain* (2018) finally brought her to a wide readership, has admitted to blushing when reading the Welsh aloud. The poem was a riposte, roughly a century late, to Dafydd ap Gwilym's earlier "Cywydd y Gal," "Ode to the Penis." Fair, you might think, is fair.

The manuscripts prove she was no isolated eccentric. According to Gramich's edition, "There are 13 manuscript copies of this cywydd, located in the University of Bangor, the British Library, Cardiff Central Library, and the National Library of Wales." She exchanged poems — jousting, flirtatious, sometimes obscene — with the leading male poets of her day, Dafydd Llwyd of Mathafarn (whom scholars think was probably her lover) and Llywelyn ap Gutyn. She answered a misogynist, Ieuan Dyfi, with a defence of women. Her range was total: she also wrote a short, cold, unforgettable poem addressed to her husband after he beat her, a curse wishing a dagger into his chest. It is documented, it is attributed to her, and it is a reminder that a woman writing gleefully about sex in the fifteenth century was not necessarily a woman who was safe.

No house of Gwerful's survives. Llanfechain is a quiet mid-Wales village and there is nothing to visit but the churchyard and the manuscripts in their four libraries. But that is rather the point of this pairing. Before print, before the Reformation, before the Puritans and the Victorians got to work, English and Welsh literature contained women and men writing about sex with a directness that the next five centuries would systematically bury. Gwerful was suppressed, Gramich argues, by the very Welsh scholars of the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries who should have been preserving her — omitted from the anthologies because she was indecent. The frankness of the Welsh material this book keeps finding — the bundling beds, the holy wells — was buried by the same hands. What survives of the boldest woman in medieval Welsh poetry is thirteen copies of a poem about a vulva, kept safe, unread, in the archives of a nation that had decided to be embarrassed by her.



## 32. Lady Chatterley's Country

*Renishaw Hall, Derbyshire & Teversal, Nottinghamshire · 1926–1960*


> **Renishaw Hall (model for Wragby Hall)** — Renishaw, Derbyshire, England
> 53.30256° N, 1.34722° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Teversal (fictional Tevershall)** — Teversal, Nottinghamshire, England
> 53.15209° N, 1.27865° W · confidence 5/5


"The word 'fuck' or 'fucking' appears no less than 30 times... 'cunt' 14 times; 'balls' 13 times; 'shit' and 'arse' six times apiece; 'cock' four times; 'piss' three times, and so on."

That is not a review. That is the prosecution. In October 1960, at the Old Bailey, Mervyn Griffith-Jones — Eton, Cambridge, a decorated war hero who had helped prosecute Nazis at Nuremberg — stood up on behalf of the Crown and read out, to a jury of nine men and three women, a tally of the rude words in a novel. He had counted them. Somewhere in the preparation of the case, a barrister had gone through *Lady Chatterley's Lover* line by line with a pencil, noting on page 204 alone one "fucking," one "shit," three "balls" and a phrase too robust to reproduce in a family history, and totting up the grand totals. It is one of the great images of British officialdom: the state, in a horsehair wig, doing sums about swearing.

The book they were prosecuting had been thirty-two years in making its way to a British bookshop. D. H. Lawrence, a Nottinghamshire miner's son from Eastwood, wrote it three times over between 1926 and 1928, each version franker than the last, at the Villa Mirenda near Florence. Unable to find any English or American publisher willing to touch it, he had the third and rudest version privately printed in Florence in 1928 by a bookseller named Giuseppe "Pino" Orioli, in an edition of a thousand signed copies. It made him more money than he had ever earned, chiefly because it was instantly, comprehensively pirated. Lawrence died in 1930. He never saw it published openly in his own country.

The novel is set in a landscape Lawrence knew in his bones: the coal-and-iron Midlands, all pit-heads and soot and blackened brick. Constance Chatterley is married to Sir Clifford, a baronet paralysed from the waist down by the war, master of Wragby Hall. Into the gap in her life steps Oliver Mellors, the estate's gamekeeper, and what follows is a love affair conducted in plain Anglo-Saxon words — a deliberate choice by Lawrence, who believed the euphemisms were the real obscenity and that calling the thing by its oldest name was a kind of honesty.

Where was Wragby? Two Midlands houses claim it, and the claims are worth separating carefully. Renishaw Hall in Derbyshire, seat of the Sitwell family for four hundred years, is the more glamorous candidate; it is widely said to have been Lawrence's model. The truth is more comic. Lawrence and his wife Frieda visited Renishaw only once, at Sir Osbert Sitwell's invitation, and by Edith Sitwell's own later account they were shown in the front door and straight out the back into the garden — so all Lawrence actually saw of the grand house was the entrance hall. Wragby in the novel is deliberately undistinguished, "a long, low old house... without much distinction," which the actual Renishaw emphatically is not. The other candidate, Teversal in Nottinghamshire — a real colliery village generally taken to be the fictional "Tevershall" — has its own Wragby claimant in the Grade II-listed Teversal Manor. Both identifications are traditional and neither is provable; the honest position is that Lawrence, walking the country around Eastwood, built Wragby out of several places and the inside of his own head.

Then came the trial. The Obscene Publications Act of 1959 had, for the first time, allowed literary merit as a defence, and Penguin Books deliberately printed an unexpurgated Chatterley to test it, handing copies to the police by arrangement. The case, *R v Penguin Books Ltd*, ran at the Old Bailey from 20 October to 2 November 1960, Mr Justice Byrne presiding. The defence called an extraordinary parade of thirty-five expert witnesses — the critic Richard Hoggart, the Bishop of Woolwich, academics, clergymen, a headmaster — to testify to the book's seriousness. The prosecution called one policeman.

And then Griffith-Jones asked his question. Warning the jury not to be priggish, he wondered aloud: "Would you approve of your young sons, young daughters — because girls can read as well as boys — reading this book? Is it a book that you would have lying around in your own house? Is it a book that you would even wish your wife or your servants to read?" The courtroom laughed. It is the single most catastrophic sentence in the history of English advocacy, and its beauty is that it revealed everything: that the prosecutor genuinely imagined a jury of 1960 Britons kept live-in servants, and that the whole apparatus of obscenity law rested on the idea that literature was safe for gentlemen but dangerous for women and the lower orders. Three of the jurors were women. Quite a few of them were exactly the sort of people the question assumed did not exist. On 2 November they acquitted Penguin in three hours. Per Sotheby's, Penguin then sold its entire 200,000-copy print run in a single day, and an estimated two million copies over the following two years; Foyle's shifted its 300 copies in fifteen minutes.

The larger point is the one Philip Larkin made, in a poem this book will gesture at but not quote, dating the start of something to the gap between the end of the Chatterley ban and a certain pop group's first LP. He was joking, and he was not. The most consequential erotic book in modern British history was set in a specific, sooty patch of the East Midlands — Byron's county, as it happens, Newstead lying just up the road — and the fight over whether Britons could read it was the moment the state finally lost the power to decide what the British were allowed to put in front of their own eyes.



## 33. Burns

*Globe Inn, Dumfries & Highland Mary's Monument, Failford, South Ayrshire · 1786–1796*


> **The Globe Inn, Dumfries (Burns & Anna Park)** — Dumfries, Dumfriesshire, Scotland
> 55.06758° N, 3.60970° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Highland Mary's Monument / River Ayr trysting bank** — Failford, Ayrshire (South Ayrshire), Scotland
> 55.48370° N, 4.42560° W · confidence 5/5


> `[PLATE 22 — The Globe Inn, Dumfries (Burns & Anna Park) · 55.06758° N, 3.60970° W]`


Robert Burns fathered at least twelve children — most accounts say by four different mothers, though the full roll of women in his bed is longer — and he wrote some of the tenderest love songs in the language, and both of those facts are true at once, and the second does not excuse the first. Any honest account of Scotland's national poet has to hold the two in the same hand.

Start at the Globe Inn, down a close off the High Street in Dumfries, established in 1610 and Burns's self-described "favourite howff" — his word for a haunt — from 1789 until his death in 1796. He had come to Dumfries as an exciseman, a taxman, having given up on farming, and the Globe was where he drank, sang, updated his ledgers, and conducted at least one of his affairs. In an upstairs bedroom he scratched verses into the windowpanes with a diamond-tipped stylus, and two of those panes survive there still, along with the chair he sat in — you can, if you visit, be made to recite a line of Burns as the price of sitting in it.

The woman in that room was Anna Park, niece of the landlady, Mrs Hyslop, and a barmaid at the Globe. She was in her early twenties. Their affair produced a daughter, Elizabeth — "Betty" — born on 31 March 1791. Nine days later, Burns's wife Jean Armour bore him a son, William Nicol Burns. Sit with that arithmetic for a moment: the poet had a legitimate child and an illegitimate child born nine days apart. Anna Park's fate is uncertain — one strong tradition holds that she died young, possibly in or soon after childbirth — and the baby was brought to Dumfries and raised by Jean Armour as one of her own. Burns wrote a song for Anna, "The Gowden Locks of Anna," which opens "Yestreen I had a pint o' wine" and which he privately rated among his best. He was, throughout, quite capable of loving the song more reliably than the woman.

Jean Armour is the quiet heroine of the whole story. She had already borne Burns two sets of twins; her father had once torn up the paper by which the couple declared themselves married. And when her husband's love-child by another woman needed a home, she took the baby in and brought her up, reportedly remarking — the line is much quoted and comes down through family tradition rather than a contemporary document, so treat it as attributed folklore — that "our Robin should hae had twa wives." Betty grew up in the household and, after Jean's death, spoke of her adoptive mother with real love.

The other place on the map is a sandstone column on a hillside at Failford in South Ayrshire, marking the traditional spot where, in May 1786, Burns and Mary Campbell — "Highland Mary" — are said to have exchanged vows across a small stream, each holding a Bible, before parting. Here the folklore and the fact need pulling firmly apart. The Bible-and-stream tableau comes largely from R. H. Cromek's account of 1808, published after both parties were dead, and the twentieth-century biographer James Mackay was among those who doubted its embroidered details. What is better documented: Mary was a servant, a dairymaid and nursemaid; Burns wrote several poems to her ("Highland Mary," "To Mary in Heaven," "Will Ye Go to the Indies, My Mary?"); they seem to have planned to emigrate to Jamaica together; and she died at Greenock in October 1786, probably of typhus contracted nursing her brother. The two-volume Bible Burns gave her, inscribed in his hand with his mason's mark, survives and is held at the Robert Burns Birthplace Museum. And the timing is, again, unforgiving: while Burns was exchanging tender vows with Highland Mary, Jean Armour was pregnant with his twins.

There is a darker footnote, and it must be handled with care. When Mary's grave at Greenock was opened in 1920 — the shipyard next door needed the land — the diggers reportedly found, among the remains in the lair, the bottom board of an infant's coffin. This prompted immediate speculation that Mary had died not of fever but in childbirth, carrying Burns's child. It is a genuinely contested point. Later investigation held that the infant's board belonged to a much later burial in the same family lair, in 1827, and one careful modern account concludes flatly that Mary was not pregnant when she died. The truth is unrecoverable, and the speculation tells us at least as much about our appetite for the story as about Mary.

The kirk knew all about Robert Burns. Scotland's Presbyterian church discipline ran on the Kirk Session, a parish court that hauled fornicators before the congregation to be publicly rebuked from the "cutty stool," the stool of repentance — a system uncannily like the Welsh chapel *seiat* that appears elsewhere in this book, a whole nation's machinery for policing what people did in bed. Burns knew the cutty stool intimately. He was rebuked at Mauchline for his affair with the servant Elizabeth Paton, mother of his first child, "dear-bought Bess," born 1785; he did penance again over Jean Armour. And in his poem "The Fornicator" he cheerfully refuses to be shamed, describing standing at the stool beside "my handsome Betsey" with his eye already wandering back to her figure. The kirk session minutes survive; the poet is in the record, unrepentant.

Be careful, though, about calling any of it charming. The roll of women is a roll of servants with little power: Elizabeth Paton; Jean Armour, twice bearing twins; Anna Park; May Cameron, an Edinburgh servant girl left pregnant and effectively abandoned, whom Burns asked a friend to pay off and get "out to some country friends"; Jenny Clow, another servant, who bore him a son and died of tuberculosis, poor and largely forgotten, while the poet fretted in a letter about a silhouette. Burns is the great exception in this chapter — the one figure with no restraint whatever on his desires, a man who wrote about sex as freely as he lived it, which is precisely why the kirk kept dragging him to the stool. His frankness is the mirror image of every thwarted, censored poet in these pages. But the freedom was his. The consequences, mostly, were theirs.



## 34. 50 Wimpole Street

*50 Wimpole Street, Marylebone, London · 1838–1846*


> **50 Wimpole Street (the Barrett–Browning elopement)** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.51861° N, 0.14833° W · confidence 4/5


In January 1845 a letter arrived at 50 Wimpole Street for Miss Elizabeth Barrett. It was from a poet she had never met, and it began: "I love your verses with all my heart, dear Miss Barrett." Then, having praised the freshness and the music and the brave new thought, it went further and told her he loved her too. Robert Browning was thirty-two, ambitious, comparatively unknown. Elizabeth Barrett was thirty-eight, famous, and an invalid confined to a single second-floor back room in her father's house, where she had spent years on a sofa, dosed with laudanum for a lung complaint and a spinal pain that had never left her since her teens. She had once said her room's shadowy walls looked "so much like Newgate's turned inside out."

What that letter began is probably the most documented courtship in English literature: over the next twenty months the two poets exchanged 573 letters, held since 1930 at Wellesley College's Margaret Clapp Library in Massachusetts. And yet — this is the strange, constrained heart of it — they conducted almost all of it on paper while living a few miles apart, because for a long time they were barely allowed to meet. Browning first visited on 20 May 1845. By his own meticulous count he came to that room ninety-one times. Every visit required the permission of Elizabeth's father.

Edward Moulton-Barrett is one of the great domestic tyrants of the age, and his tyranny took a peculiar form: he had forbidden any of his eleven surviving children ever to marry. Not Elizabeth specifically — all of them. The prohibition was total and, as far as anyone could tell, motiveless beyond the will to keep his family intact and under his hand. So the courtship was carried out in secret, letter by letter, visit by supervised visit, with Elizabeth's maid Elizabeth Wilson as go-between and her spaniel Flush as witness.

The money that paid for that comfortable, oppressive house has to be named plainly, because Elizabeth named it herself. The Barrett fortune came from sugar plantations in Jamaica worked by enslaved people. Elizabeth knew exactly what that meant and was ashamed of it. She was a committed abolitionist; when the Slavery Abolition Act passed in 1833 she declared herself glad the enslaved were "virtually — free," even though her father thought the West Indies would be ruined by it. She wrote, later, to John Ruskin: "I belong to a family of West Indian slaveholders, and if I believed in curses, I should be afraid." She wrote one of the fiercest abolitionist poems in the language, "The Runaway Slave at Pilgrim's Point." Her liberation and her guilt were tangled together: it was partly money that ultimately came to her from the same slave-worked estates that gave her the independence to defy her father.

Defy him she did. On 12 September 1846, Elizabeth slipped out of Wimpole Street with only Wilson for company, walked to St Marylebone Parish Church, and married Robert Browning. Then — and this is the detail that catches the throat — she went home. She returned to her father's house and lived there another week as though nothing had happened, before slipping out a second time, this time for good, with Wilson and Flush, to flee to Italy.

Her father never forgave her. He returned her letters unopened. He disinherited her, as he disinherited every child of his who dared to marry. She never saw him again; when he died in 1857 she was still unpardoned. In Italy her health improved, astonishingly; in 1849, at the age of forty-three, she bore a son. The sonnets she had written in secret during the courtship — she showed them to Browning only in 1849, and he gave them the disguising title *Sonnets from the Portuguese* — contain the most famous opening line of any English love poem, four public-domain words about counting the ways, which everyone can finish and which she wrote about the man she was forbidden to marry. She died in Florence in 1861.

The house is gone. Number 50 Wimpole Street was demolished in the 1930s; a plaque records that Elizabeth Barrett lived there from 1838 to 1846, and the stonework of the building now on the spot is a 1930s addition. When the whole row was pulled down, an American admirer bought the actual front door — the door Robert Browning stood before ninety-one times, waiting to be let in — and shipped it across the Atlantic to Wellesley, which already held the 573 letters. The door and the letters are together. The house is not there at all. It is a very Wimpole Street ending: the love survived, and the prison was demolished.



## 35. Keats House

*Wentworth Place (Keats House), Hampstead, London · 1818–1821*


> **Keats House (Keats & Fanny Brawne)** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.55528° N, 0.16639° W · confidence 5/5


> `[PLATE 23 — Keats House (Keats & Fanny Brawne) · 51.55528° N, 0.16639° W]`


Keats knew he was dying the moment he saw the colour. On a night in February 1820, back at Wentworth Place after a cold coach journey home to Hampstead, he coughed, saw the blood on the sheet, and — because he had trained as an apothecary and surgeon and knew exactly what he was looking at — told his friend Charles Brown it was arterial blood. "That drop of blood is my death-warrant," he said. "I must die." He was twenty-four. He had rather less than a year of poetry, and of Fanny, left.

Wentworth Place, in what is now Keats Grove in Hampstead, was built around 1815 as a clever piece of Regency real estate: one handsome white villa that was secretly two houses, divided down the middle by an internal wall. Keats moved into one half in December 1818, sharing with Charles Brown, paying £5 a month and splitting the liquor bill. In April 1819 the other half was taken by a widow, Mrs Brawne, and her children — among them Fanny, eighteen years old. The girl next door was, quite literally, the girl next door: only a party wall between the poet and the young woman he would love to distraction.

He had been sniffy about her at first. In a letter to his brother he called her "beautiful and elegant, graceful, silly, fashionable and strange." But by the summer of 1819 he was writing her letters of an intensity that still stops the reader cold, and that autumn they became secretly engaged. He gave her a ring — a modest thing, gold set with an almandine garnet, possibly his late mother's — and she wore it. It is on display at Keats House today, in the room that was hers, one of the small band of relics through which we know this story.

The garden did its part. Brown recorded that in the spring of 1819 Keats carried a chair out onto the grass under a plum tree, where a nightingale had nested, and in a single morning wrote "Ode to a Nightingale." The plum tree is long gone; a replacement has been planted where it stood, and an ancient mulberry that Keats would have known as a sapling still grows there. When he was too ill to leave the house in early 1820, he lay on a sofa by the window and watched Fanny walk in the garden, telling her by note that he would follow her with his eyes.

Then the arterial blood, and the death sentence he read in it himself. His friends raised money to send him to the warmth of Italy; he left in September 1820, sailing away from Fanny and from Hampstead, and he never saw either again. He could not bring himself to write to her from Rome, and he could not bear to open her letters — the pain, he said, went through him like a spear. He died in Rome on 23 February 1821, aged twenty-five. And here is the fact I have kept back: when Keats was buried, at his own request, Fanny's letters to him went into the coffin, unopened, laid against him in the shroud. Whatever she had written to him in his last months, no one has ever read. He took her words with him into the ground.

Fanny mourned him for years. She cut her hair short, wore black, kept his ring, walked on Hampstead Heath where they had walked, and did not marry until 1833, twelve years after his death. History repaid her badly for it. When Keats's love letters to her — thirty-seven of them survive, among the most naked in the language — were published in 1878, Victorian readers were not moved but scandalised, and the scandal fell not on the dead poet but on the living woman. Matthew Arnold thought the letters should never have been printed; the love-letter of Keats, he wrote, was "the love-letter of a surgeon's apprentice," with "something underbred and ignoble" in it. The assumption, running through decades of male commentary, was that a flighty, shallow girl had made a fool of a genius. Fanny had no defence, because her side of the correspondence was in the coffin. Only in the twentieth century, when her warm, sensible letters to Keats's sister came to light, did her reputation recover.

Wentworth Place is now Keats House, run by the City of London Corporation, its two halves long since knocked into one. The ring is in Fanny's room; a lock of Keats's hair is in the collection; the plum tree stands again, or its descendant does, over the patch of grass where the nightingale sang. It is a small house that holds an enormous quantity of feeling, and the most powerful thing in it is an absence — the letters that were never read, sealed in the dark of a grave in Rome, the one part of this love story that was allowed to stay private.



## 36. The Cleaned-Up Households of the Lakes

*Dove Cottage, Grasmere · Greta Hall, Keswick · Calais and Blois · Cumbria & France · 1791–1810*


> **Dove Cottage (the Wordsworths at Grasmere)** — Grasmere, Cumbria, England
> 54.45528° N, 3.01667° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Greta Hall — Coleridge, Opium & 'Asra'** — Keswick, Cumbria (Cumberland), England
> 54.60130° N, 3.13800° W · confidence 4/5
>
> **Wordsworth's Secret French Daughter** — Grasmere, Cumbria (Westmorland), England
> 54.45820° N, 3.02490° W · confidence 4/5


On the morning of 4 October 1802, in a farmhouse at Gallow Hill in Yorkshire, Dorothy Wordsworth put her brother's wedding ring on her own finger and wore it through the night. In the morning William came to her room and she gave it back to him; he slipped it onto her finger again, blessed her, and went off to church to marry Mary Hutchinson. Dorothy could not go. She lay on the bed until the two men came running up to say it was done, and then, she wrote, she could stand it no longer and threw herself down. The passage is one of the most quietly astonishing things in English literature, and it survives because Dorothy wrote it in the Grasmere Journal in her own hand — "I gave him the wedding ring — with how deep a blessing! I took it from my forefinger where I had worn it the whole of the night before — he slipped it again onto my finger and blessed me fervently."

What we do with that passage is another matter entirely, and here we should tread carefully. Some readers, including serious scholars, have found an unmistakable erotic charge in it and in the whole texture of William and Dorothy's life together at Dove Cottage — the shared bed on cold nights, the recorded lying-down together in a hollow as if in the grave, the intensity of a devotion that no husband and no wife ever displaced. Others regard it as an extraordinarily intense but essentially chaste sibling attachment, expressed in the heightened emotional idiom that the age permitted between family members, and warn against reading twenty-first-century categories back into 1802. Both readings are defensible. Neither can be proved. What is certain is only what Dorothy wrote — and, tellingly, what she did not leave: a passage in the journal was later scored out by an unknown hand, in ink of roughly the same age, so that we will never know what those lines said. Somebody, at some point, decided the record should be quieter.

Dove Cottage itself is a small, dark, low-ceilinged former inn at Town End, Grasmere, where William and Dorothy settled in December 1799 and stayed until 1808. It is where Dorothy kept the journal, where William wrote a great deal of his best work, and where the household expanded and contracted with an astonishing cast: Mary Hutchinson, who married William and moved in; her sister Sara Hutchinson, who came and went; and, endlessly, Samuel Taylor Coleridge, who lived twelve miles off and treated the walk as nothing. When the Wordsworths finally left, the tenancy passed to a young admirer named Thomas De Quincey, who would later frighten the neighbourhood with his opium and his memoirs. It is the same small Lakeland world, and Coleridge is the man who links its beds together.

Coleridge had taken Greta Hall, a handsome house on a rise above Keswick, in 1800, partly to be near Wordsworth. He was married to Sara Fricker, unhappily, and had been more or less from the start. And in the autumn of 1799 he had met the other Sara — Sara Hutchinson, Mary's sister — and fallen for her with a completeness that never left him and never, so far as anyone can establish, got anywhere at all. He coded her in his notebooks as "Asra", partly to distinguish her from his wife, partly to hide her; scholars think the anagram nods at "Astra", the stars. He wrote of his soul fluttering in a spider-web net of steel around the idea of Sara. It is one of the most thoroughly documented hopeless passions in literature, and it is documented because Coleridge could not stop writing it down.

The key document is a poem. On the night of 4 April 1802 — the same terrible spring in which all this was coming to a head — Coleridge sat down and wrote a verse letter of some 340 lines addressed directly to "Sara", pouring out his misery, his dead marriage, his lost creative power, and his love for a woman he could not have. Over the following months he cut it almost in half, took "Sara" out and replaced her with the neutral "Lady", removed the references to Wordsworth, and published the result as "Dejection: An Ode". The date he chose to publish it was 4 October 1802 — Wordsworth's wedding day, and the seventh anniversary of his own. The full verse letter to Sara Hutchinson did not appear in print until 1937. Coleridge had, in other words, performed on his own greatest late poem exactly the operation the Wordsworth family would later perform on William's life: he took out the woman and tidied the record.

The friendship that held all these people together did not survive. In 1810 a mutual acquaintance, Basil Montagu — a man the Wordsworths had known for years, whose motherless small son they had once brought up — repeated to Coleridge some crushing remarks William had made about his drinking and his hopelessness, to the effect that he was a nuisance and a hopeless drunkard. Coleridge, by now deep in opium, was devastated. The two men who had made English Romanticism between them were essentially estranged for years, and never fully recovered the old intimacy. Wordsworth's descendants would later insist he had been "massively supportive" and that the real fault was Montagu's tale-bearing. As so often in this story, the family wanted the record improved.

Which brings us to the thing the family most wanted improved. In 1791, aged twenty-one, William Wordsworth went to France, fell in with the Revolution, and fell in love with a young Frenchwoman named Annette Vallon, four years his senior, from a royalist Catholic family in Orléans. On 15 December 1792 the register office at Orléans recorded the baptism of Anne-Caroline, daughter of Marie Anne Vallon and William Wordsworth. He had already gone to Paris, and soon afterwards returned to England; war between Britain and France then slammed the door for the best part of a decade. When the brief Peace of Amiens reopened it, William and Dorothy travelled to Calais in the summer of 1802 to meet Annette and the daughter William had never known — a settling of accounts before his marriage to Mary that autumn. It produced the tender sonnet about a walk with a child by the sea, "It is a beauteous evening, calm and free", written to a daughter he would rarely see again.

And then the whole business simply vanished. Wordsworth concealed it; the family after him concealed it; the most morally elevated poet in Victorian England, the one whose verses were printed in improving editions for children, had a French daughter that almost nobody knew about. It stayed hidden until the American scholar George McLean Harper reconstructed it — from Dorothy's own letters, found among the British Museum manuscripts, in which she referred quite openly to "Madame Vallon" and to Caroline as her niece — and published it in his 1916 biography, following it in 1921 with a slim book bluntly titled *Wordsworth's French Daughter*. The affair had never been a secret to the Wordsworths. It had simply been kept from everyone else.

That is the argument of this whole small Lakeland world. The most respectable poets in England ran, between them, a household of quite extraordinary emotional complication — a sister who wore the wedding ring, a poet in love with his sister-in-law, a concealed child across the Channel — and then spent the best part of a century, with the diligent help of their families, tidying it into something a vicar could recommend.



## 37. The Novelist of the Barely-Touched Hand

*Jane Austen's House, Chawton · The Cobb, Lyme Regis · Hampshire & Dorset · 1795–1817*


> **Jane Austen's House, Chawton** — Chawton, Hampshire, England
> 51.12611° N, 0.98833° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **The Cobb, Lyme Regis (Persuasion & the French Lieutenant's Woman)** — Lyme Regis, Dorset, England
> 50.72139° N, 2.94250° W · confidence 5/5


> `[PLATE 24 — Jane Austen's House, Chawton · 51.12611° N, 0.98833° W]`


On the morning of 3 December 1802 Jane Austen did the single most dramatic thing she is known to have done, and she did it by leaving. The evening before, at Manydown Park near Steventon, she had accepted a proposal of marriage from Harris Bigg-Wither, the younger brother of two of her dearest friends. He was twenty-one; she was a few days short of twenty-seven. He was heir to a large Hampshire estate, tall, awkward, prone to a stammer, and — this being the whole point of the age — a solution. Marrying him would have made Jane mistress of a manor, secured her ageing parents, and relieved her brothers of two unmarried sisters. She said yes. And then she lay awake, and in the morning she found Harris, took it back, and demanded to be driven the eleven miles to her brother's rectory at once, in tears, refusing to say why. She and Cassandra fled the same day.

That is the central documented erotic fact of Jane Austen's life: not a consummation but a refusal, a woman of nearly twenty-seven turning down the only secure offer she is known to have received because she could not marry without love. It is Elizabeth Bennet's principle, tested on its author in the dark, at real cost. Everything else is fragments. There was a flirtation with a charming young Irishman named Tom Lefroy in the winter of 1795–96, documented in her own giddy letters to Cassandra — she confessed to behaving in the most "profligate and shocking" way in the matter of dancing and sitting down together — and then he was gone, packed off by prudent relatives, and that was that. There was a mysterious gentleman met at the seaside, of whom Cassandra spoke only in old age to her nieces: someone charming, seemingly serious, who meant to see them again and then abruptly died. Cassandra believed, if Jane ever truly loved, it was him. She would say no more, and there is no more to say, because the person who could have told us made sure of it.

For this is the other central fact, and it runs right through the book: after Jane's death in 1817, Cassandra Austen went through her sister's letters and burned the great bulk of them. What survives is a fraction, and Cassandra kept back or destroyed almost everything touching the years of deepest feeling — the seaside suitor, the aftermath of the Bigg-Wither night. She was not being careless; she was protecting Jane, in the way sisters did. But it means the erotic life of the greatest English novelist of manners reaches us as a series of gaps that another woman deliberately made. The map of Jane Austen's heart is mostly blank, and it is blank on purpose.

The house where most of the work happened is at Chawton, in Hampshire — a red-brick former inn (once, briefly and disreputably, an alehouse called the New Inn) that her brother Edward, adopted into the wealthy Knight family, gave rent-free to his mother and sisters in 1809. Jane lived here for the last eight years of her life, and here she revised *Sense and Sensibility* and *Pride and Prejudice* and wrote *Mansfield Park*, *Emma* and *Persuasion*. She wrote at a tiny walnut table, twelve-sided, scarcely larger than a dinner plate, set by a window in the general sitting room, because she had no study of her own and did most of her work amid the interruptions of a busy household. The often-repeated detail is that she asked that a creaking door be left un-oiled, so that its noise would warn her to cover her manuscript before anyone came in. It is a wonderful image of a private art conducted in a house with no privacy — the novelist of concealment, concealing.

And concealment is exactly how the novels generate their extraordinary erotic voltage, because there is almost nothing on the surface. Austen does not describe a kiss. She barely describes a touch. What she gives you instead is the hand — a hand pressed, a hand withdrawn, a glove, an arm offered on a walk, the precise social temperature of who may touch whom and when. When Captain Wentworth silently lifts a troublesome child off Anne Elliot's back in *Persuasion*, or removes her, wordlessly, into a carriage, the reader's nerves do more work than any bedroom scene could ask of them. The charge is entirely in the restraint. It is the negative space that hums.

Which is why Lyme Regis, on the Dorset coast, matters. Austen visited the little resort in 1803 and 1804 — she wrote to Cassandra about the bathing-machines and the walks — and she loved the place enough to give it the single most physical scene she ever wrote. In *Persuasion*, a party walks out along the Cobb, the great curving stone harbour wall, and the giddy, headstrong Louisa Musgrove insists on being "jumped down" the steep steps by Captain Wentworth. She likes the sensation; on their walks he has always had to jump her from the stiles. He obliges once, reluctantly. She runs back up to do it again, he is not ready, she leaps too soon — "she was too precipitate by half a second, she fell on the pavement on the Lower Cobb, and was taken up lifeless!" It is a fall in every sense, the closest Austen comes to letting a woman's physical craving for a man drive the plot off a wall. A critic writing in *Partisan Review* (Vol. 62, No. 1, 1995) read the whole episode as a scarcely-veiled sexual consummation, thrilling and dangerous and punished — pointing to Austen's own wry line elsewhere in the novel that Wentworth had been "making love, by breaking his mistress's head", and to the pattern that runs through the book connecting sex and childbirth with injury. The steep, rail-less steps jutting from the wall are known as Granny's Teeth, and generations of visitors have tried them, though the steps we now point to may not be the ones Austen imagined — she was more likely thinking of a flight further along. When Tennyson walked nine miles to Lyme in 1867 he brushed aside all offers of refreshment: "Don't talk to me of the Duke of Monmouth," he said; "show me the exact spot where Louisa Musgrove fell."

The Cobb had a second literary life. John Fowles lived in Lyme, and it is on that same wall, in the sea-fret, that *The French Lieutenant's Woman* (1969) opens with its motionless, staring figure — a book, and a 1981 film, in which desire is once again a matter of a woman standing where she should not, looking out to sea. Two of the most charged scenes in English fiction happen on one Dorset breakwater, and in both the eroticism is a matter of position, distance and the held breath. Austen would have understood exactly.



## 38. Gothic Desire: The Dead Woman's Nightdress and the Coffin-Ship

*Menabilly, near Fowey, Cornwall · Whitby Abbey, North Yorkshire · 1890–1969*


> **Menabilly (Daphne du Maurier's Manderley)** — Fowey, Cornwall, England
> 50.33417° N, 4.66889° W · confidence 2/5
>
> **Whitby Abbey (Dracula's landing)** — Whitby, North Yorkshire, England
> 54.48861° N, 0.60722° W · confidence 5/5


The house that became Manderley was one Daphne du Maurier never owned. Menabilly, the Rashleigh family's long, grey, shuttered mansion buried in woods on the Gribbin peninsula near Fowey, she first reached as a trespasser — walking miles through rhododendrons to find it apparently abandoned, windows barred, "like the sleeping beauty of the fairy tale", she wrote, "until someone should come to wake her." She was obsessed. She wrote *Rebecca* in 1938 while still only able to prowl its grounds by permission, rechristening the place Manderley and opening the novel with a line every novelist since has envied: "Last night I dreamt I went to Manderley again." Then, extraordinarily, in 1943 — with the house roofless, lightless, waterless, fungus on the ceilings, in the middle of a war — she talked Dr Rashleigh into leasing it to her, and she lived and wrote there for twenty-six years, until the family reclaimed it in 1969. She never got to buy it. Leaving, she said, was like tearing up a mandrake, which bleeds and shrieks.

*Rebecca* is usually filed as romance or gothic thriller, but its real subject is sexual jealousy and the erotic power a dead woman goes on exerting over a living one. The narrator — famously never named, so that the first, dead wife Rebecca dominates even the space where the heroine's name should be — arrives at Manderley as the second Mrs de Winter and finds the house, her husband and her own imagination wholly possessed by her predecessor. The custodian of that possession is the housekeeper, Mrs Danvers, who loved Rebecca with an intensity the novel never quite names and never needs to. In the celebrated scene in Rebecca's preserved bedroom, Danvers takes the shy young narrator through the dead woman's things — draws the nightdress from its case, has her feel the fabric, presses the underclothes on her, makes her handle the hairbrush and the furs. It is a seduction conducted through the body of a corpse, unmistakably erotic and faintly unbearable, and du Maurier writes it without a single word that a 1938 lending library could have objected to. The charge is all in the touching of things.

Du Maurier's own sexuality was complicated, and she described it herself, in her own private framing, in terms of a boy shut in a box. As a young woman she had adopted an alter ego she called "Eric Avon", and she spoke of a masculine self — the boy in the box — kept locked away inside the wife, mother and public persona. Her biographer Margaret Forster, who gained access to du Maurier's candid letters to Ellen Doubleday only at the editing stage of her 1993 life, concluded that the strain of feeling herself "two distinct people" both tortured du Maurier and fuelled her: without the boy in the box, Forster wrote, there would have been nothing. The two great loves that letters and biography point to were both women — Ellen Doubleday, the wife of her American publisher, for whom the feeling was an unconsummated obsession that she poured into the play *September Tide*; and the actress Gertrude Lawrence, who played the lead in that very play, and with whom Forster judged du Maurier probably did have an affair. Du Maurier disliked the plain words for all this — she preferred, drily, to call them "Venetian tendencies" — and handling her honestly means using her own frame, not a modern label she never chose.

Three hundred miles north-east, on a different coast, a different kind of desire came ashore. In late July 1890 Bram Stoker — exhausted from managing the actor Henry Irving's theatrical empire — took a holiday at Mrs Veazey's guesthouse, 6 Royal Crescent, in Whitby, on the Yorkshire coast. He walked the 199 steps up to the ruined abbey, sat in the churchyard of St Mary's, copied names off the tombstones (one, Swales, went straight into the book), and talked to the coastguards about a shipwreck they all still remembered: in October 1885 a Russian schooner named the *Dmitry*, out of Narva with a cargo of silver sand, had been driven ashore below the East Cliff in a gale. On 8 August 1890 he walked into the little lending library on the quayside and asked for an obscure 1820 book by a former British consul, William Wilkinson — *An Account of the Principalities of Wallachia and Moldavia* — and found, in a footnote, a fifteenth-century warlord and a single sentence: "Dracula in the Wallachian language means Devil." He wrote the word down, went back through his notes, and crossed out the name of his villain, "Count Wampyr", everywhere it appeared. Whitby gave him the ship — the *Dmitry* became the *Demeter*, out of Varna, an anagram of Narva — the steps, the churchyard, the storm, and the name. That much is genuinely documented, mostly from Stoker's own working notes, now at the Rosenbach Museum in Philadelphia; the wider claim that Whitby somehow "inspired *Dracula*" whole is overstated, since the vampire and the Transylvanian setting predated the visit.

But the Whitby chapters of *Dracula* (1897) are where the novel's real business — sex — becomes impossible to miss, and it should be described as what it is, because Stoker built it deliberately. It is at Whitby that the *Demeter* strikes the harbour with a dead man lashed to the wheel and a great dog leaps ashore. It is at Whitby that the vampire begins to visit Lucy Westenra — a warm, flirtatious girl who has just wondered aloud, scandalously, why a woman may not marry three men at once — meeting her at night in the clifftop churchyard as she sleepwalks. What follows is one long coded seduction. Lucy, drained and transformed, becomes "voluptuous", her sweetness turned to "voluptuous wantonness"; her fiancé and his friends must drive a stake through her to restore her to decent, dead purity. Earlier, in Transylvania, Jonathan Harker has met the three vampire women, and Stoker lets him say the quiet part almost aloud — a "deliberate voluptuousness which was both thrilling and repulsive", and "a wicked, burning desire that they would kiss me with those red lips." Modern critics read the bites frankly, as penetration and worse. The point for our purposes is simpler: in 1897, when respectable fiction could not describe married desire, a novel about a Transylvanian count could be more explicit about wanting, and about the terror of a woman who wants back, than almost anything else on the shelf. The monster was permission. He still is; every 26 October the town fills with people in black, and the anxiety Stoker smuggled in has become a very good weekend.



## 39. Dylan and Caitlin: The Drink Story

*5 Cwmdonkin Drive, Uplands, Swansea · The Boathouse, Laugharne, Carmarthenshire · 1914–1994*


> **5 Cwmdonkin Drive (Dylan Thomas's birthplace)** — Swansea, Swansea, Wales
> 51.62100° N, 3.96480° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Dylan Thomas Boathouse, Laugharne** — Laugharne, Carmarthenshire, Wales
> 51.77220° N, 4.45620° W · confidence 5/5


> `[PLATE 25 — 5 Cwmdonkin Drive (Dylan Thomas's birthplace) · 51.62100° N, 3.96480° W]`


Read the name of the town backwards and you have the joke, and the whole man, in a single word. When Dylan Thomas came to invent the Welsh seaside town for his "play for voices", he first called it Llareggub — which, spelled out in reverse, is "bugger all", a filthy little schoolboy gag hidden in plain sight at the heart of what became *Under Milk Wood*, and later softened to "Llareggyb" by nervous editors who need not have bothered, since a Welsh speaker pronounces it the same either way. That is the key to Thomas: a poet of enormous, incantatory seriousness who was also, always, a man who would set a rude joke ticking under the floorboards and wait, delighted, for it to go off.

He was born on 27 October 1914 in the front bedroom of 5 Cwmdonkin Drive, a modest Edwardian semi in the Uplands district of Swansea, and lived there for his first twenty-three years. The house matters more than most writers' birthplaces, because of what happened in one small room. In a tiny back bedroom, from a desk overlooking the town and the bay, the teenage Thomas filled notebook after notebook — and by the reckoning of the Dylan Thomas Birthplace and his biographers, wrote roughly two-thirds of the poetry he would ever publish before he was twenty. His father D. J. Thomas, a frustrated, well-read schoolmaster, kept a study downstairs full of books, and it was there, sneaking volumes off the shelves, that the boy taught himself the English poetic tradition he would go on to overload with Welsh music. The house fell into semi-dereliction as a student bedsit and was rescued and restored to its 1914 condition by a local enthusiast, Geoff Haden, opening to visitors in 2008; you can now sleep in the room where the poems were written.

In the spring of 1936, in the Wheatsheaf pub in Fitzrovia, Thomas met Caitlin Macnamara, and the story of the rest of his life begins there. She was a dancer, trained in the free, barefoot Isadora Duncan style, spirited and beautiful and already the lover of the painter Augustus John, who made the introduction. By Caitlin's own account, the drunk young Welshman crossed the room, put his beer-heavy head in her lap, told her she was beautiful and that he loved her and was going to marry her — and meant, apparently, every word. They spent the next several days more or less in bed at a Fitzrovia hotel, the bill charged to Augustus John, and Caitlin described that first lovemaking, tenderly, as shy and child-like. They married on 11 July 1937 at Penzance register office, against his parents' wishes, and in 1938 settled in the small Carmarthenshire town of Laugharne — the "strangest town in Wales", he called it — which became the raw material for Llareggub.

What followed was not a tragedy that happened to Caitlin; it was a marriage two people made together and half-destroyed together, and she was more honest about it than anyone. It was, in her most famous phrase, a life of "raw, red bleeding meat" — passionate, jealous, drunken and physically violent on both sides. She said flatly that theirs was not so much a love story as a drink story, that without the "first-aid of drink" it could never have got onto its feet; the two of them were known as the Two Terrible Children. Both drank ruinously. Both had affairs — his, and then hers in furious retaliation. And Caitlin admitted, in her memoirs *Leftover Life to Kill* (1957) and the posthumous *Double Drink Story* (1997), that she instigated most of the fights and did most of the damage, and that it was extraordinary they had not killed each other outright. It is important to be fair to her, because biographers have not always been — Paul Ferris's 1993 life of her has been called a hatchet job, painting her as egocentric, calculating and to blame for driving her husband to drink. The truth the memoirs give us is harder and more human: a gifted, thwarted woman with literary ambitions and no outlet for them, married to a man whose gift was the one thing in the house that mattered, trapped for years as the mother of three children at the Boathouse — the beautiful cliff-ledge house above the Taf estuary that a patron, Margaret Taylor, bought for them in 1949 — with no running water and no way out.

The Boathouse, and the little writing shed on the cliff above it where Thomas worked among his crumpled drafts, is now the Dylan Thomas Boathouse, run by Carmarthenshire County Council. The tidy story is that he wrote *Under Milk Wood* there. He mostly did not — the research of David N. Thomas (first published in the *New Welsh Review*, spring 2001) established that fewer than three hundred lines of the play were written at Laugharne — but he wrote much of his greatest late poetry looking out at that estuary shore, and the bawdiness of *Under Milk Wood* is the town's own: Polly Garter singing of all her dead lovers, the draper Mr Waldo working through wives and scandals, Mrs Ogmore-Pritchard bullying her two dead husbands from beyond the grave. It is a dirty, tender, entirely unembarrassed portrait of a community's desires, written by a man who found sex funny and holy in about equal measure.

It ended in New York. On his fourth American reading tour, drinking hard and gravely ill, Thomas collapsed and fell into a coma; he died on 9 November 1953, aged thirty-nine. Caitlin flew out and arrived, by her own admission, "stinkin' drunk"; the scene at St Vincent's Hospital became legend — that she raged, that she fought the staff and bit an attendant, that she was restrained in a straitjacket and briefly committed. She said she could not remember the cruellest words later attributed to her. He was brought home and buried at Laugharne under a plain white cross. Caitlin's widowhood lasted forty years and most of it was spent in Italy, where she found a lasting partner, Giuseppe Fazio, eleven years her junior, gave birth to a fourth child in 1963 at the age of forty-nine, and eventually, in her sixties, stopped drinking. She died in Catania, Sicily, on 31 July 1994, and was brought back across Europe to lie beside the man she had loved and fought for fifteen years — under the same white cross, facing, at last, the water he wrote about.



## 40. Doomed on the Page: The Wheelbarrow and the Love-Potion

*Stonehenge, Wiltshire · Tintagel, Cornwall · 1170s–1912*


> **Stonehenge (Tess of the d'Urbervilles finale)** — Amesbury, Wiltshire, England
> 51.17890° N, 1.82620° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Tintagel Castle (Tristan & Iseult)** — Tintagel, Cornwall, England
> 50.66806° N, 4.75917° W · confidence 5/5


Here is the most perfect illustration of Victorian squeamishness in the whole of English literature, and it is funny before it is anything else. When Thomas Hardy tried to get *Tess of the d'Urbervilles* into print, the magazines recoiled — one grandee complained the manuscript had "rather too much succulence" — and *The Graphic* would only serialise it in 1891 if he cleaned it up. So Hardy, with what he called "cynical amusement", set about bowdlerising his own masterpiece: he cut the seduction, cut Tess's baby, and — this is the immortal detail — rewrote a scene in which Angel Clare carries a group of dairymaids across a flooded lane in his arms, so that instead he trundles them across, one by one, in a wheelbarrow. A man carrying a woman was too much for the Victorian reader. A man pushing four women through the mud in a barrow was apparently fine. The whole absurd machinery of nineteenth-century censorship is there in that single substituted wheelbarrow, and it is worth enjoying, because it is the batch's gift.

But the reason the censorship matters is deadly serious, and it goes to the heart of what the novel is. *Tess of the d'Urbervilles* carries the subtitle *A Pure Woman Faithfully Presented*, and that subtitle was a deliberate provocation that scandalised reviewers, because the plot turns on Tess being sexually violated — the scene at the Chase, which Hardy wrote to be ambiguous between rape and seduction, and which scholars have argued about ever since. Hardy's point, the thing he was willing to be called immoral for, is that Tess remains pure regardless: that a woman's worth is not destroyed by what is done to her, and that a society which says otherwise is the guilty party. The violation must be treated as exactly that, a violation, with no gloss of romance on it; and the killing that comes much later — Tess stabbing her seducer, the blood spreading through a boarding-house ceiling — is consequence, not spectacle. Hardy keeps the murder itself offstage. What he insists we look at is the injustice.

And the novel's final movement is one of the strangest and most beautiful endings in English fiction. After the killing, Tess and Angel have a few days of fugitive happiness, and then, walking north by night, they come upon Stonehenge looming out of the dark. Tess, exhausted, lies down to sleep on one of the great flat stones — an altar, Hardy is careful to call it, a place of ancient sacrifice — and says she is "almost glad" it is ending. "One of my mother's people was a shepherd hereabout," she tells Angel; "and you used to say at Talbothays that I was a heathen. So now I am at home." At dawn the police close quietly in around the stones. She wakes and says, "I am ready." The oldest monument in England becomes the altar on which a "pure woman" is delivered up to a morality older and crueller than Christianity, and Hardy's famous, bitter closing line — that "the President of the Immortals… had ended his sport with Tess" — makes the whole cosmos complicit.

Hardy knew about love that arrives too late and love that outlives its object, because he lived it. His first marriage, to Emma Gifford, had curdled into decades of chilly estrangement under the same roof at Max Gate; after her death he found a diary she had kept, its contents said to be a long indictment of her husband, and had it destroyed. And then, undone by grief and remorse, the old man wrote for his dead wife the *Poems of 1912–13* — some of the most piercing love poetry in the language, addressed to a woman he had ignored for twenty years and could reach only now that she was a ghost. It is the exact pattern this whole chapter has been tracing: the greatest erotic writing coming from the thwarted, the estranged, the too-late.

For a love that was doomed from the very first draft, though, you must go to Cornwall, to the black cliffs of Tintagel — and here we have to separate the story from the stones, because almost everything romantic about the place is medieval invention layered onto a real but entirely un-Arthurian site. The legend is Tristan and Iseult, and it is one of the foundational adulterous love stories of Europe, older than or parallel to Lancelot and Guinevere, whose tale it partly shaped. Tristan, a knight of Cornwall, is sent to fetch the Irish princess Iseult to marry his uncle, King Mark; on the voyage the two of them drink, by mistake, a love-potion meant for the wedding night, and are bound helplessly together in an adulterous passion that destroys them all. It reaches us chiefly through the twelfth-century poems of Béroul and of Thomas of Britain, and through the great early-thirteenth-century German version by Gottfried von Strassburg — a story built, from the start, on desire that overrides law, loyalty and sense.

There may even be a real grave. A weathered sixth-century standing stone near Fowey, the Tristan Stone, carries a Latin inscription that reads, in the usual interpretation, DRUSTANUS HIC IACIT CUNOMORI FILIUS — "Drustanus lies here, son of Cunomorus" — and since "Drustanus" is an early form of Tristan, and a King Mark of Cornwall is elsewhere given the name Cunomorus, romantics have long wanted this to be Tristan's memorial. It is a genuine sixth-century monument and a genuine tantalising coincidence; it is not proof of anything, and an earlier antiquarian read the worn letters differently.

As for Tintagel Castle itself, the truth is better than the legend. The dramatic ruins on the headland are thirteenth-century, built in the 1230s by Richard, Earl of Cornwall — and here is the delicious inversion — apparently *because* of the Arthurian legend, not the other way round. Geoffrey of Monmouth had named Tintagel, around 1136, as the place where King Arthur was conceived; the site had no military value whatsoever; Richard, one of the richest men in Europe, seems to have swapped good manors for this useless, spectacular rock purely to buy himself a share of the myth. But underneath the medieval fantasy is something real and remarkable: excavations, including major digs in 2016–17 by the Cornwall Archaeological Unit, have shown that in the fifth to seventh centuries Tintagel was a wealthy, high-status settlement — very likely a seasonal seat of the kings of Dumnonia — trading directly with the Mediterranean, its ground littered with fine pottery and glass from North Africa and the eastern Mediterranean. More of this imported material has been found at Tintagel than at any other site in Britain; the 2016 season alone turned up more than two hundred pieces of Mediterranean pottery. The place was genuinely a seat of Cornish kings; it simply had nothing to do with a round table.

English Heritage now manages the headland, and its efforts to bring the legend alive have not always gone down well. A face of Merlin carved into the rock beneath the island, and then in 2016 an eight-foot bronze figure called *Gallos* — Cornish for "power" — a hooded, crowned, sword-holding spectre installed on the summit, drew accusations from Cornish nationalists and some historians of "Disneyfying" a profound site. English Heritage insists *Gallos* represents not King Arthur specifically but the whole layered history of the place, its real Dark Age kings included. It is, in its way, the perfect Tintagel row: an argument about invented tradition, staged on a rock that was mythologised once already, eight hundred years ago, by an earl who wanted a piece of a story that a Welsh cleric had made up a century before that. The desire came first. The stones came later, to match it. That is how the whole erotic map of Britain tends to work.



\newpage

# Chapter 6 · Hidden in Plain Sight

*Queer Britain, from the molly houses to the Caravan Club*


> `[PLATE 26 — chapter frontispiece]`


The queer past reaches us in exactly two forms. There is what people encrypted themselves, and there is what the state wrote down while trying to destroy them. Almost everything else was burned.

Anne Lister is the first kind, and she is very nearly unique. An estimated five million words of diary, roughly a sixth in a code of Greek letters and algebra she devised herself, recording her lovers, her techniques and her own vocabulary — a "kiss" for an orgasm, a cross in the margin for masturbation. In plain hand she wrote that she loved and only loved the fairer sex. When her descendant cracked the cipher in the 1890s, a friend advised him to burn the diaries; he hid them behind the panelling instead, which is the single luckiest act of cowardice in this book.

Mother Clap's molly house is the second kind. We can describe the inside of that Holborn establishment — forty or fifty men drinking, dancing, taking female names, going in couples into the room they called the marrying room — because a constable infiltrated it in 1725 and swore to what he saw, and because his deposition was printed at the Old Bailey before three of the men he described were hanged at Tyburn on 9 May 1726. The Caravan Club at 81 Endell Street survives in the same way: police surveillance logs, seized membership cards, a torn-up love letter recovered from under a divan, all filed at the National Archives after the raid of August 1934. The evidence exists because somebody was compiling a prosecution.

Between those two poles lies everything ambiguous, and this chapter is careful there. The Ladies of Llangollen lived together for fifty years, dressed alike, called each other beloved, and were furious when a newspaper implied what everyone suspected. Whether they were lovers is unknowable, and the "romantic friendship" framing they insisted on was both a genuine category of their age and, conveniently, a defence. Anne Lister herself went to Plas Newydd in 1822 specifically to find out, and came away doubting it was platonic — one queer woman's assessment of another couple, which is the best evidence anyone has and is still only a guess.

The law behaves differently on either side of the ledger, and the asymmetry is instructive. Sex between men was capital until 1861 and remained criminal until partial decriminalisation in England and Wales in 1967, and for over a decade longer in Scotland and Northern Ireland; sex between women was never criminalised at all — not because Queen Victoria refused to believe in it, which is a myth with no basis, but because male legislators chose deliberate silence, partly for fear of giving respectable women ideas. The practical effect was that lesbian spaces were policed by licensing and social pressure rather than by the dock, which is why the Gateways survived behind a green door on the King's Road for half a century while molly houses were raided.

The families did the rest of the damage. The sixth Marquess of Anglesey destroyed his cousin's papers, which is why the Dancing Marquess can only be inferred. Forster wrote Maurice in 1913 and refused to publish it for the rest of his life, because it ended happily.



## 41. I Know My Own Heart: Anne Lister

*Shibden Hall, Halifax, West Yorkshire · and Holy Trinity Church, Goodramgate, York · 1791–1840*


> **Shibden Hall (Anne Lister & Ann Walker)** — Halifax, West Yorkshire, England
> 53.72400° N, 1.83700° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Holy Trinity Church, Goodramgate ('birthplace of lesbian marriage')** — York, North Yorkshire, England
> 53.96100° N, 1.08100° W · confidence 5/5


> `[PLATE 27 — Shibden Hall (Anne Lister & Ann Walker) · 53.72400° N, 1.83700° W]`


There is a scrap of paper, and on it, in Anne Lister's small crowded hand, the words "In God is my —", the last word written in a code of Greek letters, algebra and zodiac symbols. That word was "hope". In the 1890s a Halifax antiquarian named John Lister, the last of his line to live at Shibden Hall, and his friend Arthur Burrell, a Bradford schoolmaster, used those four letters to crack the whole cipher. When they had read enough of what it concealed, Burrell advised John Lister to burn the diaries. He could not do it. Instead he hid them behind the panelling at Shibden, where they stayed until after his death in 1933. That single act of cowardice, or love, or both, is why we have the most detailed record of a lesbian life anywhere in the English language.

Anne Lister kept a diary from her teens until the year she died, and by the end it ran to some five million words across twenty-six volumes and nearly eight thousand pages. Roughly a sixth of it is in the code she called her "crypt hand". The plain-hand passages record a busy Tory landowner: coal pits and canal shares, rents and roof repairs, mountains climbed and languages learned. The coded passages record the rest of her — her venereal complaints, her opinions of her neighbours, and above all her sexual life, set down with an anatomical frankness that startled the first modern readers so thoroughly that some assumed the diaries were a hoax until the code was checked against her plain hand.

She did not have the word "lesbian" in the sense we use it. She had better words, her own. On 29 January 1821 she wrote, in plain hand, one of the flattest and bravest self-descriptions in the language: "I love & only love the fairer sex & thus beloved by them in turn, my heart revolts from any other love than theirs." In code she was more particular. A "kiss" was not a kiss but an orgasm — hers or a lover's. "Grubbling" was fingering; she was, the diaries make clear, almost always the one who gave rather than received. "Queer" was her word for the vulva. When she pleasured herself she drew a small cross in the margin and wrote that she had "incurred a cross". These are not euphemisms a modern editor has imposed; they are Lister's, recovered by the historian Helena Whitbread, whose first volume, *I Know My Own Heart*, appeared in 1988 and whose second, *No Priest but Love*, followed in 1993, and who called the diaries what they are — a life lived, and recorded, without apology.

The affairs are a whole social world. There was Eliza Raine, the York schoolgirl with whom she first shared the code, the two of them writing to each other as husband and wife.
