There was Isabella "Tib" Norcliffe, and there was Mariana Belcombe, the great love of her early adulthood, who married a Mr Lawton for security and from whom Lister contracted a venereal infection that she then had to treat, recording the boils and the mercury with the same steady attention she gave the weather. There was Maria Barlow in Paris. And finally, from 1832, there was Ann Walker, a shy and wealthy neighbour whose estate marched conveniently alongside her own, and whom she set out to win with a frankness that reads, at moments, like a business plan and, at others, like devotion.

The town knew what she was, more or less. She dressed always in black, walked like a man, was nicknamed "Gentleman Jack" — not kindly — and did not much care. She ran her estate, opened her own coal pits in the teeth of a male rival, travelled to Paris and the Pyrenees, made the first recorded ascent of the Vignemale in 1838, and died of a fever on 22 September 1840 at Kutaisi, in what is now Georgia, thousands of miles from Shibden, with Ann Walker beside her. Walker had her embalmed body carried the whole way home.

Shibden Hall still stands in its wooded valley outside Halifax, a rambling timber-framed house that Lister loved and improved and drew mortgages against. It is a museum now, run by Calderdale, and since the BBC drama *Gentleman Jack* it receives more visitors in a day than it once did in weeks. The diaries themselves are at the West Yorkshire Archive Service, and in 2011 UNESCO added them to its UK Memory of the World Register, the citation singling out not the coal pits but the "comprehensive and painfully honest account of lesbian life". Every page has been digitised; an army of volunteer transcribers is still working through the plain hand, which turns out to be harder to read than the code.

Twenty-two miles away, in York, is the second place. Holy Trinity, Goodramgate, is a small medieval church reached down an alley off a shopping street, easy to walk past, full of box pews and old glass. On Easter Sunday, 30 March 1834, Anne Lister and Ann Walker knelt here and took the sacrament together. They had already exchanged rings; Lister's diary records that she prayed their union might be happy. Neither the Church of England nor the law had any category for what they were doing, and they knew it. They treated it as a marriage anyway. It is now widely called the first lesbian marriage in Britain, which is a modern honour laid on a private act, but the private act was real and Lister recorded it plainly.

In July 2018 York Civic Trust put up a plaque outside the church — the country's first permanent rainbow plaque. It described Lister as a "Gender-nonconforming entrepreneur" and did not use the word lesbian. The omission caused a national row. A petition started by the campaigner Julie Furlong gathered more than 2,500 signatures, arguing, reasonably, that Lister was indeed gender-nonconforming all her life and was also, unmistakably and in her own words, a woman who loved only women. In February 2019 the Trust replaced it. The new plaque calls her, correctly, "Lesbian and Diarist", and Helena Whitbread gave a talk to mark the unveiling. The Trust also quietly fixed the rainbow border, which had first gone up upside down.

It is worth holding the shape of the whole thing in mind. A woman writes down her desire in a code so that it will survive; a man decodes it, is appalled, and hides it rather than burns it; historians a century later read past the coal accounts to the crosses in the margin; and a plaque committee in our own decade has to be argued into using the right word. The record survived, in other words, only because Lister encrypted it and then someone lost his nerve at the crucial moment. She had wondered, in that same 1834 spring, whether she and Walker could ever live openly. Twelve years earlier she had asked exactly that question of another couple, two elderly Irishwomen in a Gothic cottage in North Wales, and driven the length of the country to find out.



## 42. Delicious Retirement: The Ladies of Llangollen

*Plas Newydd and St Collen's Church, Llangollen, Denbighshire · 1778–1831*


> **Plas Newydd (the Ladies of Llangollen)** — Llangollen, Denbighshire, Wales
> 52.96710° N, 3.16550° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **The Ladies of Llangollen's Tomb** — Llangollen, Denbighshire, Wales
> 52.97170° N, 3.16750° W · confidence 4/5


The woman she had come the length of the country to interrogate was, in the end, not much help. When Anne Lister called at Plas Newydd in July 1822 she was on a private mission: she wanted to know whether two women really could build a life together, and whether the most famous such pair in Britain were lovers. She met only Sarah Ponsonby, then in her sixties, and tried to establish the point obliquely, asking whether the Ladies were "classical" — code, in Lister's world, for Sapphic. Ponsonby gave nothing away. But Lister came away certain enough, and confided it to her diary and to her own lover Mariana: "I cannot help thinking that surely it was not platonic. Heaven forgive me, but I look within myself & doubt." One queer woman had assessed another couple and reached a verdict. It is the sharpest single piece of evidence we have, and it is still only a guess.

Rewind forty-four years, to a spring night in 1778 in County Kilkenny. Lady Eleanor Butler, daughter of a grand and impoverished Catholic family and by then approaching forty, and Sarah Ponsonby, an orphaned Protestant of twenty-three fending off the unwanted attentions of her guardian, climbed out into the dark and ran. Ponsonby, the story goes, was in men's clothes and carrying a pistol. They were caught and dragged back. Butler was threatened with a French convent; Ponsonby hid her, at one point, in a bedroom while a loyal servant named Mary Carryll smuggled in food. The families raged, then wearied, then relented, and settled small allowances on the pair to be rid of the scandal. In May 1778 the two women left Ireland for good, with Carryll, and after wandering North Wales for some weeks they leased a cottage above the little town of Llangollen. They renamed it Plas Newydd, and there they stayed for half a century.

What they made of it was extraordinary and slightly mad. They turned a plain stone cottage into a black-and-white Gothic fantasia, encrusting it with carved oak salvaged from churches, fitting pointed arches and stained glass, setting a medieval font in the garden. They read in French, Italian and Spanish, kept a library, laid out walks, and dressed alike in dark riding habits with their hair powdered and cropped short, so that visitors never quite knew what to call the effect. They referred to their life as "delicious retirement" — and then proceeded to be anything but retiring, receiving a stream of the age's most famous people. Wordsworth came, and wrote a not very good sonnet. Byron, Shelley, Walter Scott, the Duke of Wellington, Josiah Wedgwood, Madame de Genlis, the young De Quincey — all made the pilgrimage up the hill. Queen Charlotte took an interest and helped secure them a royal pension in 1788.

The question of what they were to each other was raised in their own lifetime, and they hated it. In July 1790 an article headed "Extraordinary Female Affection" appeared in the *St James's Chronicle* and was reprinted in the *General Evening Post*. It praised the cottage and its occupants but then drew a portrait that any reader could decode: Miss Butler "tall and masculine", always in a riding habit, hanging up her hat "with the air of a sportsman"; Miss Ponsonby "polite and effeminate, fair and beautiful". This was the standard eighteenth-century sketch of a mannish woman and her feminine companion, and the Ladies were furious enough to consider legal action. In private, the diarist Hester Thrale — who counted them as friends — called them, in her own journal, "damned Sapphists", and reckoned no woman would spend a night at Plas Newydd without a man for propriety. Byron, imagining an ideal love with a Cambridge chorister, listed the Ladies among history's great couples he hoped to outshine.

And yet. The honest answer to the obvious question is that we do not know, and cannot. No diary of theirs records a "kiss" in Lister's sense; no letter confesses. They shared a bed, called each other "my Beloved" and "the darling of my heart", named a dog Sappho, and lived as a married couple in every visible respect for fifty years. They also presented themselves, fiercely, as models of chaste and elevated "romantic friendship" — a category their era permitted to women, and one that functioned, whether they intended it or not, as a defence. To insist they were lovers is to project; to insist they were chaste is to project the other way, and to do the era's own respectability work for it. What is certain is the devotion, the half-century, and the shared grave.

Eleanor Butler died in 1829, in her ninetieth year and by then nearly blind. Sarah Ponsonby followed in December 1831. They are buried together at St Collen's Church in Llangollen under a large three-sided monument, each face given to one of the three women who had run away together and stayed together. For the third face belongs to Mary Carryll, the servant who had hidden Eleanor in a bedroom in 1778, who kept house for them without wages for over thirty years, who saved and bought land and left it to them, and who died in 1809, twenty years before either of the women she served. They had promised she would lie with them, and she does. Whatever the Ladies of Llangollen were to each other, they were, all three, a household — and it is the maid, arriving first, who has waited longest under the stone.



## 43. The Marrying Room: Mother Clap's

*Field Lane, Holborn, London · c.1724–1726*


> **Mother Clap's Molly House** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.51900° N, 0.10700° W · confidence 2/5


> `[PLATE 28 — Mother Clap's Molly House · 51.51900° N, 0.10700° W]`


Go looking for Mother Clap's molly house and you will find a viaduct. Field Lane, the narrow Holborn street where it stood between an archway and the Bunch o' Grapes tavern, was swallowed whole in the 1860s by the building of Holborn Viaduct, and there is nothing to see — no plaque, no fragment, no doorway. This is worth saying plainly at the outset, because the erasure is part of the story. The men who drank and danced and paired off in Margaret Clap's rooms have no monument. What they have instead is a set of trial records, gathered by the people who wanted them dead.

A molly house was a tavern, coffee house or private room where men who desired men could meet — to drink, to talk, to dance, to take female names and personae, and to have sex. "Molly" was the word gay men of the period used among themselves. There had been such places in London since about 1700, and the standard modern study, Rictor Norton's *Mother Clap's Molly House* of 1992, argues persuasively that most were not brothels but something warmer: social clubs, meeting places, somewhere to be among your own. Margaret Clap's, in Field Lane, was the most celebrated of them in the mid-1720s. It was owned in her husband's name but was hers to run, and on a Sunday night it might hold forty or fifty men. There was a fire, there was drink, and, by the account of one hostile witness, there was a bed in every room.

We know what went on there in humiliating detail because a constable named Samuel Stevens infiltrated the place in the autumn of 1725, pretending to be the "husband" of a molly turned informer, and later swore to what he had seen. His deposition, read out at the Old Bailey at Gabriel Lawrence's trial in April 1726 and preserved in the printed trial reports, is the fullest description we have of the inside of such a house — and it is, of course, a prosecution document, written to hang people. Read it that way and it becomes something the constable never intended: a picture of a party. "I found between 40 and 50 Men making Love to one another," Stevens deposed. "Sometimes they would sit on one another's Laps, kissing in a lewd Manner... Then they would get up, Dance and make Curtsies, and mimick the voices of Women. O, Fie, Sir! — Pray, Sir. — Dear Sir. Lord, how can you serve me so!... Then they'd hug, and play, and toy, and go out by Couples into another Room on the same Floor, to be marry'd, as they call'd it." That other room, the mollies called the marrying room. A doorkeeper named Eccleston stood guard so that the couples inside were not disturbed.

The mollies had their own rituals and their own theatre, though the record here needs careful handling. The most famous is the mock "lying-in", in which a man mimed the whole performance of childbirth and was "delivered" of a wooden doll, which was then mock-christened. That scene is vivid and real, but it comes from a satire — Ned Ward's account of a "Mollies Club" published in 1709, well before Clap's existed, describing an unnamed City tavern where a "Sodomitical" brother, his belly cushioned up and dressed in a woman's nightgown, groaned to be delivered of "a joynted Babie". Ward wrote to titillate and moralise, not to bear witness; but he did not invent the ritual whole, for versions of it recur across the century, and men were caught performing it as late as the 1810s. The mock marriage is better attested — Stevens's "to be marry'd, as they call'd it" is trial evidence — and at a separate molly house, George Whittle's Royal Oak off Pall Mall, a room was reportedly called "the Chapel". So: the marrying room at Clap's is court record; the wooden baby is Ned Ward's satire; and it is worth keeping them apart, because the whole difficulty of this history is that almost everything we know was written down either to mock these men or to kill them.

In February 1726 the raids came. They were driven by the Societies for the Reformation of Manners, self-appointed moral vigilantes who paid informers and had made it their business to rid London of sodomites, prostitutes and Sabbath-breakers. Clap's had been watched for two years; the informing was set off, it seems, by a molly named Mark Partridge, outed by a lover and then pressured into betraying his friends. On a Sunday night the constables blocked the exits and took some forty men to Newgate. None had been caught in the act of sodomy — a few had their breeches unbuttoned — and most were eventually released for want of evidence. But the machine, once started, needed victims. On 9 May 1726 three men were hanged together at Tyburn on the triangular gallows, each from his own beam: Gabriel Lawrence, a forty-three-year-old milkman and widowed father; William Griffin, a furniture upholsterer of the same age who had lodged at Clap's; and Thomas Wright, who kept his own molly house. They were hanged, essentially, on the word of a single turncoat, Thomas Newton, who had agreed to testify in exchange for his own skin. The crowd was large; the mollies shared the day with a famous husband-murderer, and a spectators' scaffold collapsed, killing and maiming onlookers who had come to watch lovers die.

Margaret Clap herself was tried in July 1726 for keeping a disorderly house. She protested, reasonably enough, "I am a Woman, and therefore it cannot be thought that I would ever be concern'd in such Practices." It did her no good. She was sentenced to stand in the pillory at Smithfield, to pay a fine and to serve two years' imprisonment. The pillory was not a mild punishment; a crowd could maim or kill a person pinned in it, and it is often said she died of her injuries, though the record simply loses her after the sentence. That is where Mother Clap vanishes — not into legend, but into the ordinary silence that closes over people the state has finished using.

The joke of the whole episode, if there is one, is entirely at the expense of the Societies for the Reformation of Manners, who spent two years and several lives to close one coffee house and succeeded only in creating, in the trial papers, the richest surviving record of the very subculture they meant to erase. They wrote it all down so that they could destroy it, and by writing it down they preserved it. Two hundred years later, and a mile to the west, the Metropolitan Police would do exactly the same thing to a basement club in Endell Street — and once again the surveillance file would outlast the persecution and become the memorial.



## 44. The Love That Dare Not: Wilde's Arc

*Portora Royal School, Enniskillen, County Fermanagh · Magdalen College, Oxford · 34 Tite Street, Chelsea · and the Cadogan Hotel, Sloane Street, London · 1864–1900*


> **Portora Royal School (Oscar Wilde's schooling)** — Enniskillen, County Fermanagh, Northern Ireland
> 54.35100° N, 7.65500° W · confidence 3/5
>
> **Magdalen College, Oxford (Wilde's aesthetic years)** — Oxford, Oxfordshire, England
> 51.75222° N, 1.24694° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Oscar Wilde's house, 34 Tite Street** — London, Greater London, England

>

> 51.49700° N, 0.15900° W · confidence 5/5


On a classroom windowsill at Portora Royal School, in Enniskillen, County Fermanagh, a boy once scratched his initials — O.W. In 1895, when that boy had become the most famous playwright in London and then, within a season, the most notorious convict in England, a headmaster took the trouble to have the two letters scraped away. That small act of erasure — the effort of it, in a large school scrimshawed with the graffiti of generations of bored boys — tells you almost everything about how the age chose to remember Oscar Wilde: by unremembering him. His name was painted out of the school's honours board too. It went back up, decades later, and now, the story goes, shines brighter than the names around it because the gilt is newer. Whether the exact detail is legend or fact scarcely matters; the shape of it is true. They tried to remove him and could not.

Wilde was at Portora from 1864 to 1871, a bookish boy nicknamed "Grey Crow" who was not, by his own later account, happy there and preferred to remember only a year of it. From Enniskillen he went to Trinity College Dublin and then, in October 1874, to Magdalen College, Oxford, on a demyship — a scholarship peculiar to the college. Oxford made him. He took a double first in classics, won the Newdigate Prize in 1878 for his poem *Ravenna*, and fell under the two contrary influences that would pull at him for the rest of his life: John Ruskin, who believed beauty carried a moral duty, and Walter Pater, whose *Studies in the History of the Renaissance* urged instead a life lived for intense sensation, burning always with a hard, gemlike flame. Wilde took the flame. He grew his hair, decorated his rooms, cultivated the pose of the aesthete, and left Oxford already half-famous for being himself.

In 1884 he married Constance Lloyd, and the following January the couple moved into a narrow house at 16 Tite Street, Chelsea — now number 34 — in the artists' quarter by the river. E. W. Godwin decorated the interior, much of it startling white, and here Wilde spent his ten years of fame. Both his sons were born in the house; here he wrote almost everything that matters — *The Happy Prince*, *The Picture of Dorian Gray*, the great comedies. And here the double life took hold. In 1886 he began an affair with Robert Ross, then seventeen and probably the first of his male lovers, later the most loyal of his friends and his literary executor. In 1891 he met Lord Alfred Douglas — "Bosie" — a beautiful, spoilt, twenty-one-year-old aristocrat, and the affair that would destroy him began.

Here the honest telling has to slow down. Douglas introduced Wilde to a world of young working-class men — some procured through Alfred Taylor, who kept a house that functioned as an introducing-place — whom Wilde took to hotel rooms and dinners and showered with gifts and money. Several were teenagers of seventeen or eighteen; several were "renters", who traded sex for cash; the class gulf between them and Wilde was enormous, and money changed hands. None of this fits the martyr's outline neatly, and it should not be sanded down. But two things must be held alongside it. First, almost everything we "know" of these encounters comes from a criminal prosecution that set out to destroy Wilde and paid and pressured its witnesses accordingly, so the record is skewed by the same hostility that shaped the molly trials a century and a half before. Second, the men were consenting adults or near-adults under the law of their day, and what was on trial was not exploitation but desire between men as such. To sanitise Wilde is dishonest; to join his prosecutors is worse.

The catastrophe was of Wilde's own making, at least in its timing. Bosie's father, the Marquess of Queensberry — a violent, half-mad aristocrat who had given the world the rules of boxing — left a card at Wilde's club accusing him of "posing somdomite", the misspelling now more famous than anything Queensberry did on purpose. Against all advice, Wilde sued him for criminal libel. It was a disaster. Queensberry's defence had assembled a file of witnesses; under cross-examination by Edward Carson the case collapsed, and on the morning of 5 April 1895 Wilde withdrew it. A warrant for his own arrest followed within hours.

He spent that day not fleeing but waiting, in the Cadogan Hotel on Sloane Street, in a room now generally given as 118, drinking hock and seltzer with Ross and Reggie Turner while friends begged him to catch the boat train to France. He would not go. At about twenty past six in the evening two detectives came up the stairs and took him away. The exact date is worth pinning, because sources differ: the arrest is variously dated 5 and 6 April, and the confusion is real — the libel case collapsed on the morning of the 5th and the arrest happened that same evening, though many accounts, including the hotel's own, give the 6th. John Betjeman later made the scene into one of his best-loved poems, all Nottingham lace and the plainclothes men at the door; it is in copyright, so take it as recommended rather than quoted, but its picture of Wilde too drunk and too proud to run is close to the truth.

The trials that followed were held under Section 11 of the Criminal Law Amendment Act 1885 — the Labouchere Amendment, which criminalised "gross indecency" between men without troubling to define it, and which lawyers rightly nicknamed the blackmailer's charter. The first jury could not agree; the Crown tried again. On 25 May 1895 Wilde was convicted and sentenced to two years' hard labour, the maximum, the judge complaining it was too little. He served it in Pentonville, Wandsworth and finally Reading Gaol, where the regime broke his health and where, given paper at last, he wrote the long, wounded letter to Douglas that Ross would publish, cut down, as *De Profundis*. Released in 1897, bankrupt and disgraced, he went into exile in France under an assumed name, wrote *The Ballad of Reading Gaol*, and died in a cheap Paris hotel on 30 November 1900, aged forty-six, of meningitis following an ear infection. Robbie Ross was at the bedside. Bosie was chief mourner at the funeral.

Tite Street carries a plaque. The Cadogan sells a room and a cocktail on the strength of the arrest. Portora put the name back on the board. And in 2017, under the provisions of the Policing and Crime Act — the "Alan Turing law" — Wilde was among tens of thousands of men pardoned posthumously for convictions that would not be crimes today. It was the right thing, and it was also, for Wilde, a piece of paper issued to a man a hundred and seventeen years dead, for a crime that was never a crime, by the successors of the state that had jailed him. It changed nothing and it corrected nothing that could still be felt. The scraped windowsill at Portora had already been proved wrong the moment the name went back up.



## 45. All-Night Gaiety: The Caravan Club

*81 Endell Street, Covent Garden, London · July–August 1934*


> **The Caravan Club, 81 Endell Street** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.51520° N, 0.12490° W · confidence 4/5


> `[PLATE 29 — The Caravan Club, 81 Endell Street · 51.51520° N, 0.12490° W]`


The door is still there. Walk east from Cambridge Circus into Endell Street and, on a building that now houses ordinary offices and flats, you can find number 81 — a dark doorway with a row of bells and a small grille, the sort of entrance you would pass a hundred times without a glance. Down the stairs behind it, for six weeks in the summer of 1934, was the most extraordinary queer social space in interwar London, and the reason we can describe what it looked like at all is that the Metropolitan Police wrote it all down before they destroyed it.

The Caravan Club opened on 14 July 1934 in the basement of 81 Endell Street, and advertised itself with cheerful nerve as "London's Greatest Bohemian Rendezvous, said to be the most unconventional spot in town", promising "all-night gaiety" and "dancing to Charlie", the club's musician. "Bohemian rendezvous" was a code any knowing reader could break: it meant a place where men could dance with men and women with women. It was run by Jack Neave — a former escapologist and strongman known as "Iron Foot Jack" for the metal brace that lengthened his short leg, and who styled himself King of the Bohemians — and financed by a young man named William Reynolds, called Billy, who had a string of convictions and a recent inheritance to spend. They rented the basement, furnished it fast and cheap with divans and cushions and draped fabric in a vaguely oriental style, and threw open the doors.

The response was immediate. In six weeks the Caravan enrolled 445 members and drew, by the police count, 2,004 visitors. Membership cost a shilling, or one and six on the door. And from the start it was watched. Neighbours and Holborn Borough Council complained; one anonymous handwritten note, now in the file at The National Archives, denounced the place as an "absolute sink of iniquity... frequented by sexual perverts, lesbians and sodomites", and begged the authorities to act. They already were. Plainclothes officers had been observing the club from an unused part of the Shaftesbury Theatre and an old school building nearby, keeping hour-by-hour logs of who came and went and straining to overhear anything that would let them call the place a "disorderly house" in law. It is from these surveillance reports, and the ones written after the raid, that we know the Caravan from the inside.

They are remarkable documents, and like Samuel Stevens's deposition two centuries before, they record with hostile precision the very life they meant to end. The officer in charge of the raid, Divisional Detective Inspector Clarence Campion, described a small dance floor so crowded his men could barely take their positions unnoticed, men dancing together, couples pressed close, make-up and plucked eyebrows and effeminate dress noted down as evidence. When he stopped the music he announced that the police were there and that everyone present was aiding and abetting a house kept "in a manner likely to corrupt morals". The file preserves individual voices too. Torn up under a divan, with a powder puff, the police found a love letter from a member who signed himself Cyril — Cyril Coeur de Leon — which included the extraordinary, matter-of-fact line, "I have only been queer since I came to London." And when the raid began, Cyril, according to Campion's own report, approached the inspector and asked, coolly, whether he might have one of the officer's "nice boys" to take home. The prosecution kept his letter to damn him; instead it kept him.

The raid came in the early hours of 25 August 1934. A total of 103 people were arrested and taken to Bow Street. Crowds gathered outside the magistrates' court to watch the accused arrive. The members faced charges of aiding and abetting; the proprietors, Neave and Reynolds, faced the graver charge of keeping a disorderly house. The trial ended in late October 1934. Both men were convicted and sentenced to hard labour — Reynolds to twelve months, Neave to twenty. The *Daily Express* reporter noted that as Neave was taken down to the cells, the heavy clamp of his iron boot could be heard on the stairs.

And there the Caravan should have vanished, as Mother Clap's vanished, as Field Lane vanished under its viaduct. Instead its persecution became its preservation. The police files — catalogued at The National Archives as MEPO 3/758 and DPP 2/224, and including the surveillance logs, the seized membership cards, Cyril's retyped love letter, a sketch plan of the basement and photographs of the empty, draped interior taken during the raid — turn out to be the fullest surviving record of an interwar queer social space anywhere in Britain. Historic England has made the file a centrepiece of its Pride of Place and LGBTQ heritage work; the National Trust and The National Archives recreated the Caravan, cocktails and accordion and all, in 2017, to mark fifty years since the partial decriminalisation of homosexuality. We can throw that party again only because a detective inspector once tried to end it and filed his report.

It is exactly the pattern of the molly houses, two centuries and a mile apart. In 1726 the constables of the Societies for the Reformation of Manners watched Mother Clap's, gathered their evidence, and hanged three men — and left behind, without meaning to, the richest picture we have of the eighteenth-century molly subculture. In 1934 the Metropolitan Police watched the Caravan, gathered their evidence, and jailed two men — and left behind the richest picture we have of the interwar one. The persecutors kept the only albums. Everything the lovers themselves might have written was torn up under a divan, or hidden behind panelling, or encrypted, or never set down at all. What the state wrote down to destroy them is, too often, all that is left to remember them by — and it is, against every intention of the men who compiled it, enough.



## 46. Julian, in a Bandage

*Knole & Sissinghurst Castle · Kent, England · 1892–1962*


> **Knole (Vita's lost inheritance)** — Sevenoaks, Kent, England
> 51.26900° N, 0.20500° E · confidence 5/5
>
> **Sissinghurst Castle (Vita Sackville-West)** — Cranbrook, Kent, England
> 51.11700° N, 0.58200° E · confidence 5/5


The document was in a Gladstone bag, locked, in the tower room at Sissinghurst, and it had been there for the best part of forty years. When Vita Sackville-West died in 1962 her younger son, Nigel Nicolson, found it and read what his mother had written in 1920, when she was twenty-eight and had just come back from the edge of throwing her whole life away. He sat on the discovery for a decade. Then, in 1973, he published it, wrapped in three chapters of his own, as *Portrait of a Marriage* — one of the most extraordinary sexual confessions in the English language, and certainly the frankest ever written by a woman of her class about loving other women.

Start with the wound that never healed, because Vita never stopped pressing it. She was born at Knole, in Kent, a house so vast it was said to have a room for every day of the year, held by the Sackvilles since Elizabeth I gave it to a cousin in 1566. Vita grew up there, the only child, showing visitors round it, loving it the way other people love a person. And she could not have it. The estate was entailed to the male line; when her father died in 1928 it went to her uncle and then her cousin, and Vita — who by every measure of feeling was Knole's true heir — got nothing but the ache. She put it exactly once, in a letter, and never bettered it: her exile from Knole, she wrote, was like having had a liaison for years with a beautiful woman who, from force of circumstances, never wholly belonged to her. She turned her disinheritance into a metaphor for lesbian longing. Only Vita would have done that.

In 1913 she married Harold Nicolson, a diplomat, in the chapel at Knole. Both of them were, in the language of the time, homosexual; both knew it; and the marriage they built lasted almost fifty years and outlasted every affair either of them had, which was a great many. It has become fashionable to call it an open marriage, but that undersells the strangeness and the tenderness of it. They wrote to each other nearly every day when apart. They simply did not accept that loving someone else meant they had stopped loving each other. Harold had his young men. Vita had her women. The centre held.

Then came Violet.

Violet Keppel — later, unwillingly, Violet Trefusis — had known Vita since they were children; the daughter of Alice Keppel, who happened to be Edward VII's mistress, so the whole thing ran in a straight line of magnificent unrespectability. In 1918 the friendship became an affair, and the affair became a conflagration. They went to Cornwall, then to France, and it is here that the most astonishing figure in the whole story appears: Julian.

Julian was Vita. In France and in London, Vita dressed as a young man — a wounded soldier home from the war — and walked out with Violet on her arm. The disguise was specific and it was documented, and the best contemporary account of it comes from the critic John Richardson, reviewing the book for the *New York Review of Books* in 1973, drawing directly on Vita's manuscript: she wore khaki, and a khaki bandage round her head that conveniently hid her hair, and in this get-up she escorted Violet up Piccadilly and around the Palais Royal in Paris. "I never felt so free," she wrote. In Monte Carlo the performance was so complete that a French couple tried to interest the handsome young officer in marrying their daughter, and Vita had to improvise some reminiscences of the front to account for her injury. Her own boast was the thing that carries the whole episode: she felt, stepping off the kerb into Piccadilly, that if she met her own mother face to face her mother would not know her. She was never once caught out.

It is worth pausing on how much nerve this took, and how little it had to do with mere disguise. This was a woman discovering, in wartime London and on the Riviera, that she could move through the world as her true, wanted self — free of the drawing room, free of "Mrs Harold Nicolson," free of Knole and its rules — for the length of an evening. The name Julian she loved enough to give it to the hero of her novel *Challenge*, which she wrote with Violet and which her mother had suppressed in Britain as too obvious.

It nearly ended two marriages. In February 1920 the two women were together in Amiens, on the point of leaving their husbands for good, when Harold Nicolson and Violet's husband Denys Trefusis pursued them across the Channel — flying, in a small two-seater aeroplane, in an era when that was itself an act of some derring-do — and there were terrible scenes, and the women were talked home. Vita went back to Harold. Violet, who had married Denys on the explicit condition that the marriage never be consummated, went back to a life she had never wanted, and it is important to say plainly that of the four people in this story Violet was the one who paid most. Vita could be imperious, possessive and cruel; she wrote of the affair in the language of conquest, of having Violet, of not caring. Violet loved harder and had less power, and history, which forgave Vita everything and turned her into a National Trust heroine, has been meaner to her.

There was one more great love, and it was gentler. In 1925 Vita began an affair with Virginia Woolf, and out of it Woolf wrote *Orlando* (1928), the novel whose hero changes sex across four centuries and which is, transparently, a portrait of Vita and of Knole — the house Woolf gave her in fiction because the law would not give it to her in life. Nigel Nicolson called it the longest and most charming love letter in literature, and the original manuscript now lives, with a neatness that would have made Vita weep, at Knole.

And Sissinghurst. In 1930, still raw from losing Knole, Vita and Harold bought a wrecked Elizabethan castle in the Kent Weald — a ruined tower, a moat, some derelict farm buildings, weeds to the horizon. It had once belonged, by marriage, to her own Sackville ancestors, which she felt as a kind of restitution. They made it habitable and then, over thirty years, they made the garden: a series of outdoor rooms divided by hedge and old brick, laid out across barely five acres, which the National Trust reports draws more than 150,000 visitors a year and is the most visited garden in England. Harold drew the bones of it, the sightlines and the walls. Vita filled it, with a planter's ruthlessness and a poet's eye. She wrote in the tower, up a spiral stair, in a room almost nobody was allowed to enter.

She had wanted a house she could not have. She built one instead, room by room, out of hedges — walls of yew for the walls of stone she had lost. The confession stayed in its bag until she was safely dead. Her son let it out, and in doing so did for his mother exactly what she had spent her life refusing to do for herself: he said it out loud.



## 47. Two Quiet Households

*Millthorpe & Brackenburn · Derbyshire and Cumbria, England · 1883–1941*


> **Millthorpe (Edward Carpenter & George Merrill)** — Millthorpe, Derbyshire, England
> 53.28417° N, 1.53278° W · confidence 3/5
>
> **Hugh Walpole's Brackenburn — Queer Lakeland** — Keswick (Catbells/Derwentwater), Cumbria (Cumberland), England
> 54.56900° N, 3.15900° W · confidence 3/5


> `[PLATE 30 — Millthorpe (Edward Carpenter & George Merrill) · 53.28417° N, 1.53278° W]`


A man touched another man on the backside, gently, just above the buttocks, and out of it came a novel that could not be published for fifty-seven years. That is not a joke, and the man it happened to did not treat it as one. It is one of the strangest and most touching moments in English literary history, and it happened in a smallholding in Derbyshire, and to understand it you have to meet the couple who lived there.

Edward Carpenter was born in 1844 into comfortable Hove respectability, took holy orders, held a Cambridge fellowship, and then, in his late thirties, walked away from all of it. He had read Whitman; he had decided that the whole apparatus of Victorian middle-class life was a kind of disease; and in 1883, with money inherited from his parents, he bought seven acres at Millthorpe, near Sheffield, and set about living by hand — growing vegetables, making sandals, writing. What he was really doing, though it took him years to say so plainly, was building a place where he could love men without hiding.

In 1891, on a train, he met George Merrill. Merrill was from the Sheffield slums, working-class to the bone, no formal education, plain-spoken, flirtatious, fond of a drink — about as far from a Cambridge fellow as it was possible to get. In 1898 Merrill moved into Millthorpe, arriving in a blizzard, wheeling all his worldly goods over the hills in a handcart. He stayed for thirty years. The two men lived openly as a couple, cooking, keeping house, receiving visitors, for the better part of four decades — and they did it in plain sight, in the years on either side of Oscar Wilde's trial, when other men were burning their letters and fleeing the country. Officially, for the census and the electoral roll, Merrill was the servant. Everyone who visited understood perfectly well that he was not.

And they came in numbers, up the lane to Millthorpe, to see the prophet — socialists, vegetarians, suffragists, poets. Carpenter had put into print what almost no one else dared: *Homogenic Love* in 1894, and *The Intermediate Sex* in 1908, among the first works in English to argue calmly and in public that love between people of the same sex was natural, valuable, and no crime against anything. He wrote it in the very decade Wilde was jailed for living it. The two men make a matched pair across the chapter — Wilde broken by the law, Carpenter quietly evading it, both of them saying, in their different registers, the same forbidden thing.

Among the pilgrims, in 1912, came a shy novelist named E. M. Forster. And George Merrill, who by all accounts touched most people, touched him — laid a hand on him just above the buttocks. Forster remembered it for the rest of his life, and described it, in the Terminal Note he wrote for *Maurice* in 1960, with a precision that has become famous: the touch, he said, "seemed to go straight through the small of my back into my ideas, without involving my thoughts." He went home and began to write *Maurice*, a novel about a middle-class Englishman who finds love, and lasting happiness, in the arms of a working-class gamekeeper. Carpenter and Merrill are Maurice and Alec Scudder; the cross-class household at Millthorpe is the model for the ending, the happy ending, the one that says two men of different classes could simply walk off into the greenwood together and live.

And Forster put it in a drawer. He showed it to trusted friends — Sassoon, Strachey, Isherwood — and refused, for the rest of his life, to publish it, because it ended happily and he knew that a happy ending was the one thing the law and the public would not forgive. *Maurice*, written in 1913–14, appeared in 1971, the year after Forster died, fifty-seven years after he wrote it. That suppression is the point. The most hopeful gay novel in early English modernism existed the whole time, complete, and its author dared not let it out. The record did not go quiet by accident. Forster silenced it himself, out of fear that was entirely rational.

Fifty miles north and forty years on, another man built another quiet household, and could not speak of it either. Sir Hugh Walpole was, in the 1920s and 1930s, one of the best-selling novelists in the English-speaking world — thirty-odd novels, lecture tours of America, a knighthood in 1937. In 1923 he bought Brackenburn, a house of local slate on the slopes of Catbells above Derwentwater, near Keswick in Cumberland, and called it his little paradise. He filled it with books — some fifteen thousand of them — and paintings, and he wrote there his best-remembered work, the Herries novels, a great family saga rooted in the Borrowdale landscape just across the water.

At the end of 1924 he met Harold Cheevers. Cheevers was a policeman — a former revolver champion of the British Isles, as it happens — married, with children. He left the force and entered Walpole's service as chauffeur, and became, in Walpole's own phrase, his secretary, chauffeur, friend and rock. He was the "perfect friend" Walpole had spent his whole life looking for, and the two were inseparable until Walpole died. The arrangement was discreet to the point of invisibility; Cheevers's wife and children were housed, and the men were careful, because Walpole knew that indiscretion would have cost him the knighthood and very likely his readership. When Walpole died at Brackenburn in 1941, of a heart attack after over-exerting himself at a Keswick war-fundraising march, his obituary in the local paper described him as a bachelor and named his surviving brother and sister. Harold Cheevers, the man who had been beside him for seventeen years, the man to whom he left the house, was not mentioned. That is what the record looked like when the men themselves could not speak: a companion of two decades, edited out of a death notice, present only as an unnamed figure in a photograph.

Walpole is buried at St John's, Keswick, up the hill above the lake. Carpenter and Merrill are buried together in Surrey; when Merrill died in 1928 Carpenter said there was only the end to be desired, and followed him within the year. Two households, two counties, two ways of loving in a country that forbade it. One man wrote it down and dared not publish; another lived it and dared not name it. The difference between Millthorpe and Brackenburn is the difference between a life lived almost openly and a life lived almost in code — and the thing to hold onto is that both households lasted, both were happy, and both were real.



## 48. The Man Whose Family Burned the Evidence

*Plas Newydd · Anglesey, Wales · 1875–1905*


> **The Dancing Marquess & the Gaiety Theatre** — Llanfairpwllgwyngyll, Anglesey (Ynys Môn), Wales
> 53.21470° N, 4.19660° W · confidence 4/5


His car had a modified exhaust pipe, so that instead of fumes it sprayed perfume in its wake as he drove. This is the detail people remember about Henry Cyril Paget, and it is a good one, because it tells you at once that here was a man with more money than sense and a genius for spectacle — but it is also, in a way, the least of him, and it is worth getting past the perfume to the person, because the person is a good deal more interesting, and a good deal sadder, than the anecdotes.

He was born in 1875, in Paris, into one of the great landed families of Wales and England — the Pagets, Marquesses of Anglesey, whose first marquess had famously lost a leg at Waterloo. There was gossip from the start that Henry's real father was a French actor, and the boy was fostered for a time in Paris by an actor's sister, which may explain the theatricality that ran through everything he later did. When his father died in 1898 Henry, aged twenty-three, became the fifth Marquess, and with the title came some thirty thousand acres and an income of around £110,000 a year — a colossal fortune, worth many millions today.

He spent it, more or less all of it, in about six years, and he spent it on beauty.

The centre of the spending was the theatre. Henry took the chapel at Plas Newydd, the family seat on Anglesey overlooking the Menai Strait, and converted it — a consecrated family chapel — into a 150-seat theatre modelled on the opera house at Dresden, fitted by 1901 with electric stage lighting, and christened the Gaiety. He hired professional actors from London, luring them off their engagements at nearby Llandudno with wages nobody could refuse, and staged lavish productions — pantomime, comedy, and, tellingly, Oscar Wilde's *An Ideal Husband*, put on within a few years of Wilde's imprisonment, which was either oblivious or brave and was quite possibly both. He took the company on tour around Britain and Europe, travelling with its own orchestra and sets.

And in the intervals he danced. His signature turn was the "Butterfly Dance," borrowed from the American dancer Loïe Fuller, in which he moved under coloured light in a vast robe of transparent silk, waved like wings — sinuous, snakelike, sensual. He sewed real diamonds and other precious stones onto his costumes; his jewels alone would later realise some £80,000 at auction. He was known to appear aglow with diamonds from head to foot. A platoon of dressers was needed to get him in and out of the silk. For this he was dubbed, half in wonder and half in mockery, the Dancing Marquess.

He married, in 1898, his cousin Lilian Chetwynd. The marriage was annulled for non-consummation. There is a much-repeated story that he made her cover herself in the family jewels and did nothing else, which is unverifiable and belongs to legend rather than record, so treat it as such; what is documented is that the marriage produced nothing, was declared never to have been a marriage in the physical sense, and freed him to live as he wished.

The money ran out. On 11 June 1904 he was declared bankrupt, with debts of £544,000 — an almost unimaginable sum. The response was the Great Anglesey Sale, a bankruptcy auction of his effects; the sale of his wardrobe and personal effects ran, astonishingly, for some seventeen days, part of a liquidation that comprised many thousands of lots, in which everything went: the costumes, the jewels, the motor cars, the furs, even, it is said, his little dogs in their embroidered silk coats. He retreated to Monte Carlo and died there on 14 March 1905, of tuberculosis, aged just twenty-nine. The newspapers were merciless; one called his a "wasted life."

Then came the burning. The title passed to his cousin, who became the sixth Marquess, and who set about the memory of Henry Cyril with a thoroughness that is the whole reason we must be careful. He destroyed the fifth Marquess's papers — all of them. He turned the Gaiety Theatre back into a chapel. The family, for the better part of a century, kept Henry as an embarrassment; the present Marquess has said his ancestor's photographs ended up stuck in a bathroom while the war heroes got columns and statues.

This matters enormously when we come to the question everyone wants answered, which is whether Henry Cyril Paget was gay. The honest answer is that we cannot know, and the reason we cannot know is that the people best placed to tell us made sure of it. The direct evidence — his letters, his diaries, whatever he confided to paper — was deliberately destroyed by his own family. What survives is circumstantial: the cross-dressing performances, the jewels, the transparent silk, the annulled and unconsummated marriage. On that basis he is claimed today, reasonably and warmly, as a queer and gender-nonconforming icon. But the claim rests on inference, because the family burned the proof, and it is more honest — and more damning of them — to say so than to pretend to a certainty the record cannot bear.

The reclamation has been real and rather joyful. The National Trust, which has held Plas Newydd since 1976, now displays the very photographs the family once hid. Seiriol Davies made a celebrated musical about him, *How to Win Against History* — a title that says everything about a man whose history was taken from him. And in 2024 came a film, *Madfabulous*. The current, eighth Marquess speaks of him now with affection. Henry Cyril Paget has, in the end, won the long game.

So Chapter Six closes where its whole argument has been leading: on a man whose truth survives only as inference, because the family destroyed the rest. The queer past, again and again in these pages, comes down to us as private code, or hostile gossip, or a locked bag, or an unnamed mourner — and here, most starkly of all, as a bonfire. What the sixth Marquess could not burn was the photographs, and the memory, and the perfume still hanging faintly in the air of a story too good to be lost.

# Chapter 7 · Doomed and Determined

*Running for the border*


> `[PLATE 31 — chapter frontispiece]`


This is the shortest chapter in the book, deliberately. Most of Britain's evasions left a trail of raids, ruined lives and vanished rooms. Gretna Green produced something rarer: a place where the evasion became more durable than the rule.

Its importance lies in scale rather than complexity. A difference between English and Scottish marriage law turned the first village beyond the border into a national shorthand for escape. The couples came first; innkeepers, farmers and anvil priests built the industry around them. Long after the legal loophole closed, people kept coming because the journey itself had acquired meaning.

There is therefore no need to make a procession of one stop. The chapter crosses the border once and stays there long enough to see how a legal accident became a trade, a legend and finally a tradition.



## 49. The Village Eight Miles Over the Border

*Gretna Green & the road north · Dumfries and Galloway / Cumbria · 1754–present*


> **Gretna Green (the runaway marriages)** — Gretna Green, Dumfries and Galloway, Scotland
> 54.99472° N, 3.06444° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **The Road to Gretna — Cumbria's Elopement Route** — Carlisle (to the border), Cumbria (Cumberland), England
> 54.89240° N, 2.93820° W · confidence 3/5


> `[PLATE 32 — Gretna Green (the runaway marriages) · 54.99472° N, 3.06444° W]`


Picture the scene, because it happened thousands of times. A carriage, driven hard through the night up the Great North Road, the horses lathered, a young couple inside who are not yet married and mean to be by morning. Somewhere behind them, another carriage, driven harder — a father, or a guardian, or a lawyer, purple with fury, trying to close the gap before the border. And ahead, over a small river called the Sark, a nondescript Scottish village where a blacksmith is very possibly still up, because business is good. The whole of British erotic history is, in one sense, a long argument between desire and regulation, and there is no purer illustration of it than this: a law passed in London to control who could marry whom, and a village eight miles over the border that turned the evasion of that law into an industry lasting a century.

The law came first. In 1753 the Lord Chancellor, Lord Hardwicke, pushed through an Act "for the Better Preventing of Clandestine Marriage," in force from 25 March 1754. Before it, England was awash with irregular weddings — hurried, unregistered, sometimes drunken affairs, most notoriously in the precincts of the Fleet Prison in London, where disreputable clergymen married anyone for a fee. Hardwicke's Act shut all that down. From 1754, a valid marriage in England and Wales required banns read in church on three Sundays or a licence; it required a ceremony in an Anglican church before witnesses; and, crucially, it required the consent of a parent or guardian for anyone under twenty-one. A clergyman who broke the rules faced fourteen years' transportation. It was, in effect, the state and the Church seizing control of the marriage bed.

There was one enormous hole in it. The Act did not apply in Scotland.

Scots law had never accepted that marriage needed a church, a priest, banns, or anybody's permission. Under Scottish "irregular marriage," a couple were validly and bindingly married if they simply declared themselves so before two witnesses. That was the whole ceremony. No waiting, no residence, no parental veto; a boy could marry at fourteen and a girl at twelve. So the moment the new English requirements took effect, a couple thwarted in England had an obvious remedy: get to Scotland, declare yourselves married in front of two people, and it was done — as binding as any wedding in Canterbury Cathedral, and a good deal quicker.

Which brings us to the blacksmith, and to why he, of all people, became the emblem of runaway love. Gretna Green was the first village of any size across the border on the main coaching road up from London through Carlisle — the last English staging post. In the 1770s a new toll road funnelled the traffic straight through it, and the village never looked back. And at Gretna, at a junction where five roads met, stood the blacksmith's shop. Almost anyone could conduct a Scots irregular marriage, and in practice fishermen, weavers, toll-keepers and innkeepers all did a trade in it; but the blacksmith, standing at the crossroads, was often the first respectable-looking man an arriving couple clapped eyes on, and English couples in particular seem to have wanted some figure of standing to give the thing the feel of a ceremony. So the smith downed his hammer and became a "priest." The nickname stuck: the anvil priests. The tradition grew up that he would seal the marriage by bringing his hammer down on the anvil — joining two people, went the patter, as he joined two pieces of hot metal, in the heat of the moment but bound for good.

The mechanics were brisk and the price was whatever the market would bear. Two questions — are you of age, are you free to marry — a declaration, a bang of the hammer, a certificate, and it was over in minutes. The fee slid according to what the smith reckoned the couple could pay. Ordinary folk might marry for a few shillings or a dram of whisky; a fleeing aristocrat could be relieved of a great deal more. The last of the anvil priests, Richard Rennison, worked from 1926: he generally asked a pound but could take twenty for a good catch, and by the time irregular marriage was abolished he had recorded 5,147 ceremonies.

The pursuing fathers were real, and so, occasionally, was the violence. In a debate in the House of Commons in 1939, a member recalled that Lord Westmorland, marrying at Gretna, escaped the bride's pursuing father only by firing a pistol at one of the horses of the pursuing carriage. Lord Erskine, a future Lord Chief Justice, married there and paid £20; grander clients could pay a hundred guineas. And there is at least one documented tragedy in the chase, a death among a pursuing party, which is a reminder that behind the comedy of the runaway wedding there were real families torn and real stakes — money, land, reputation, and the ancient question of who gets to say no.

The state, predictably, tried to close the hole. Lord Brougham's Act of 1856 — the "cooling-off act", effective from the following year — required that at least one of the parties reside in Scotland for twenty-one days before the marriage. It slowed the trade sharply. It did not kill it, because the good people of Gretna simply started taking in lodgers, and farmers rented out their barns, and the twenty-one days were served. What finally ended the old way was the Marriage (Scotland) Act 1939, which from 1 July 1940 abolished irregular marriage by declaration altogether; from then on only a registrar or minister could tie the knot. The anvil priests hung up their hammers.

And here is the joke the whole chapter has been building towards: none of it stopped Gretna Green. The romance had outgrown the loophole that created it. In 2020 the BBC reported that the Gretna Green area hosted about twelve per cent of Scotland's weddings, while the local operator reports about five thousand couples marrying in the area each year. Many still choose to be "married over the anvil" in a ceremony that has, since 1940, no legal force whatsoever and enormous sentimental power. People who could marry anywhere on earth drive to a small village in Dumfries and Galloway to do it, because of what it has always meant.

Which is the point, and it echoes backwards through this book. British erotic history is a history of rules and the ducking of them. When George IV wanted Maria Fitzherbert he married her in secret and the Royal Marriages Act made the marriage void — regulation defeating desire. When Welsh couples wanted a wishing-well blessing there was Ffynnon Fair, the "Welsh Gretna Green" that never quite became one. Gretna is the same story with the ending reversed: here, for once, desire got round the regulation, wholesale, for a hundred years, and built an industry on it, and is building on it still. The law was made in London. The evasion was perfected on the far side of the border, and the sound of it was a hammer coming down on an anvil.



\newpage

# Chapter 8 · The Century of Scandal

*When the newspapers got cameras*


> `[PLATE 33 — chapter frontispiece]`


The twentieth century's great sex scandals were never really about sex. They were about class, power, and who gets believed — and the reliable test is to ask who ended up ruined.

The pattern is not subtle. The men involved were protected by the institutions they belonged to or elevated by the myth-making that followed; the women carried the shame for everyone. What made these episodes possible was a press that had acquired cameras, telephoto lenses and a proprietor's appetite for the bedroom, arriving at exactly the moment when deference to the governing class was beginning to fail. The combination was combustible.

There is a third element, easily missed. Both scandals hinged on a lie told to an institution — Profumo to Parliament, the Redlands prosecution to itself about what it had actually found — and in both cases the institution's need to be seen to act mattered more than the facts. That is why the pattern recurs. It was never the sex that brought governments down. It was the discovery that the people in charge had been lying about it, and the ancient British conviction that somebody less important should pay.



## 50. Nineteen

*Murray's Cabaret Club, Cliveden & Wimpole Mews · London and Buckinghamshire · 1959–1963*


> **Cliveden (the Profumo pool)** — Taplow, Buckinghamshire, England
> 51.55800° N, 0.69200° W · confidence 5/5
>
> **Murray's Cabaret Club (where Profumo met Keeler's world)** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.51250° N, 0.13722° W · confidence 4/5
>
> **Stephen Ward's flat, Wimpole Mews** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.52070° N, 0.14930° W · confidence 4/5


She was nineteen years old, working-class, and without a shred of power, and the men were middle-aged and running the country. Everything else about the Profumo affair — the spies, the swimming pool, the lies in the Commons, the government that fell — flows from that single imbalance, and any honest telling has to begin there, because for sixty years the telling did not. Christine Keeler was cast as the scarlet woman, the good-time girl, the tart who brought down a minister. She was, in fact, a young woman used and discarded by a series of powerful men, and this chapter of the book opens on her not because she was scandalous but because she was wronged.

Begin at Murray's. Murray's Cabaret Club stood at 16–18 Beak Street in Soho, a members' club that had been running since 1913, and it worked on a nicely hypocritical formula. The showgirls appeared topless — but under the rules of the Lord Chamberlain, who censored the London stage, a nude on stage was not permitted to move. If she stood still, in a "tableau," she was Art; if she so much as shifted her weight, she was Obscenity, and the club could be closed. It was the identical rule that governed the more famous Windmill Theatre round the corner, summed up there in a phrase that could stand as an epigraph for the whole of British attitudes to sex: "If it moves, it's rude." So the girls of Murray's walked bare-breasted to their positions and then froze, and afterwards, dressed, they sat with the wealthy clientele as hostesses for a fee of five pounds a time. Keeler reckoned she made about thirty pounds a week that way, in a club where, as she drily put it, the customers could look but could not touch.

It was at Murray's, in 1959, that Christine Keeler — then seventeen — met Stephen Ward. Ward was a fashionable osteopath with a gift for friendship and a client list that ran from film stars to royalty; he sketched portraits on the side, including one of Prince Philip. He took Keeler up, in a relationship both always described as like brother and sister rather than lovers, and she moved into his flat. It is worth being clear-eyed about what this was. Keeler's childhood had been one of real deprivation and abuse; she had left school at fifteen, and at seventeen had already borne and lost a baby. Ward, twenty-six years older and superbly connected, took a vulnerable teenager and drew her into a world far above her own — and if that reads today less like kindness than like a man building his own social capital out of a girl who had none, that is the more honest reading. At Murray's, Keeler also met a younger dancer, Mandy Rice-Davies, who became her friend and shared the ride down.

Then Cliveden. Cliveden was the vast Italianate house of the Astor family, above the Thames in Buckinghamshire, and Stephen Ward rented a cottage on its grounds. On the weekend of 8–9 July 1961, Lord Astor was hosting a grand dinner up at the house; down at the walled swimming pool, Ward had a small party of his own, Keeler — now nineteen — among them. She was in the pool. John Profumo, Secretary of State for War, aged forty-six, married to the actress Valerie Hobson, was among the dinner guests who wandered down from the house when they heard the laughter. What happened next has been embroidered for sixty years into a scene of deliberate seduction — the snatched swimming costume, the naked girl rising from the water. Strip the embroidery away and the documented core is simpler and stranger: a middle-aged cabinet minister came upon a nineteen-year-old showgirl at a pool on a summer night, and found her, in the plain sense of the word, entrancing. He asked Ward for her telephone number. A brief affair followed, over within a few months by the autumn of 1961.

There was a second man at Cliveden that weekend, and he is the reason the affair became history rather than gossip. Yevgeny Ivanov was a Soviet naval attaché — an intelligence officer — and a friend of Ward's, and Keeler was, around this same period, also involved with him. A cabinet minister and a Soviet spy sharing the bed of the same young woman, at the frozen height of the Cold War, months before the Cuban Missile Crisis: that is the fact that would later turn a private indiscretion into a matter of state. It should be said at once, because it is the single most important corrective in the whole story, that when Lord Denning came to investigate — his report published as a Command Paper in September 1963 — he concluded there had been no leak of security, no breach at all arising from the Ivanov connection. The danger to the nation, so luridly imagined, was never shown to have existed.

What actually cracked the thing open was not espionage but a gun. By late 1962 Keeler had become entangled with two other men, and on 14 December 1962 one of them, Johnny Edgecombe, turned up at Ward's flat at 17 Wimpole Mews in Marylebone, where Keeler had been staying, and — refused entry — fired shots at the door. The shooting put Keeler in front of the press and the courts, and once journalists began pulling at the thread, the whole tapestry came away: the minister, the showgirl, the Russian, the osteopath.

The political end came fast. On 22 March 1963 Profumo stood up in the House of Commons and told it flatly that there had been no impropriety in his acquaintance with Miss Keeler. It was a lie, told to Parliament, and it was the lie, far more than the sex, that destroyed him. On 5 June 1963 he confessed and resigned. In October Harold Macmillan, the Prime Minister, resigned too, his health and his authority broken; the Conservatives lost the general election the following year. A government had, in the end, been felled — not by a Soviet plot, but by a minister who could not tell the truth about his own bed.

And then they needed someone to punish, and they chose the man least able to fight back. Stephen Ward was arrested and put on trial for living on the immoral earnings of Keeler and Rice-Davies — for pimping, in effect. It is the settled view of most modern historians that this was a scapegoating: a prosecution got up, on thin and pressured evidence, to give the establishment a villain and to punish the man who had introduced a minister to a showgirl. His grand friends, who had used his flat and his hospitality for years, abandoned him almost to a man. Midway through the trial, on the night of 31 July 1963, facing a hostile judge and certain conviction, Ward took an overdose of sleeping pills. He was found guilty as he lay in a coma, and died on 3 August without regaining consciousness. Barristers and writers have argued ever since — Ludovic Kennedy at the time, Geoffrey Robertson QC decades later, who titled his book *Stephen Ward Was Innocent, OK* — that the conviction was unsafe and the trial rigged; the papers that might settle it remain, extraordinarily, sealed.

That leaves Christine Keeler, which is where a humane account has to end, because she is the one the story used worst. In December 1963 she was sent to Holloway prison for nine months, not for anything to do with Profumo, but for perjury in a separate case — a case, note, in which she had herself been the victim of an assault. She was twenty-one, without money or protectors, and she was made to serve time while the powerful men around the affair walked away: Profumo rehabilitated himself through decades of charity work and died honoured in 2006; the Astors kept their house. Keeler was hounded by the press for the rest of her life, cast forever as the woman in Lewis Morley's famous photograph astride a chair, and died in 2017, aged seventy-five, still trying to outrun a notoriety she had never deserved. Since her death her son has campaigned to clear her name, and reassessments — a Criminal Cases Review Commission referral, a petition for a posthumous pardon — have gathered pace, arguing what should have been obvious all along: that she was a young woman wronged, not a villain.



## 51. The Sunday They Came for the Butterfly

*Redlands, West Wittering, West Sussex & Eel Pie Island, Twickenham, Middlesex · 1956–1971*


> **Redlands (the Rolling Stones bust)** — West Wittering, West Sussex, England
> 50.77861° N, 0.88778° W · confidence 3/5
>
> **Eel Pie Island** — Twickenham, Greater London, England
> 51.44417° N, 0.32611° W · confidence 4/5


> `[PLATE 34 — Redlands (the Rolling Stones bust) · 50.77861° N, 0.88778° W]`


Just after eight o'clock on the evening of 12 February 1967, a face appeared at the window of a thatched Elizabethan house near West Wittering, in West Sussex. Inside, eight or nine people were coming down gently from a long day on LSD, listening to Bob Dylan's "Rainy Day Women #12 & 35" go round and round. One of the party thought the face at the glass must belong to a fan. It belonged, in fact, to one of eighteen police officers — two of them women, brought along in case a female body-search was needed — waiting outside Keith Richards's country house, Redlands, with a warrant. Keith got up and opened the door. What followed was not, whatever the newspapers would shortly claim, an orgy. In the words of one guest present that night, the art dealer Christopher Gibbs, it was a scene of pure domesticity.

That phrase is the whole tragedy in miniature, because domesticity was the one thing nobody wanted to hear.

The bust at Redlands is one of the two great establishment-versus-the-young scandals of the decade — the other being Profumo, four years earlier — and like Profumo it was never really about what it said it was about. The police found very little. Four amphetamine tablets in a green velvet jacket, which Mick Jagger said were his: they had in fact been bought, perfectly legally, over a counter in Italy by his girlfriend, Marianne Faithfull. A lump of hashish, and some heroin belonging to the art dealer Robert Fraser. The officers confiscated a stash of Keith's hotel soaps, apparently unsure what they were, and failed to recognise other substances entirely. The tip-off, it is widely believed, came via the *News of the World*, which Jagger was then suing for libel and which had every commercial reason to see him disgraced.

And then there is the Mars bar.

Let us be very precise, because for the rest of her life other people were imprecise on Marianne Faithfull's behalf. When the police entered, Faithfull — who was twenty — had just had a bath and was wrapped in a large fur rug, and nothing else. That is the entire factual basis of the legend. There was no chocolate. The story that the police interrupted Jagger performing a sexual act involving a Mars bar and Faithfull's body appeared in no charge, in no piece of court evidence, and in no newspaper report at the time. It seems to have spread by word of mouth after the trial — quite possibly seeded by police-canteen gossip — and it attached itself to her like a burr. She was still denying it decades later, and denying it with real weariness. She called it a dirty old man's fantasy, a policeman's idea of what people got up to on acid. Keith Richards, asked about it, said simply that he had no idea how the Mars bar got into the story, and that it showed you what was in people's minds.

Notice what the rumour does. It makes the men outlaws and the woman a punchline. Jagger and Richards became folk heroes, their sentences a *cause célèbre*; Faithfull became "the naked girl in the fur rug", and would say later that a male drug-user in that situation was glamorised, while a woman in the same room became a slut and a bad mother. The record here goes quiet in exactly the way the record usually goes quiet about women: through an invention repeated so often it hardens into fact.

The trial came at Chichester in late June 1967, before Judge Leslie Block. Faithfull recalled one small mercy — that the men were beautifully dressed, Jagger in a green velvet suit, Richards in silk — which helped make them look romantic rather than depraved. Fraser pleaded guilty to the heroin. Jagger got three months for his four tablets; Richards got a year for allowing cannabis to be smoked on his premises. They were sent down. For a first offence involving a handful of pep pills, the sentences were preposterous, and the country knew it.

What saved them was the least likely rescuer imaginable: William Rees-Mogg, the impeccably conservative editor of *The Times*, who on 1 July 1967 ran a leader under a borrowed line from Alexander Pope — "Who Breaks a Butterfly on a Wheel?" His argument, put plainly, was that Jagger had received a heavier sentence than any anonymous young man would have done, and that this was not what British justice was for. It is a measure of how far the thing had swung that the Establishment's own paper thought the Establishment had lost its head. On appeal at the end of July, Richards's conviction was quashed and Jagger's reduced to a conditional discharge. Between them they had spent a couple of nights inside.

Faithfull was not so lucky, because there is no appeal against a rumour. Within three years her life came apart. The relationship with Jagger ended in 1970; she lost custody of her son; and she spent roughly two years addicted to heroin and homeless on the streets of Soho. She came back — *Broken English*, in 1979, is one of the great comeback records — and she lived, garlanded at last, until 2025. But the girl in the fur rug was thrown to the wolves while the men she was with became legends, and the story that did it to her was never true. She deserves the same care this book has tried to give Christine Keeler, because it is the same crime, committed the same way, four years on.

A few miles upriver, and a few years earlier, the sixties were being invented on a sprung dance floor. Eel Pie Island is a small ait in the Thames at Twickenham, in Middlesex, reachable in those days only by a chain ferry or, from 1957, a footbridge. Its Victorian hotel had a ballroom with a bouncing sprung floor, built in 1898 for Edwardian tea dances. By the mid-1950s the place was decaying, and decay turned out to be exactly what the young wanted.

From 1956, jazz sessions began under a local trumpeter, and a Kingston junk-shop owner named Arthur Chisnall took the club on and turned it into something like a social experiment with a beat. Through the early 1960s the roll-call of who played the island reads like the founding charter of British rock: the Rolling Stones (a Wednesday residency), the Yardbirds, the Who, John Mayall's Bluesbreakers with Eric Clapton, the Tridents with Jeff Beck, Rod Stewart, and later Pink Floyd, David Bowie and Black Sabbath. One regular remembered the floor bouncing the whole crowd up and down in time; the guitarist Don Craine of the Downliners Sect called it an automatic sex machine — you played the music and the floor did the rest.

In 1967 the police forced the hotel to close over a repair bill of £200,000 that the owner could not meet. It reopened briefly in 1969 as Colonel Barefoot's Rock Garden, but by then the building had been colonised by a hippy commune — for a time one of the largest in Britain — and the residents were burning the floorboards for warmth. In 1971, with a demolition order pending, the hotel burned down in what every source calls a mysterious fire.

Today the island is a private enclave of artists' studios and boatyards, opened to the public a couple of weekends a year. The ballroom is gone; the sprung floor that helped teach Britain to dance is ash. Worth remembering, though, when Redlands is invoked as the moment the young collided with the law, that the Stones learned their trade on a bouncing floor over the Thames — a short row upstream from Hampton Court, where a very different court had once done its own scandalising.



\newpage

# Chapter 9 · Monuments to Pleasure

*The buildings and objects that changed what Britain was allowed to want*


> `[PLATE 35 — chapter frontispiece]`


A statue, a clinic, a basement and a shop. Between them, these four addresses did more to change what Britain was allowed to want than any king, poet or Act of Parliament in the preceding chapters — which is a large claim, and this chapter sets out to earn it.

Start with the joke, because it sets the tone. The winged figure above Piccadilly Circus is not Eros. Alfred Gilbert made it as Anteros, god of selfless and requited love, to crown a memorial to the Earl of Shaftesbury, the campaigner against child labour. Officialdom, panicking at a nude, tried to rename it the Angel of Christian Charity. The public looked at a naked archer over the West End and called it Eros anyway — and the monument to chaste philanthropy became, over the following century, one of the best-known pick-up spots in London. The fountain bankrupted its sculptor, who fled to Bruges. Britain's most famous erotic landmark is the wrong god, on the wrong subject, ruining the right man.

The pattern is worth naming. Change did not arrive by proclamation. It arrived at four ordinary addresses where a monument, a clinic, a club and a shop changed what could be acknowledged, controlled, shared and sold. Each worked because people used it until the law, the market or public memory was obliged to catch up.



## 52. Eros Isn't Eros

*Shaftesbury Memorial Fountain, Piccadilly Circus, London · 1886–present*


> **Shaftesbury Memorial Fountain ('Eros')** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.50984° N, 0.13449° W · confidence 5/5


The winged archer over Piccadilly Circus is not Eros. He is Eros's brother, and the difference is the entire point. London's most famous erotic monument was never meant to be erotic at all. The sculptor Alfred Gilbert intended it as Anteros — the god of selfless, mature, requited love, as against what Gilbert dismissively called the frivolous tyrant Cupid. It stands atop the Shaftesbury Memorial Fountain, unveiled in 1893 to commemorate Anthony Ashley-Cooper, 7th Earl of Shaftesbury, the factory reformer and campaigner against child labour — a man of such conspicuous Christian earnestness that a nude love-god would have been the very last thing anyone would choose to honour him. That, at any rate, was the theory.

Gilbert described his intention in properly Victorian prose: blindfolded Love, he wrote, sending forth his missile of kindness indiscriminately yet with purpose, ever soaring onwards. The figure was modelled on his studio assistant, an Anglo-Italian boy named Angelo Colarossi, who was fifteen when he posed for it in 1891. And it was, notably, the first statue in the world cast in aluminium — then a rare and novel metal, light enough to let the figure balance on one tiptoe at a jaunty angle that bronze could never have held. Beneath it Gilbert lavished a bronze fountain crawling with fish and crustaceans, a whole marine bestiary, into which the water was supposed to fall in a graceful dome.

It did not fall in a graceful dome. The basin was too narrow, the water splashed passers-by, and the surrounding area turned to mud. Gilbert had also chained drinking cups to the fountain; they were stolen almost at once.
