And the figure meant to embody chaste philanthropic love was immediately denounced as far too sensual for a memorial to the saintly Earl, and quite unsuitable for the theatre district, which respectable opinion regarded as a haven for exactly the sort of behaviour Anteros was supposed to rise above. Officialdom tried to fix this by renaming it the Angel of Christian Charity — roughly what you would get if you asked a committee to un-invent a joke.

Nobody listened. The public looked at a naked winged archer over Piccadilly and decided, sensibly enough, that it was Eros, god of desire, and Eros it has been ever since. There is a persistent story that Gilbert aimed the bow deliberately down Shaftesbury Avenue as a pun — the shaft buried, *Shaftes-bury* — but the arrow has been repointed so many times during the statue's various exiles that the pun, if it was ever intended, is now largely an accident of traffic engineering. The likelier truth is that the pun grew up afterwards to explain a statue that had already been misnamed.

The fountain ruined the man who made it. Gilbert's commission was for £3,000; the memorial is said to have cost him around £7,000, much of it on that gorgeous, doomed bronze base and on the high price of copper. He called the whole thing a great financial débâcle. He fell into debt, resigned from the Royal Academy, and left England for Bruges in disgrace, not to be rehabilitated for a quarter of a century. He refused even to attend the unveiling. At his lowest he is said to have suggested melting the whole thing down and using the metal to build shelters for the homeless — which would at least have honoured Shaftesbury properly.

The statue itself has led a well-travelled life. It was taken down in the mid-1920s while the tube station was built beneath, and stood for years in the Victoria Embankment Gardens; it went to Egham, in Surrey, for safety during the Second World War while its base was boarded up; it came home in 1947 in heavy rain, before thousands of spectators, re-sited to the corner where it now stands, arrow pointing somewhere new.

And here is the final irony, the one Gilbert never lived to see. A monument raised to selfless, non-sexual, Christian love, in a spot chosen for a man who fought to protect children, became over the twentieth century one of the great pick-up points of London. As "the Dilly", Piccadilly Circus was the acknowledged capital of male prostitution in the West End, a trade documented from Oscar Wilde's day down to the young men who leaned on the very railings around the fountain. Mervyn Harris's 1973 study *The Dilly Boys* followed six of them over a year; the poet Jeremy Reed later traced the whole submerged history in a book simply titled *The Dilly*. Both make the same point: this was a real world, mostly of the poor and the young, and mostly ignored until it was policed.

So London's most famous statue of love is the wrong god, on the wrong site, ruining the right sculptor — and it became, of all things, a byword for precisely the kind of love its maker was at pains to disown. Every couple who has ever said "meet me under Eros" has been keeping an assignation under the god of chastity. He would, one suspects, have seen the funny side. Gilbert never did.



## 53. The Mothers' Clinic, 1921

*Marie Stopes's Mothers' Clinic, 61 Marlborough Road, Holloway, London · 1921–1925*


> **The Mothers' Clinic for Constructive Birth Control** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.56400° N, 0.13100° W · confidence 3/5


> `[PLATE 36 — The Mothers' Clinic for Constructive Birth Control · 51.56400° N, 0.13100° W]`


Here is the object at the centre of the story: a small, high-domed rubber cap, about the size of an eggcup, with a tab for easy removal and, printed clearly across the top of the dome, the words "Pro-Race". It is a cervical cap, a barrier contraceptive, and for tens of thousands of British women in the 1920s it was the first reliable control they had ever been offered over their own fertility. The name — Pro-Race — tells you, before we have gone a step further, that this is not going to be a simple story of a heroine.

The woman who designed it and gave it away at cost was Marie Stopes, and she was, on the evidence, both a liberator of women and a eugenicist. There is no honest way to write her that keeps only one of those facts.

Stopes came to the subject the hard way. A distinguished palaeobotanist — the first woman on the science faculty at Manchester — she married, in 1911, a man whose impotence, she said, left the marriage unconsummated, and she claimed to have worked out what was wrong only after private study in the British Museum reading room. (Historians, notably the Wellcome's Lesley Hall, think the tale of total innocence sits at some angle to the truth; Stopes was a great curator of her own myth.) The marriage was annulled in 1916. Out of it came *Married Love* in 1918, a book which argued, to a scandalised nation, that women felt sexual desire, that they were entitled to satisfaction, and that a good marriage required a husband who understood as much. It sold out its first printing in a fortnight and ran through edition after edition; couples kept it like scripture. Later the same year came *Wise Parenthood*, which did the practical work *Married Love* had only gestured at, and told readers how to prevent conception.

On 17 March 1921, Stopes and her second husband, the aircraft manufacturer Humphrey Verdon Roe, opened the Mothers' Clinic for Constructive Birth Control at 61 Marlborough Road, in working-class Holloway, north London. It was the first birth-control clinic in Britain. It was free. It was open to married women only. It was staffed by midwives — the first was Nurse Hebbes — with a visiting doctor, and it was deliberately run by women, in an ordinary house, so that the women who came would not have to be examined by men.

What actually happened there is worth spelling out, because the record is unusually good. A woman would be seen, examined, and fitted with the small rubber cervical cap — Stopes's own high-domed design, a modified French pattern, sold at cost. She was shown how to insert it, moistened with soapy water or a quinine-based ointment, and advised in some cases to use a soluble quinine pessary as well. Stopes disapproved of the condom, of withdrawal, and of the "safe period", which she thought both unreliable and joyless. In that first year an average of three women a day came through the door. In 1925 the clinic moved to Whitfield Street in central London, and that year Stopes published its own report under the title *The First Five Thousand*, claiming that fewer than one per cent of her patients had fallen pregnant while using her methods. By 1929 some ten thousand women had been given contraceptive advice.

The opposition was ferocious, and loudest from the Catholic Church. In 1923 Stopes sued a Catholic doctor, Halliday Sutherland, for libel after he attacked her clinic in print, accusing her in effect of experimenting on the poor. The case ran up through the courts; a jury reached a famously muddled verdict; Stopes was awarded a hundred pounds; and on final appeal to the House of Lords, in 1924, she lost. The Catholic community had raised a substantial fighting fund for Sutherland. The publicity, characteristically, did her cause more good than the verdict did harm.

Now the part that must not be softened, because it was not incidental. Stopes was a committed eugenicist, a life fellow of the Eugenics Society, and her birth-control work was framed, in her own words, around "racial" improvement. The Pro-Race brand was no accident of marketing; her clinic's parent body was called the Society for Constructive Birth Control and Racial Progress. She wanted contraception concentrated in poor districts precisely because she believed the poor were breeding the nation into degeneration, and she advocated the compulsory sterilisation of those she judged unfit for parenthood. She applied the creed to her own family with a coldness that still shocks: when her only surviving son, Harry, married Mary Wallis, who was short-sighted — a defect Stopes regarded as heritable — she refused to attend the wedding and cut him almost entirely out of her will. And in August 1939, weeks before the war, she sent a volume of her love poems to Adolf Hitler with a fawning covering note. She admired the Nazi enthusiasm for "fitness", though she disapproved of some of the regime's aims.

Both of these things are true at once, and the clinic is where they meet. The same small rubber cap that gave a Holloway mother, worn out by a child a year, the power to say "no more" was branded with a slogan of racial purity. You cannot have the liberation without the ideology attached; they came into the world in the same house, on the same day, from the same woman.

It is right to hold both. Think back through this book — the Welsh bundling couples with no sure way to know, Anne Hathaway three months gone at her wedding, the string of children Burns fathered and could not always keep — and you see what the absence of reliable contraception actually cost women, century after century. Stopes ended that, or began to. She gave hundreds of thousands of women a measure of control over their own bodies that no poet, king or Act of Parliament in the preceding chapters had ever managed. And she held views that are indefensible. The plaque on Marlborough Road records the first; it does not mention the second; and the honest reader has to carry them both up the street together.



## 54. The Gateways

*The Gateways Club, 239 King's Road, Chelsea, London · c.1931–1985*


> **The Gateways Club** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.48480° N, 0.16880° W · confidence 5/5


The door was painted green — not a striking green, not a green anyone would remark on. That was the point. For fifty years you had to know it was there, at 239 King's Road on the corner of Bramerton Street in Chelsea, and know what was behind it. Down a steep flight of steps into a windowless basement was the Gateways, described by English Heritage as London's best-known and longest-lived lesbian social venue and often called one of the longest-running lesbian clubs in the world. An English Heritage blue plaque was unveiled beside the door on 2 July 2025.

It opened around 1931 as a bohemian drinking club that welcomed the people other places threw out: artists, black musicians, "theatrical types", and, increasingly, women who loved women. A man called Ted Ware took it over in 1943 — legend says he won it in a poker game, or possibly a boxing bet — and it was his wife, the Italian actress Gina Cerrato, who from the later 1950s ran the place with an iron hand, joined by an American ex-airforce woman known only as Smithy, whose exact relationship to Gina was never discussed and widely assumed. In 1967, the year male homosexuality was partly decriminalised, the Gateways went the other way and barred men altogether. It became women-only, and stayed that way to the end.

The physical world of it was tiny and specific. Through the green door in Bramerton Street, down the steps, past Gina at the cloakroom, into a smoky cellar room only about thirty-five feet long: a bar at one end, usually worked by Smithy; a jukebox with, as one visitor put it, a distinct preference for songs about girls; a fruit machine by a central pillar; and a dance floor barely big enough to move on, which was rather the appeal. The artist Maggi Hambling remembered it as all sweat and sway — so many people dancing in so small a space that the crush *was* the excitement.

It ran on rules. No admission after ten. Troublemakers barred on the spot, which mattered, because the nearest alternative was in Brighton. No kissing in the bar — that was for the toilets. And, most tellingly, the place ran on the butch-and-femme codes of its era. Newcomers were, in effect, asked which they were and expected to dress and behave accordingly: the butches in tailored suits and shirts and short hair, the femmes in dresses and heels. The oral historians who later gathered the members' own testimony — above all Jill Gardiner, whose *From the Closet to the Screen* collected some eighty voices — recorded the friction when a younger generation arrived who did not want to choose. One of them, quoted as Brighton Sue, remembered the old diehards with their Brylcreem eyeing the newcomers and demanding: what are you — butch or femme?

In 1968 the club got the fame it never wanted. The director Robert Aldrich filmed scenes for *The Killing of Sister George* there and, wanting authenticity, used the real members as extras, paying them ten pounds a day. Gina herself appears, as the receptionist. For many women in Britain it was the first glimpse they had ever had of a room full of people like them — but the film's portrayal of lesbian life was hardly flattering, Gina regretted taking part, and the exposure brought a mixture of curiosity and hostility to the green door. The Gay Liberation Front later picketed the place for being insufficiently political; Gina, who had no time for politics in her bar and asked members to leave their debates at the top of the stairs, called the police on them.

Which brings us to the law, and to a myth that needs killing on sight. It is often said that sex between women was legal in Britain only because Queen Victoria refused to believe it existed and struck it out of the 1885 Act. This is nonsense, twice over. There was never a clause about women for her to strike out; and the monarch cannot veto part of a bill in any case — royal assent is all or nothing, and the sovereign does not even see the final text. The historian Caroline Derry, whose *Lesbianism and the Criminal Law* is the authority here, has shown that lawmakers knew perfectly well that lesbians existed and chose silence deliberately, partly for fear of putting ideas into respectable women's heads. The story about Victoria seems to have grown up only after a 1977 lesbian protest at a statue of her in New Zealand.

The practical effect of that legal silence is the whole reason a place like the Gateways existed as it did. Because sex between women was never a crime, there were no molly-house raids, no dock, no Oscar Wilde of the lesbians — but there was also no recognition, and the policing was done another way: through licensing, through landlords, through the constant low pressure of a society that preferred you not to exist. A green door in Chelsea was the answer to a law that would not say your name.

The Gateways lost its late licence in 1985, after complaints about the noise, in a Chelsea grown far too expensive for it, and closed in September that year. On the final private night the members prised the nameplate off the door and took it away as a souvenir. The plaque went up forty years later, on 2 July 2025. It is the direct descendant, this basement, of Anne Lister's coded diaries and the Ladies of Llangollen's respectable cottage and the Caravan Club's brief, defiant life — the same long story of women who loved women, told this time behind a door painted the exact green of not being noticed.



## 55. Marble Arch, 1970

*First Ann Summers shop, Edgware Road / Marble Arch, London · 1970–present*


> **First Ann Summers Shop** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.51360° N, 0.15890° W · confidence 2/5


> `[PLATE 37 — First Ann Summers Shop · 51.51360° N, 0.15890° W]`


In 1981 a twenty-one-year-old with next to no business training sat in a friend's front room in Thamesmead at what was meant to be a Tupperware party. Someone had asked her to bring along a few things from the family firm, which happened to sell sex toys. Out they came at the end of the evening, and the room — full of perfectly ordinary women — dissolved into giggling, buying and frank conversation. Jacqueline Gold watched it happen and understood something no one running a sex shop in Britain had ever grasped: that the customer for this stuff was a woman, and that she would buy it happily among friends, and never, ever in a shop with a blacked-out window.

To see how large that idea was, you have to go back eleven years and half a city away. On 9 September 1970, the first Ann Summers shop opened on the Edgware Road, near Marble Arch, in London. It was the creation of Michael Caborn-Waterfield, a raffish figure known as "Dandy Kim" — a former black-market nylons trader who had had a long affair with Diana Dors, served time in a French jail, and, it is said, written a sex manual under a pseudonym. He named the shop after his young secretary and former girlfriend Annice Summers (born Annice Goodwin), persuading her to take the surname in return for a directorship, because he thought his own notoriety might frighten the customers. The *Daily Mirror* photographed her outside it and reached for the phrase of the day: a "sex supermarket".

The stock, by the accounts that survive — chiefly the 2016 obituaries, which are better read as trade lore than as audited fact — ran to sex aids, magazines, erotic books, massage lotions and inflatable dolls: the contents of the Soho under-the-counter trade, dragged blinking into a high-street window. It was, unmistakably, a shop for men. Annice fell out with Caborn-Waterfield within months and left, later complaining that she had never agreed to the more explicit goods being sold under her name; the business foundered and went into voluntary liquidation. In 1971 or 1972 — sources differ — the brothers Ralph and David Gold bought the wreck, the name and its debts for a figure usually given as around £10,000. David Gold's daughter Jacqueline joined the firm in 1981, hated the atmosphere of what was still, essentially, the sex trade as everyone pictured it, and then found herself in that suburban front room.

The party plan she built out of it is one of the genuinely revolutionary ideas in modern British retail, and its genius lay entirely in *who it was for*. An Ann Summers party was held in a woman's own living room, hosted by a friend, women only, no men admitted: an evening of drink, games, giggling and the discreet passing-round of lingerie and sex toys, with an order taken at the end. It sold the exact same products as the furtive shop, but it moved them out of the place women found intimidating and into the one place they felt entirely safe. It also, not incidentally, sidestepped the law: because there was no shop and no public display, it slipped past the rules that governed sex retail.

She had to fight for it. She has described taking the idea to Ann Summers's all-male board and being, in her own account, nearly laughed out of the room — one director reportedly threw down his pen and announced that women were not even interested in sex, which told her, she said, rather more about his marriage than about her plan. The banks were wary; she later said she was sent a bullet in the post. She got it launched anyway, and by the end of the first year had recruited more than five hundred party organisers. The model grew at around twenty per cent a year for two decades. Gold became chief executive in the late 1980s, took Ann Summers onto the high street proper, and turned a failing men's sex shop into a chain of well over a hundred stores, run — as the company liked to say — by women, for women. By the 2000s thousands of organisers were hosting thousands of parties a week.

The wider law was catching up all the while, and not in Ann Summers's favour. The Local Government (Miscellaneous Provisions) Act 1982 introduced, for the first time, a system of local-authority licensing for "sex establishments" — a response, in part, to public disquiet about the spread of Soho-style shops. Whether an Ann Summers counted as a "sex shop" under the Act turned on whether it sold sex articles "to a significant degree", and the company spent years arguing, shop by shop and council by council, that a high-street store full mostly of lingerie and nightwear did not. It is a very British kind of victory: the shop that had once been too disreputable for the high street now insisting, in licensing hearings, that it was really quite respectable.

And that is the note on which to close a chapter called *Monuments to Pleasure*. The great erotic revolution in modern Britain did not happen in Soho, behind the blacked-out windows and the beaded curtains, run by men for men. It happened in suburban living rooms in places like Thamesmead and Sheffield, over a glass of wine, run by women for women — and it was built by a twenty-one-year-old the board had laughed at. A statue that turned out to be the wrong god; a clinic that gave women their bodies back; a basement club behind a green door; and a shop that finally worked out its customer had been a woman all along. Not a law, not a poet, not a king. A handful of buildings and objects and businesses, quietly changing what Britain was allowed to want.



\newpage

# Chapter 10 · London After Dark

*The old trades, the licensed and the unlicensed — a chapter for adults*


> `[PLATE 38 — chapter frontispiece]`


A word before we go in, because the register changes here and you should know why.

This chapter follows London's commercial sex trade and erotic nightlife across eight centuries, and it is written for adults. Where the record documents what people did, what they wore, what they paid and what apparatus they used, this chapter says so plainly, in ordinary English, without asterisks and without the coy Victorian circumlocutions that made so much of this material unreadable in the first place. The reticence practised elsewhere in this book would here amount to a kind of dishonesty: you cannot write about the Bankside stews or a Georgian flogging house and then decline to say what happened in them. There is also, unavoidably, material about the exploitation of the poor and the young — the Cleveland Street prosecutions of 1889 turn entirely on it — and where that is the subject, the comedy stops. If you would rather not read explicit historical description, this is the chapter to skip. The book resumes its ordinary manners on the other side.

Now the argument, which is the sharpest in the book.

London's sex trade was never simply suppressed by the authorities. It was also licensed, taxed and regulated by them, usually by the same institutions publicly condemning it. The brothels of medieval Bankside operated under ordinances issued in the name of the Bishop of Winchester, who drew rents from the stews for centuries, until Henry VIII suppressed the licensed houses in 1546; the women were nicknamed Winchester Geese after their landlord. An annual printed directory of Covent Garden prostitutes, giving names, addresses, prices and specialities, sold thousands of copies a year for the better part of four decades in the eighteenth century, advertised in newspapers and sold openly at booksellers' stalls. It was not prosecuted until 1794 — by which point its publisher could tell the court, accurately, that nobody had thought it worth prosecuting before.

What changed in the nineteenth century, historians argue, was not morality so much as access. As explicit material became cheaper and reached beyond the collectors whom the authorities trusted, regulation hardened. The closer the trade came to working-class and youthful readers, the more urgently the state discovered an interest in suppressing it. That is a historical interpretation rather than the stated object of any one statute, but it reveals the argument beneath this chapter: obscenity law in Britain has always been, at bottom, a law about class and price.



## 56. The Bishop of Winchester's Geese

*Bankside stews & Cross Bones Graveyard · Southwark, London · c.1161–1853 (graveyard closed), reclaimed from 1996*


> **The Bankside stews & Crossbones Graveyard** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.50280° N, 0.09240° W · confidence 4/5


The women were called Winchester Geese, after the bishop who licensed their brothels and drew rent from the Bankside stews. The founding document of that arrangement, drawn up in the fifteenth century, tells you almost everything about the English attitude to sex, money and God, and it does so with a straight face. It is titled, with the sonorous pomposity of the very holy, the "Ordinances Touching the Government of the Stewholders in Southwark under the Direction of the Bishop of Winchester". It sets out the rules for running brothels. It was issued in the name of the Bishop of Winchester. And its author claimed, dishonestly, that it dated back to 1161 and to a parliament held under Henry II — a parliament that could not have existed, there being no such thing at the time. The Wellcome Collection, which has examined the thing carefully, dates the surviving text to the fifteenth century and notes drily that the bishop was gilding his own paperwork. Even the founding charter of England's licensed sex trade, in other words, is a forgery of sorts. It is a fitting beginning.

The setting was Bankside, a strip of the south bank of the Thames directly opposite the City of London, and its whole character depended on a legal accident. This was the Liberty of the Clink: a manor that lay technically in Surrey but answered to neither the sheriff of Surrey nor the aldermen of the City. It answered instead to the Bishop of Winchester, whose London palace stood on what is now Clink Street. Because the City's writ stopped at the river, the things the City forbade within its walls — the playhouses, the bear-baiting rings, the brothels — simply crossed the water and set up shop where the bishop, rather than the mayor, took the rents. It is the founding principle of this entire chapter: London's vices were not banished but relocated, licensed and taxed, usually by the very authorities that condemned them.

The brothels were called "stews", a word with two possible parentages. It may descend from the fish stew-ponds that bred carp on the bishop's land; it may equally come from *estuves*, the Norman-French for the heated bath-houses where, then as now, a great deal more than bathing tended to go on. Either way the brothel-keeper became a "stewholder" even where his premises offered no bath at all. The nickname given to the women proved just as durable. To be "bitten by a Winchester goose" was to catch a venereal disease; the phrase "goose bumps" is sometimes traced, plausibly if not certainly, to the same unhappy source.

What the ordinances actually contain is the surprising part. A good number of the rules read less like the fantasies of a lecherous bishop than like early, imperfect labour law. A stewholder who detained a woman against her will faced a fine. A woman who "lives by her body" was not to be forced to board on the premises but must be free to lodge where she chose and to come and go as she pleased — the clause about coming and going freely is the one modern writers quote most, and rightly, because it is genuinely striking. Married women were barred, and so were nuns. Women with a "burning disease" were not to work. The house was forbidden to hold any man prisoner over a debt. And crucially, the stews were not supposed to sell food and drink, precisely so that a woman could not be trapped in a spiral of debt to her own employer for her supper and her ale — though the surviving court records show stewholders flogging illicit ale anyway, and repeat offenders treating the fines as an operating cost. There were fines, too, for a woman who dragged a man in off the street by his clothing.

It would be pretty to imagine the medieval Church running a model of ethical sex work. It would also be wrong. The regulations protected the women partly because protected women were a more reliable revenue stream, and the bishop's income was made up substantially of the rents and the fines. The point is not that the bishops were kind. The point is that they were businessmen in mitres, and that the trade they profited from was, by the later fifteenth century, a serious enterprise — eighteen licensed houses along the bank, according to records of the period, though the number rose and fell with plague, war and royal temper.

The whole arrangement lasted, astonishingly, for centuries, and it was killed not by a sudden attack of conscience but by disease. There had been earlier attempts — Henry VII closed the stews around 1505 after an outbreak of the pox, and they mostly reopened within the year. It was Henry VIII, in 1546, who finally put them down. John Stow, writing his great *Survey of London* in 1598, records that the ban was "proclaimed by the sound of trumpet" — one imagines the trumpeter doing his best to look solemn — and the driving fear was syphilis rather than sin. It did not, of course, end the trade. It merely scattered it. The geese packed up and dispersed across London, to Cock Lane and Petticoat Lane and streets whose names are too frank to have survived on the maps.

A short walk from the river, on Redcross Way, is the coda to the story, and it is one that has to be told carefully, because affection has a way of hardening folklore into fact. In his 1598 *Survey*, Stow mentions "a plot of ground called the Single Woman's churchyard", where the Bankside prostitutes, denied Christian burial for their trade, were laid in unhallowed ground. Local tradition has long identified this churchyard with Cross Bones, the disused burial ground on Redcross Way. It is a powerful identification and it may well be true. It is not, however, proven; historians who have looked at it, including those quoted by the Smithsonian, note that the link rests essentially on Stow's line and on tradition, not on documentary certainty. That caveat matters, because it protects the dead from being conscripted into a better story than the one they actually lived.

What the ground itself says is grimmer and harder to romanticise. When the Jubilee Line was extended in the 1990s, an electricity substation was to be built on the site, and Museum of London archaeologists were given a few weeks to excavate a corner of it. In the 1999 report by Brickley, Miles and Stainer they removed 148 skeletons, all dated to the graveyard's final decades, roughly 1800 to 1853, and estimated that this represented "less than 1% of the total number of burials" — pointing to something like 15,000 in all. The demographics are the part that stops you: 104 of the 148, over 70 per cent, were children, at least 98 of them aged six or under. The bones carried the marks of rickets, scurvy and syphilis; one skeleton, later shown on television, was a girl of sixteen to nineteen, four feet seven inches tall. By 1853 the ground was closed as "completely overcharged with dead". This was, by then, not the geese's graveyard at all but a paupers' pit for the poorest parishioners of St Saviour's. Wherever the comedy of this chapter belongs — and there is comedy, a great deal of it, in a bishop taxing brothels — it does not belong here.

The site was rescued, in the end, by the living. From 1996 the writer John Constable, who also wrote as "John Crow", took up its cause and wove its history into *The Southwark Mysteries*; a vigil has been held at the gates on the 23rd of every month since 2004, and in 2021 the Bankside Open Spaces Trust secured a thirty-year lease. Today the iron gates on Redcross Way are thick with ribbons, tokens and hand-written names, tied there for the outcast dead. Bankside was always London's licensed elsewhere — the same stretch that later gave us the Tabard from which Chaucer's pilgrims set out, and that sat a river's width from the molly houses of the eighteenth century. It made the Church rich for four hundred years. Its women got a nickname, a disease named after a bird, and a corner of unconsecrated ground that the city forgot and then, slowly, remembered.



## 57. Harris's List

*Covent Garden bagnios · London · 1757–1795*


> **Covent Garden bagnios & Harris's List** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.51230° N, 0.12250° W · confidence 3/5


> `[PLATE 39 — Covent Garden bagnios & Harris's List · 51.51230° N, 0.12250° W]`


It was a small thing, this book — a pocket-sized annual, cheaply printed, sold for two shillings and sixpence, the sort of object a gentleman could slip inside his coat and produce in a coffee house with a raised eyebrow. It was called *Harris's List of Covent Garden Ladies*, and for the best part of forty years, from 1757 to 1795, it was one of the reliable bestsellers of Georgian London. The German visitor Johann von Archenholz, in his *A Picture of England* of 1791, recorded that the lists were printed by a tavern-keeper in Drury Lane and that "eight thousand copies are sold annually" — a genuinely startling figure in an age before mass literacy. Each edition described between roughly 120 and 190 women working in and around Covent Garden: where to find them, what they looked like, what they charged, and what, in the coy and heavily italicised euphemism of the day, they specialised in.

The "Harris" of the title was real, more or less. Jack Harris was the working name of a man born John Harrison, head waiter at the Shakespear's Head Tavern on the Covent Garden piazza, who had styled himself, with magnificent cheek, "Pimp-General of All England". He kept a handwritten ledger of some four hundred women, and it was probably his notoriety that sold the printed version, since his name appeared on the title page — written, as it happens, in someone else's hand. The someone else was Samuel Derrick, a debt-ridden Irish poet and Grub Street hack who, according to the historian Hallie Rubenhold, whose *The Covent Garden Ladies* (2005) is the definitive modern account, wrote the thing in an act of near-desperation to keep himself out of debtors' prison. Derrick knew Samuel Johnson and James Boswell; he ended his days, improbably, as Master of Ceremonies at Bath. The little book of harlots was a poet's survival strategy.

The trade it served had its own architecture. The bagnios of Covent Garden — the word comes from the Italian *bagno*, a bath — had begun life as Turkish-style bath-houses and slid, by an entirely predictable gravity, into brothels and houses of assignation where a room, a meal and company could be had discreetly for the night. Around them clustered the whole apparatus of the Georgian sex market: the taverns, the coffee houses with a woman's arm holding a coffee-pot painted on the sign as a knowing signal, the theatres of Drury Lane and Covent Garden disgorging their audiences into the piazza.

And here, because there is no more sensible place for it in the whole book, we must talk about contraception, or rather about "armour". The Georgian condom was a real and thriving article of commerce, and it was made, unappetisingly, from the caecum — the blind gut — of a sheep or lamb, soaked, turned, scraped, treated and dried, then tied at the open end with a length of coloured ribbon to hold it on. It was sold, and it was reused. The two great names in the London trade were both women and both rivals: Mrs Phillips, who traded at the Sign of the Green Canister on Half Moon Street off the Strand, and Mrs Perkins. A surviving trade card for Mrs Phillips, held at Duke University, boasts of thirty-five years in the business, of exports to France, Spain, Portugal and Italy, and offers, with a poet's touch, the couplet: "To guard yourself from shame or fear, / Votaries to Venus, hasten here." The most famous testers of the product left their own record. Casanova is described inflating them like balloons to check for holes before use — an early and sensible piece of quality control. And James Boswell, whose *London Journal* of 1762–63 is the frankest sexual diary of the century, recorded picking up "a strong, jolly young damsel" and conducting her to Westminster Bridge, where "in armour complete did I engage her upon this noble edifice." Boswell being Boswell, he also recorded, repeatedly, the times he went without, and the poxes that followed.

What did the *List* actually say? At its best the prose is a peculiar hybrid of estate-agent's puffery and mock-classical verse, all "Cyprian nymphs" and "dairy hills of delight". Prices ran, roughly, from five shillings to five pounds, and occasionally higher for the celebrated. Most entries flatter. A few are startlingly, needlessly cruel: the 1773 notice for one Miss Berry pronounces her "almost rotten, and her breath cadaverous". Others carry, buried in the smut, a flash of real biography and even sympathy — the woman "led astray" by a designing man, the country girl come to London and undone. It is worth holding both of those facts in the head at once. The *List* was, as Rubenhold has argued, essentially a work of erotica, written by men, for the private pleasure of men, and its women are specimens arranged for the male gaze. And yet buried in its pages are real people, with addresses and ages and, occasionally, the outline of a life.

That is the note on which to end, because it is the honest one. These were not characters. Kitty Fisher, who appears in the *List*, was a genuine celebrity courtesan, painted by Reynolds. Charlotte Hayes, who features briefly in the 1761 edition, went on to run one of the grandest brothels in London and to amass, with her gambler partner, a small fortune. Most of the others left no such trail, and it is the achievement of Rubenhold's book to have gone into the parish records and the Grub Street journalism and reassembled a few of them into something like biography. The *List* itself was killed in 1795, when public opinion had turned and its publishers were fined and imprisoned. But for four decades it did a brisk trade, entirely in the open, licensed by nothing more than the Georgian state's happy willingness to look the other way — the same tolerance that lit the Dark Walks at Vauxhall and let the pleasure-seekers of London find each other in the shrubbery. Nobody prosecuted a bestseller about prostitutes. That would come later, and it would come, as we shall see, only when the trade got cheap.



## 58. The White House, Soho Square

*Manor House, 21 Soho Square · London · c.1778–1801*


> **The White House, 21 Soho Square** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.51590° N, 0.13130° W · confidence 4/5


A cupboard opens and a human skeleton lurches into the room on hidden machinery, grinning at the gentleman who has just been shown into what looked like an ordinary chamber. This was the "Skeleton Room", and it was, we are told, one of the attractions of the White House at 21 Soho Square, the most theatrical brothel in Georgian London. It is a wonderful image. Before we get too attached to it, though, we should be honest about where it comes from, because the White House is a case study in how much of sexual history is reliable record and how much is a good story told long after the doors have closed.

Start with what is solid. The house at 21 Soho Square — the building now called Manor House — is documented in the *Survey of London*, published by the London County Council and available through British History Online, which is about as sober an authority as this subject possesses. It records that the house was kept as a hotel by one Thomas Hooper, that it "became notorious in the annals of Fashion as the infamous White House", and that "it is likely that the house was used as a brothel". It describes the reception rooms as "garishly decorated": three of them, known from their fittings as the Gold, Silver and Bronze rooms, had walls "inlaid with mirrored panels". There was a Painted Chamber, a Grotto, a Coal Hole, and the Skeleton Room, "where, for the delectation of the patrons, a skeleton could be made to step out of a closet with the aid of machinery". That the house had themed rooms and a mechanical skeleton, in other words, is attested by a serious topographical survey. So far, so gloriously peculiar.

Now the softer ground. The fuller, more lurid version — the one where whole rooms transform, where innocent-looking furniture springs traps, where the place becomes something close to a mechanised pleasure-dungeon — rests mainly on Henry Mayhew, writing in 1861. Mayhew, the great chronicler of *London Labour and the London Poor*, called the White House "a notorious place of ill-fame" and wrote that some of its apartments "were furnished in a style of costly luxury; while others were fitted up with springs, traps, and other contrivances, so as to present no appearance other than that of an ordinary room, until the machinery was set in motion." It is vivid stuff. But Mayhew was writing sixty years after the house had closed, from reputation and hearsay rather than from any inventory, and the detail has grown in the retelling ever since, until modern accounts cheerfully describe a "transforming sex dungeon". The mirrored rooms and the skeleton have a documentary spine. The rest is memory, and memory of this kind tends to improve with age.

What is not in doubt is the milieu. Soho and neighbouring St James's were, by the later eighteenth century, the luxury end of the London sex trade, and the White House belonged to a recognisable species of establishment: the theatrical, expensive, discreet house of assignation for men who could pay. The genre had a founding mother. Mrs Goadby — Jane, or Margaret, Goadby — is credited with importing the French model to London, fitting up a house in Marlborough Street "in an elegant stile", engaging "some of the first-rate filles de joye", and, tellingly, employing a surgeon to inspect the women and turn away any of doubtful health. It was, for its clientele, a considerable selling point. Her success bred imitators, the most famous of whom was Charlotte Hayes, who established herself at King's Place, off Pall Mall, and ran what the trade wittily called a "nunnery": the women were her "nuns", she the "abbess", and the price of admission cheerfully excluded anyone below the very rich. King's Place became a small colony of these convents-that-were-nothing-of-the-sort, and the joke — pious language for profane trade — is exactly the joke the Bishop of Winchester had been making, unintentionally, five hundred years before.

The White House sits in this company as the show-off, the one with the special effects. It kept its clientele deliberately mysterious, and the tradition — recorded a little breathlessly in the Victorian *Old and New London* — has it frequented by the Prince of Wales, the old Duke of Queensberry and the Marquis of Hertford, which may be true and is certainly the sort of thing that gets said about a fashionable brothel whether it is true or not. It seems to have operated from around 1778 to 1801 under Hooper, though some accounts stretch its notoriety to 1836.

And then, as these things do, it stopped. The building at 21 Soho Square was altered or rebuilt in 1838 for Edmund Crosse and Thomas Blackwell, and there is a certain magnificent flatness to what came next. The house that had allegedly boasted a mechanical skeleton and a Grotto and rooms lined with mirrors became the headquarters of Crosse & Blackwell, the pickle and preserve firm — a place of chutney, piccalilli and potted meat. It is Grade II listed now, an office block, its Georgian scandals invisible behind a Victorian façade. The Hellfire crowd staged their theatrical vice out at West Wycombe in caves and mock-abbeys; the White House staged it in a townhouse on a Soho square, and did it, if the *Survey of London* is to be believed, with a working skeleton. Of all the ways for a brothel to end, becoming the home of England's favourite pickle is not the one anyone would have scripted.



## 59. Mrs Berkley's Machine

*Site of 28 Charlotte Street, now 84–94 Hallam Street (Weymouth House) · Marylebone, London · c.1828–1836*


> **Attributed site of Theresa Berkley's establishment** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.52080° N, 0.14520° W · confidence 2/5


> `[PLATE 40 — Attributed site of Theresa Berkley's establishment · 51.52080° N, 0.14520° W]`


The most precise fact attached to Theresa Berkley is an address, and even that sends visitors to the wrong street. Her supposed establishment stood at 28 Charlotte Street in Marylebone, not the restaurant-lined Charlotte Street in Fitzrovia. The road was renamed Hallam Street in 1905, and Weymouth House now covers the site. There is no plaque. More awkwardly, there is no secure independent record yet identified in the sources used for this book that places Berkley there at all.

The standard story calls her the queen of London's flagellation trade and gives September 1836 as the month of her death. In the spring of 1828, it says, she invented the Berkley Horse: an adjustable padded frame, something between a ladder and a piece of gymnasium equipment, to which a client could be strapped face-down and birched from above while being pleasured from below. The same account gives her an armoury of implements, a rich and titled clientele, and a fortune made from the flogged backsides of the English upper classes. It is a magnificent story, in the sense that it has grown grander with handling.

Nearly all its load-bearing details come from Henry Spencer Ashbee, the Victorian collector who catalogued his enormous library of erotica under the name Pisanus Fraxi. Ashbee quotes an unnamed source describing birches kept wet, needle-pointed cats, leather straps, holly, furze and green nettles. He also supplies the memoirs, the executor called Dr Vance, the rejected inheritance and the claim that the original Horse was presented to the Society of Arts. No independent court case, will, rate-book entry, parish record or accession record has yet been produced to support that biography. This does not prove Berkley was invented. It means that the woman described with such confidence by later reference works is resting largely on a pornographer's bibliography and the repetitions that followed it.

The wider trade is on firmer ground. Flagellation houses, usually called houses of discipline, existed in Georgian and Victorian London. Continental writers called the appetite *le vice anglais*, the English vice, and historians have linked it to a society in which schoolboys and sailors encountered the birch as a routine instrument of authority. Rictor Norton reads the dominant fantasy as passive: grown men returning, in imagination, to the position of the punished schoolboy. The publishing history is equally real. The English edition of *A Treatise of the Use of Flogging in Venereal Affairs* appeared in 1718, and flagellation erotica remained a profitable trade long afterwards. There was a golden age of the governesses. It is this particular governess who will not stand still for the historian.

The Horse may have existed. Berkley may have existed. What does not yet exist is an independent biography separating her from the men who found her commercially useful as a fantasy. That is the distinction the section needs to preserve. Stand outside Weymouth House and the standard reference works can still give you a death date, a profession and an address accurate to the yard. The documentary woman remains much harder to find.



## 60. Holywell Street

*Holywell Street (site now under Aldwych/Australia House) · London · c.1820s–1903*


> **Holywell Street ('Booksellers' Row'), the Strand** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.51280° N, 0.11720° W · confidence 3/5


Gladstone still stands at the eastern end of the Strand, looking towards a street respectable London removed from the map. Holywell Street ran behind him: narrow, overhung and packed with booksellers whose windows made Victorian newspapers lose their composure. One called it "the most vile street in the civilised world". The exact wording and attribution have shifted in the retelling. The disgust has not. Long before Soho acquired the reputation, this short, crooked lane was London's principal market for dirty books and obscene prints.

Holywell Street was a narrow, crooked lane running parallel to the Strand, its timber-framed houses leaning drunkenly over the roadway and blocking out the light, as though a piece of medieval London had been left behind by mistake. It had been known as "Rag Street", for the second-hand clothes dealers, and as "Booksellers' Row", for the print shops, and it was the second trade that made its name. By 1834, one investigation counted fifty-seven shops in this one short street selling indecent material, their windows crammed with suggestive title pages and obscene engravings designed to stop a passer-by in his tracks.

The street's transformation was mostly economics. Holywell Street first attracted radical pamphleteers, freethinkers selling dangerous material on politics, religion and sex after the French Revolution. When prosecutions made sedition expensive, some discovered that erotica was safer and considerably more profitable. The presses stayed where they were. The product changed.

Its presiding genius was William Dugdale, a former radical whom the critic Steven Marcus later called the Napoleon of the trade. Dugdale reissued old pornography under new titles, advertised common books as rare ones, borrowed other men's chapters and wrote admiring notices for books he had produced himself. He traded through aliases and shifting addresses, keeping the explicit stock upstairs or behind the counter for customers he trusted. Photography and the stereoscope joined the inventory as soon as the technology allowed.

For much of the century obscenity was policed less by the state than by private moral reformers. The Society for the Suppression of Vice bought incriminating material and prosecuted the people who sold it. In 1857 Lord Campbell's Obscene Publications Act gave magistrates statutory powers to seize and destroy obscene stock. Campbell spoke of protecting the morals of youth. Later historians have noticed the class line running underneath the concern. A gentleman could lock an expensive collection in a cabinet. Dugdale sold to anyone who had the coins.

Repeated prosecutions finally caught up with Dugdale. He died in 1868 in the Clerkenwell House of Correction while serving hard labour. Holywell Street followed between 1900 and 1903, demolished for the widening of the Strand and the construction of Aldwych and Kingsway. The shops disappeared. The stock moved west, first towards Charing Cross Road and then into Soho. Local lore says the holy well itself stayed behind, somewhere in a basement near Australia House, which would be a suitably stubborn survival.



## 61. The Discreet Square Mile: Shepherd Market

*Shepherd Market, Mayfair · London, England · c.1686–present*


> **Shepherd Market, Mayfair** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.50640° N, 0.14700° W · confidence 4/5


> `[PLATE 41 — Shepherd Market, Mayfair · 51.50640° N, 0.14700° W]`


Shepherd Market is reached by going somewhere quieter. Leave Piccadilly, slip down White Horse Street, and the traffic falls away into a knot of alleys that has spent three centuries making a business of not being noticed. Its origin was noisier. The May Fair brought cattle dealers, booths, gamblers, fighters and prostitutes to the open ground from the 1680s until it was finally suppressed in 1764. Edward Shepherd began building his market there in 1735, adding paved alleys, a duck pond and a two-storey market topped by a theatre. He removed the fair and kept the useful part: privacy.

The resulting precinct feels less like Mayfair than a village that has wandered into it by mistake. There are pubs, restaurants and small shops, with the streets pinched closely enough to make every doorway feel private. Writers noticed. Anthony Powell lodged his narrator Nick Jenkins opposite a block of flats inhabited, in the novel's phrase, almost exclusively by tarts. Olivia Manning lived at 50a and found the neighbouring women fascinating. Shepherd Market offered the rare London privilege of watching without being watched back.

The mechanics of the trade are worth setting out plainly, because they explain how it survived so long. The classic Mayfair arrangement was the "walk-up": a flat above a shop, reached by its own staircase, with a discreet card by the bell — "Model," most often, that beautifully useless euphemism — and frequently a "maid" on the premises, who was not a maid but a combination of receptionist, bookkeeper and lookout. A man walked up; hence the name. No soliciting in the street was required, no loitering on a corner, nothing for a passing constable to see. And that turned out to matter enormously, because in 1959 the law changed in a way that made the walk-up not merely convenient but standard.

The Street Offences Act 1959 was the government's response to the Wolfenden Committee, which had been asked, in the words of the Home Secretary who appointed it, to do something about a state of the West End streets that "was a scandal and a disgrace." The Act made it an offence for a "common prostitute to loiter or solicit in a street or public place," stiffened the penalties, and — as everyone including Wolfenden himself predicted — drove the trade indoors rather than out of existence. The numbers are startling: in 1958, the last full year before the Act, there were 19,663 prosecutions of prostitutes for street offences; by 1966 the figure was 2,137. The women had not vanished. They had gone upstairs. The proliferation of "call-girls" and the "thinly disguised advertisements of various kinds," as one peer put it in 1968, was the direct and foreseen consequence. Shepherd Market, with its ready-made stock of small flats over small shops, was perfectly equipped for the new dispensation. What the Georgian bagnios of Covent Garden had been to the eighteenth century, the Mayfair walk-up was to the twentieth: the same trade, licensed by nothing but discretion, taxed by nothing but rent, and tolerated precisely because it kept quiet.

Privacy attracted organised crime as well as writers. By the late 1940s the Messina brothers controlled a large network of Mayfair and Soho brothels and, by police estimates, at least two hundred women. The women surrendered most of their earnings; the brothers benefited from corruption and the difficulty of proving who controlled what behind a row of separate front doors. The operation was exposed in September 1950 by Duncan Webb of *The People*, who interviewed women, traced the flats and named the men. Several brothers fled and the organisation unravelled through arrests and deportations. The trade did not disappear. It changed management.

The single most famous scandal to run through Shepherd Market came later and involved no gangsters at all, only a novelist with a genius for self-destruction. In September 1986 a prostitute named Monica Coghlan picked up a client here; a businessman who saw them identified the client as Jeffrey Archer, then deputy chairman of the Conservative Party. When the *Daily Star* alleged that Archer had paid Coghlan for sex, he sued for libel, swore in court that he had never met her, and in July 1987 won £500,000 in damages — the highest sum ever granted in a British libel case, helped along by the judge's now-notorious summing-up, in which he invited the jury to consider whether Archer's wife had "elegance" and "fragrance." It took thirteen years to unravel. In 1999 a former friend admitted providing Archer with a false alibi; Archer was charged, and in July 2001 he was convicted at the Old Bailey of perjury and perverting the course of justice and sentenced to four years. Monica Coghlan did not live to see it. She had been killed in a car crash in April 2001, weeks before the trial began. Archer was believed first and jailed only after people in his own circle broke ranks. Coghlan, disbelieved in court and dead before his conviction, never saw that correction.

The old trade is far less visible now, and restaurants and estate agents dominate the particulars. The alleys have changed less than the businesses. Shepherd Market still feels like a place where London lowers its voice without quite knowing why.



## 62. The Money in His Pocket: Cleveland Street, 1889

*19 Cleveland Street, Fitzrovia · London, England · 1889–1890*


> **The Cleveland Street scandal (male brothel, 1889)** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.52070° N, 0.13760° W · confidence 4/5


Accounts differ over whether Police Constable Luke Hanks found fourteen or eighteen shillings in the pocket of fifteen-year-old telegraph messenger Charles Swinscow on 4 July 1889. Either sum was large enough, and sufficiently irregular, to demand an explanation. Swinscow gave one. He had earned the money at 19 Cleveland Street, a house run by Charles Hammond where he and other Post Office boys were paid to have sex with wealthy men. The scandal began with the cash in a child's pocket. Everything that followed turned on the difference between what happened to the boys who took it and the men who paid it.

The boys were children and adolescents, roughly fifteen to nineteen years old, drawn from the poorest end of the labour market. Telegraph messenger was a respectable job for a working-class boy and it paid very little. A clerk named Henry Newlove, himself still a teenager, recruited others from the Central Telegraph Office to supplement their wages at Cleveland Street. This was the sexual exploitation of poor children by rich men, and the unequal consequences are the reason the place belongs on the map.

The investigation was handed to Inspector Frederick Abberline of Scotland Yard — the same detective who, the previous year, had led the hunt for Jack the Ripper. Abberline obtained warrants under Section 11 of the Criminal Law Amendment Act 1885, the Labouchere Amendment, which criminalised gross indecency between men. Six years later the same section would destroy Oscar Wilde. Cleveland Street had already shown how differently it treated poor witnesses and powerful clients. When officers reached Cleveland Street on 6 July, Hammond had already fled — tipped off by Newlove — and he made his way abroad, eventually to the United States. The British government pointedly declined to seek his extradition. A brothel-keeper willing to name his clients in open court was the last thing anyone in authority wanted.

For the clients were the problem. On the way to the police station, Newlove named names, and they were extraordinary ones: Lord Arthur Somerset, a major in the Royal Horse Guards and an equerry to the Prince of Wales; Henry FitzRoy, Earl of Euston; and an army colonel named Jervois. Somerset — identified by the boys as a "Mr Brown" who had visited the house — was interviewed by police in August 1889 and then, while the authorities conspicuously dragged their feet, allowed to slip abroad. By October he was in France. He never came back, living out his life in exile on the Continent and dying at Hyères in 1926. No client was ever prosecuted.

The boys were another matter. Newlove and a co-defendant, George Veck — a man who posed as a clergyman, dog-collar and all — pleaded guilty at the Old Bailey in September 1889 to procuring; Newlove received four months' hard labour, Veck nine. The telegraph boys themselves were treated with a leniency that the radical press rightly found suspicious, and which was widely believed to have been the price of their silence. But leniency is a relative term. They were named, they were disgraced, and in December 1889 the Post Office formally dismissed Swinscow, Thickbroom, Wright and Perkins from their jobs — a fact recorded in sworn testimony at a later trial. There was also an attempt, organised through Somerset's solicitor Arthur Newton, to bribe several of the boys to emigrate to America or Australia and so remove them as witnesses. That scheme failed: the boys tipped off the police, Swinscow's own parents raised the alarm, and Newton was later convicted of obstruction and sentenced to six weeks in prison. It is a small, telling detail. The establishment's instinct was not to help these children but to ship them out of the country.

The rumour that has clung to Cleveland Street for more than a century concerns Prince Albert Victor, Duke of Clarence, the eldest son of the Prince of Wales and second in line to the throne. It must be stated plainly: there is no evidence whatever that he ever visited 19 Cleveland Street. The allegation appears to have originated in a threat by Somerset's own solicitor that a "very distinguished person" would be implicated if Somerset were prosecuted — a piece of leverage, in other words, not a piece of evidence. The British press never named the prince; only overseas papers hinted. What the rumour did was corrupt the handling of the case from the top down: the Prince of Wales's own household worked to shut the investigation off, and the fear of where the trail might lead unquestionably helped the aristocratic suspects escape. The prince was created Duke of Clarence the following year and died of influenza in 1892. The rumour has coloured his biographers ever since, and it remains exactly that — a rumour, unsupported, and repeated here only to be set aside.

The one man who tried to make the establishment answer for it was a journalist, and the establishment made him pay. Ernest Parke, the young radical editor of the *North London Press*, questioned in print why the boys had been given such light sentences while their aristocratic clients walked free, and in November 1889 named Somerset and the Earl of Euston. Euston — who, unlike Somerset, had not left the country — sued for criminal libel. At the trial in January 1890 he admitted going to the house, but claimed he had been handed a card advertising *poses plastiques* (nude tableaux of women), had realised the true nature of the place and left at once. The jury convicted Parke and he received a year in prison. The journalist who forced the aristocratic names into print went to prison. No client did.

There was a reckoning of sorts in Parliament, though it changed nothing. On 28 February 1890 Henry Labouchère — the same radical Liberal MP who had authored the very "gross indecency" law under which the boys had been snared — moved in the Commons for an inquiry into what he alleged was a government conspiracy to protect the guilty and let Somerset escape. He was rebutted by the Attorney General, Sir Richard Webster, who had himself been prosecuting the case. The debate ran for some seven hours; the motion was heavily defeated, by 206 votes to 66. Labouchère, refusing to withdraw the remark "I do not believe Lord Salisbury" — a plain accusation that the Prime Minister was lying — was named by the Speaker and suspended from the House. The state closed ranks and the matter was allowed to die.

Cleveland Street runs today between Euston Road and Goodge Street, an ordinary Fitzrovia thoroughfare. Number 19 itself was long ago swept away in the expansion of the Middlesex Hospital, which occupied the block until it closed in 2005 and was demolished in the years after; the site is now luxury flats. A few doors along, at what is now number 44, the old Cleveland Street Workhouse still stands — the last Georgian workhouse in London, and probably the building young Charles Dickens had in mind for *Oliver Twist*. The surviving workhouse is a fitting neighbour. Both buildings belonged to a city that knew how to use the poor and how to hide the people who used them. The boys lost their jobs in December 1889. No client was prosecuted.



## 63. The Golden Calf: London's First Nightclub

*Cave of the Golden Calf, 9 Heddon Street · London, England · 1912–1914*


> **The Cave of the Golden Calf, Heddon Street** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.51100° N, 0.14050° W · confidence 4/5


> `[PLATE 42 — The Cave of the Golden Calf, Heddon Street · 51.51100° N, 0.14050° W]`


Eric Gill carved the calf. It stood in the foyer — a gilded, frankly phallic little idol of the biblical golden calf, the thing the Israelites worshipped while Moses was up the mountain, the very symbol of forbidden pleasure and false gods — and it told you at the door exactly what sort of establishment you had entered. Beyond it, down a staircase past a huge Wyndham Lewis painting of a carnival, you descended into a low-ceilinged basement blazing with colour: Spencer Gore and Charles Ginner had covered the walls with hunting scenes, tigers and jungles; Jacob Epstein had sheathed the iron support pillars in painted plaster caryatids, human and animal forms with the heads of hawks, cats and camels; Lewis had painted a drop-curtain depicting raw meat. This was the Cave of the Golden Calf, and it opened in a basement at 9 Heddon Street, just off Regent Street, on 26 June 1912. It was, by most reckonings, the first true nightclub in London — a place designed for artists and bohemians to drink, dance and misbehave after the theatres had closed — and it was the creation of one of the most formidable women in Edwardian London.

Frida Strindberg, born Frida Uhl in Vienna in 1872, was the divorced second wife of the Swedish playwright August Strindberg, though calling her merely that does her a disservice. She was a translator, journalist and impresario who had already helped start cabaret in Germany, and she arrived in London trailing a reputation for headlong passions — she had, among other things, twice threatened a former lover with a revolver. One acquaintance called her, with what sounds like real fear, "an amazingly masterful, intelligent, and in her way fascinating Austrian" with "a mesmeric faculty for getting people to do things for her." What she got people to do was build her a nightclub. Modelled on the Cabaret Fledermaus in her native Vienna, the Cabaret Theatre Club — to give it its official name — was to be a place "given up to gaiety," a European-style *cabaret artistique* of a kind London had never seen.

London had dance halls, theatres and private clubs. It had not had this combination: ragtime and tango in a painted basement, aristocrats beside artists, and a serious programme of new theatre and music running above the drinking and dancing. Members paid to get in, and members also had access to a genuinely serious cultural programme upstairs in the club's guise as the Cabaret Theatre Club — Sunday performances of plays by Aristophanes, Dostoevsky and Strindberg himself, and a music programme that ran to Mozart, Pergolesi and, remarkably, Schoenberg's *Pierrot Lunaire*, staged in London only months after its Berlin premiere. Ezra Pound was a fixture and reportedly got his meals free if he took them at Frida's own table; Katherine Mansfield, Ford Madox Ford, Augustus John and Wyndham Lewis all passed through. Osbert Sitwell remembered it as a "super-heated Vorticist garden of gesticulating figures." The Cave sits in the direct line of London bohemia that would later wash up at the Fitzroy Tavern in Bloomsbury — the same collision of art, money, drink and sexual latitude, a generation earlier and several notches more expensive.

The Cave's life was brief because Frida's arithmetic was hopeless. The decorations cost a fortune, the artists were not always paid, and Strindberg subsidised struggling writers out of a till that was already emptying. The club went bankrupt and closed in early 1914, less than two years after it opened. Strindberg herself left for America in 1914, the club's collapse coinciding with her departure. She died in 1943, her extraordinary two-year experiment in London nightlife long forgotten by almost everyone.

Almost none of the interior survives. Spencer Gore's murals, presumably painted on canvas, have vanished without trace; Ginner's jungle scenes are lost; Epstein's painted pillars went with the building's later gutting; Lewis's raw-meat curtain is gone. What remains is fragmentary and precious. Wyndham Lewis's *design* for the drop-curtain survives in the Victoria and Albert Museum, along with the club's printed programme and menu, now held by the Museum of London; Gore's preparatory oil studies for the murals are in public collections including the Tate. Of the actual interior — the thing itself, the blazing painted cave — there is essentially nothing. One of the most important interiors in the history of British modernism was thrown up in a basement, enjoyed for eighteen months, and thrown away.

Heddon Street, though, is still there, a short pedestrianised courtyard off Regent Street now lined with restaurants. Sixty years later Brian Ward photographed David Bowie on the same short street for the cover of *The Rise and Fall of Ziggy Stardust and the Spiders from Mars*. Heddon Street helped London invent the look of its nightlife twice. Neither invention left much of itself behind.



## 64. If It Moves, It's Rude: Clubs and the Censors

*Windmill Theatre, Raymond Revuebar and Stringfellows · Soho and Covent Garden, London · 1932 to present*


> **The Windmill Theatre / Rhino at the Windmill** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.51280° N, 0.13450° W · confidence 4/5
>
> **Raymond Revuebar and the Walker's Court clubs** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.51330° N, 0.13340° W · confidence 4/5
>
> **Stringfellows & the modern club scene** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.51280° N, 0.12760° W · confidence 5/5


Soho performed one of its familiar disappearances in 2025. In February, Westminster approved a new sexual-entertainment venue for the old Madame Jojo's premises in Walker's Court. In September, the company operating the Windmill went into liquidation and the doors closed after a dispute with its landlord. By April 2026 the Windmill was open again, now as Rhino at the Windmill under Spearmint Rhino. Nothing had vanished except an operator. The address, the licence and the argument carried on.

The old argument turned on a rule of such perfect British absurdity that it hardly needs improving. A nude woman on a public stage was permissible provided she did not move. A living statue was art; a moving one was obscene. The Lord Chamberlain's Office licensed the stage, examined proposed tableaux and required the performers to hold their poses. Decency had become a matter of velocity, and an industry grew up around keeping the body still while everything around it moved.

The Windmill got there first. Laura Henderson bought the former Palais de Luxe cinema and opened it as a theatre in 1931. Her manager, Vivian Van Damm, launched the continuous-variety programme Revudeville in February 1932. Later that year the motionless nude tableaux arrived and changed the business. Britannia, goddesses and other classical excuses stood under subdued light while comedians worked around them. During the war the theatre adopted the boast "We Never Closed", apart from the compulsory closure imposed on London theatres in September 1939. London immediately improved the slogan to "We Never Clothed". The Windmill remained a nursery for comedians until it closed as a theatre in 1964. It later returned to nude revue, table dancing and other forms of nightlife, lost its sexual-entertainment licence in 2018, reopened under a different concept, closed with its operator in 2025 and returned again in 2026. A venue famous for never closing has become very good at reopening.

Paul Raymond found the more profitable loophole. In 1958 he opened the Raymond Revuebar in Walker's Court as a private members' club. The Lord Chamberlain licensed public theatres, not private clubs, so inside Raymond's basement the women could move. Membership reached tens of thousands and the neon announced the "World Centre of Erotic Entertainment". A 1961 conviction for keeping a disorderly house did little to slow him. The money went into Soho property, which proved more durable than any show. The Revuebar closed in 2004. Raymond died four years later, having made a fortune less from defeating censorship than from owning the ground beneath it.

The Theatres Act 1968 abolished the Lord Chamberlain's stage censorship, but regulation did not disappear. It became local. Sexual-entertainment licences, no-touching conditions, planning rules and residents' objections replaced the courtier and his rubber stamp. Peter Stringfellow's club, opened in Covent Garden in 1980 and converted to table dancing in the 1990s, belonged to this new world. Its trade operated openly, subject to council paperwork rather than a fiction about motionless art. Stringfellow called himself a feminist. The claim need not detain us. The important change was administrative: what had once been obscenity became a licensed use of premises.

That is where the story stands now. Walker's Court has a newly granted licence. The Windmill has a new operator and a new sign. The Lord Chamberlain has gone, but committees still decide hours, visibility, touching, security and the precise route by which performers leave at night. Soho has not become respectable or disreputable. It has become regulated in a different vocabulary, which is one of its oldest tricks.



## 65. The Living Site: Hampstead Heath

*West Heath, Hampstead · London, England · nineteenth century–present*


> **Hampstead Heath - West Heath** — London, Greater London, England
> 51.56230° N, 0.18170° W · confidence 3/5


> `[PLATE 43 — Hampstead Heath - West Heath · 51.56230° N, 0.18170° W]`


Hampstead Heath did not out George Michael. A public lavatory in Beverly Hills did that in 1998. The Heath entered his story eight years later, when a tabloid photographed him leaving the West Heath at night and presented the discovery that he cruised as a fresh scandal. Michael refused the role offered to him. In 2006 he called the matter private, defended anonymous no-strings sex and said the police pragmatically accepted what happened there at night. In 2009 he described the Heath as a nicer place for "quick and honest sex" than a bar. In 2011 he put the principle in capitals: "I HAVE NEVER AND WILL NEVER APOLOGISE FOR MY SEX LIFE." The statements belong to different moments, but the position never changed.

The ground is the West Heath, a wooded corner of Hampstead Heath that has been used as a gay cruising place since at least the nineteenth century. Historic England describes it as one of the most notable such sites in Europe. Before legal meeting places were available, parks, alleys and dark ground offered men a chance to find one another beyond the eyes of families, employers and police. The West Heath endured because it was large, wooded and close to the city while feeling outside it.

For much of the twentieth century there was no accommodation, only surveillance and arrest. Police operations continued through the 1970s and 1980s, and exposure could cost a man his work, home and family. Attitudes changed unevenly. By 2006 Michael could describe a practical tolerance in which the police knew what happened at night and did not treat every encounter as a case to be manufactured. Cruising itself is not an offence. Public sexual conduct can still fall within the criminal law, so the Heath's settlement has always been one of discretion rather than permission.

The truce is not permanent, and it is not uncontested, as 2025 made clear. In February that year anonymous posters appeared on the West Heath urging "dog walkers and residents" to "take back" the area, one of them reading, "Cruising for sex? Homosexuality is legal. Use a hook-up app like Grindr or Sniffies and get a room." The signs were torn down, furiously, by locals who took a live-and-let-live view, and the row made national news. In response, on 2 March 2025, a group of campaigners staged a satirical protest march to defend the site's history and its continued use — and they called it the Sodomites' Walk.

The name is a deliberate, learned borrowing, and it reaches back to the very beginning of this book's story of queer London. In the early eighteenth century a path in Moorfields, running along the wall that separated the Upper Field from the Middle Field, was known — the term is contemporary, not invented — as the Sodomites' Walk. It was one of Georgian London's most notorious cruising grounds, documented in the work of the historian Rictor Norton, whose book on the molly houses is a foundation of the subject. It was there, in 1726, that a man named William Brown was arrested by an agent provocateur for "attempted sodomy," and it was there that he gave the answer that ought to be carved somewhere permanent: asked why he had taken such liberties, he said "I think there is no crime in making what use I please of my own body." The wall came down in 1752; the path survives as the south side of Finsbury Square. When campaigners borrowed the name in 2025, they linked the Heath to an older claim made under much greater danger: that a person's body is not the state's property.

This is the right place to finish the numbered map because it is not a ruin, a plaque or a prosecution file. The West Heath is still used, defended and disputed in the present tense. The molly house vanished under a road and the Caravan survives in police photographs. Here the space itself remains. The final entry is not something Britain once did. It is somewhere people still go tonight.



\newpage

# Coda


Desire does not disappear. It changes address.

The map has been saying this all along. Put a wall around the City and the stews, theatres and pleasure gardens gather beyond it. Tighten English marriage law and carriages rattle north to the first Scottish village. Clear the women from the pavement and the trade moves upstairs. Raid a basement club and the party survives in the police file. Demolish Holywell Street and the books travel west. A rule changes the route far more reliably than it ends the journey.

Yet movement is only half the story. The other half is what gets left behind. Authority preserved the rooms it entered with a warrant, the letters it seized and the names it needed for an indictment. Lovers made a different archive: a diary in cipher, a ring engraved on the inside, a letter repaired after it had been torn, a carved spoon, an unopened envelope taken into a grave. The state recorded the offence. People recorded the meaning.

That is why the places in this book do not all belong to the same kind of erotic history. A royal bedchamber mattered because succession passed through it. A Welsh well held curses, marriages and hopes for children. A Yorkshire parsonage contained almost no lived romance and produced some of the fiercest desire in English fiction. A green door in Chelsea mattered because the women behind it could be together without explanation. A patch of woodland matters because it is still doing the work for which earlier generations had so few safe rooms.

The map is therefore not a catalogue of sex acts. It is a map of pressure points, the places where private appetite met law, class, money, belief and memory. Sometimes the collision produced a church, a trial or an abdication. Sometimes it produced a joke. Often it produced a silence, and the silence was not empty. Somebody had burned the letters, changed the name, removed the carving or decided that the servant did not belong in the obituary.

Discretion runs through the book, but it was never simply a national virtue. It could be shelter or prison. It allowed two women to build a household in Llangollen without supplying posterity with a confession. It also allowed families to destroy evidence and call the destruction kindness. It kept a club alive behind an unmarked door and kept a partner out of a death notice. The same instinct protected people from the world and protected the world from having to acknowledge them.

The myths deserve their place too. The Long Man did not lose a penis to the Victorians. The Green Man was named in 1939. The Mars bar was never at Redlands. Theresa Berkley's biography may be resting on a collector's appetite for a good story. Removing these additions does not make the history smaller. It restores the people and places that the embroidery had hidden. A fact need not be ancient to be moving, and a story need not be true to tell us something about the century that wanted it.

Change is real, although it arrives late and cannot compensate the dead. Wilde received a pardon that altered nothing he had endured. Anne Lister's code became a world document long after she had been carried home from Georgia. The Gateways acquired a plaque forty years after the green door closed. Laws were repealed, names restored and archives opened. Progress is genuine, uneven and never retroactive.

The present tense continues the pattern. A nightclub closes and reopens under another operator. A royal censor gives way to a council licensing committee. An app offers privacy in a pocket, while men still choose the wooded ground their predecessors used before the telephone. Technology changes the doorbell. It does not abolish the knock.

Britain never lacked an erotic history. It placed it across the river, over the border, behind the panelling, beneath the pavement and inside the ring. The map remains unfinished because desire has never been good at staying where it is put.

**A note from the publisher**

The Marquis de Mayfair is a present-day business and claims no documentary descent from the people or premises in this book. Its connection is one of place and manner: private objects, carefully chosen, offered without embarrassment or instruction. The commercial interest has been declared in the Foreword. The history is left to stand on its own.
